- **[Beyond SAEs: Consistent Concept Discovery with Sparse Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23801)**  `arXiv:2610.23801`  
  _Jinho Choi, Hyesu Lim, Jaegul Choo, Steffen Schneider_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) explain foundation model representations by decomposing activations into sparse combinations of learned dictionary atoms. However, the learned dictionaries can vary across training runs, limiting the reliability and reproducibility of SAE-based analyses. We propose Consistent Sparse Dictionary Learning (ConSDL), which replaces the standard linear encoder with per-sample sparse coding. This is motivated by our synthetic analysis showing that coefficients from a single linear encoder pass are suboptimal compared with those obtained by per-sample optimization, suggesting that sparse coding is a reliable mechanism for dictionary learning. Across 16 synthetic settings, ConSDL achieves near-perfect dictionary and coefficient recovery. These gains translate to state-of-the-art cross-seed consistency across four vision and language settings, where ConSDL improves dictionary and coefficient consistency by 17.6% and 35.9%, respectively, over the average of six SAE baselines.
  </details>

- **[FuTransformer: FP8 prefill and INT4 decode for Llama-style inference on Hopper](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23796)**  `arXiv:2610.23796`  
  _Wei Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Transformer prefill reuses weights across tokens and is dominated by matrix multiplication and activation data movement; single-token decode has limited reuse and is dominated by weight and KV-cache reads. We present FUTRANSFORMER, a CUDA implementation of a Llama-style Transformer block for NVIDIA Hopper GPUs. It applies CODA's GEMM-plus-epilogue approach to inference, adding direct KV-cache placement, folding the fixed RMSNorm gain into weights, writing FP8 output directly with delayed scaling, and supporting prefill without padding. Decode uses INT4 projection weights and an INT4 KV-cache copy. Weights and keys follow Tensor Core fragment layouts for direct dequantization into MMA operands without full intermediate tiles. A block-diagonal Hadamard transform reduces cache quantization error from outlier channels. On one H100, prefill is up to 1.48x faster than compiled PyTorch FP8 and reaches 1.29 PFLOP/s. For gate/up projection with SwiGLU at the 8B shape, our BF16 path is 1.13x faster than CODA. Our FP8 path is 2.02-2.18x faster than the evaluated BF16 CODA path. Across 8B and 70B shapes at batches 1-32, INT4 decode is 1.3-2.4x faster than the faster of PyTorch's BF16 and INT4 blocks at each point: 2.1-3.3x faster than compiled, CUDA-graphed BF16 and 1.3-6.3x faster than the same block using PyTorch's INT4 projection kernel, which targets a single query row and loses to BF16 at batch 32. It is also 31-51% faster than our FP8 path. Complete Llama-3.1-8B decode takes 2.8 ms per step at batch one, versus 8.1 ms for the compiled transformers implementation. Decoding every WikiText-2 token through the INT4 weight path raises perplexity from 6.24 to 7.23 with round-to-nearest weights or 6.99 with GPTQ in the same format; the rotated INT4 cache adds 0.20. The kernels match a quantized reference computation within 0.001 perplexity.
  </details>

- **[DrugHarness: An Evolvable Agent Harness for Computational Drug Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23791)**  `arXiv:2610.23791`  
  _Yuxing Chen, Zhisheng Zhang, Cheng Tang, Wei Li, Minghui Zhang, Ziyan Huang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Computational drug discovery requires agents to compose scientific operations and adapt to changing evidence and constraints. Here we introduce DrugHarness, an evolvable agent harness coupling composable scientific execution with conditional skill induction. Its environment integrates over 80 scientific operations, biomedical resources and task-specific code, preserving associations among candidates, results and artifacts. From execution trajectories and outcome feedback, DrugHarness induces action hypotheses with observable applicability conditions, recommended actions and supporting evidence. An independent review agent evaluates candidate skills derived from these hypotheses through source verification and, when needed, targeted execution trials. Admitted skills guide subsequent tasks and are revised through feedback. Evaluation on MolBench and SMDD-Bench demonstrates capabilities in molecular screening, optimization and constrained design. Without evolution, DrugHarness exceeds reported MolClaw-CC results by 10.8 percentage points in affinity-comparison accuracy and 55% in mean top-three active hits on MolBench. Retrospective evaluation on skill-development tasks shows that evolution increases Qwen's affinity-comparison accuracy from 62.2% to 81.1% and optimization success from 79.5% to 87.2%. In a prospective HPK1 study, DrugHarness achieves a more favorable median docking score than Biomni among system-specific candidates. A synthesized compound achieves a cellular IC50 of 14.19 nM, a 2.64-fold potency improvement over the best input reference, with activity competitive with two selected compounds designed by experienced medicinal chemists. These results support conditional skill evolution for scientific agents and demonstrate a workflow connecting computational drug design with experimental validation.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Large Language Models: A Mental World Model Framework for Mental Modeling, Simulation, Evolution, and Interpretability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23722)**  `arXiv:2610.23722`  
  _Qingshi Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding and simulating how subjective cognition and mental states emerge, evolve, and shape behavior is essential for advancing artificial intelligence toward deeper social cognition and general intelligence. Existing large language models (LLMs) and Theory of Mind (ToM) methods primarily infer mental states from linguistic information, lacking unified mechanisms for subjective world construction, cognitive-psychological interactions, dynamic evolution, and historical dependencies. This paper introduces the Mental World Model (MWM) and its implementation, the Multi-Branch Mental Evolution Architecture (MMEA), which explicitly constructs subjective worlds, models interactions between cognition and mental states, and simulates continuous mental evolution shaped by historical experiences. On DynToM and RecToM, MMEA achieves 96.48% and 94.00% accuracy, respectively, outperforming leading LLMs and specialized ToM methods. Additional experiments demonstrate improvements of 28.7, 39.3, and 16.6 percentage points in mental state and behavior prediction, long-sequence prediction, and implicit mental state inference, respectively. The results demonstrate that MMEA provides an interpretable framework for continuous mental modeling, enabling AI to move beyond surface-level semantic representations to infer individuals' underlying mental states from linguistic information and dynamically simulate their internal cognitive processes and evolution over time. MWM also provides a promising foundation for causal reasoning, personalized AI, sustained human-AI interaction, and future advances toward artificial general intelligence (AGI).
  </details>

- **[OwlSight: A Panoramic Monitor for Chain-of-Thought Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23670)**  `arXiv:2610.23670`  
  _Lewen Yan, Yanxu Zhu, Jie Zhang, Jing Shao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chain-of-Thought (CoT) monitoring offers actionable evidence for oversight AI systems, with the potential to enable early detection and intervention. However, existing approaches are focused on specific tasks, whereas the regulation of AI, particularly reasoning models, requires monitoring a broader range of tasks. We introduce Factor for CoT monitoring, a strategy that supports customizing monitoring requirements in the prompt, which enables a single monitor to cover different tasks by relying on different factors. Furthermore, we introduce two types of factors, fine-grained and coarse-grained (e.g., monitoring whether a specific email address has been leaked versus monitoring privacy leakage), to accommodate diverse user monitoring needs. Based on this, we develop a CoT monitor named OwlSight that relies solely on factor to monitor behaviors related to multiple dimensions, including capability, safety, and trustworthiness. Experiments across 15,971 reasoning trajectories show that OwlSight surpasses general-purpose LLMs by 1.32-30.57 and 11.71-27.81 points at fine and coarse granularity, respectively, and achieves state-of-the-art results on OpenAI's CoT monitorability benchmark. It also generalizes to unseen models' CoTs and remains robust to Factor rephrasings, CoT compression, and obfuscation, offering a promising paradigm for CoT monitoring that can contribute to the oversight of frontier AI.
  </details>

- **[GEMS: Molecular Structure Elucidation as Learned Search over Isomer Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23617)**  `arXiv:2610.23617`  
  _Utku Umur Acikalin, Yingheng Wang, Goncalo J. Gouveia, Tyler J. Schwertfeger, Frank C. Schroeder, Carla P. Gomes_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Identifying small-molecule structures from molecular formulas and tandem mass spectra remains a bottleneck in untargeted metabolomics and natural-product discovery. Even when database retrieval or de novo prediction misses the exact structure, the resulting candidates can provide starting points for exploring nearby structures through a few bond edits. We introduce GEMS (Guided Editing of Molecular Structures), which refines candidates through learned 2-switch bond edits that preserve the molecular formula and each atom's total valence. GEMS uses MIST, a pretrained spectrum-to-fingerprint model, to predict fingerprint probabilities once per spectrum. A neural policy compares each candidate's fingerprint with these predictions, links present features to the atoms and bonds that generate them, and scores all valid edits in one forward pass. The policy is trained on shortest edit paths between structures, so it can be pretrained on PubChem isomers without spectra and then fine-tuned with spectrum-predicted fingerprints. At inference, GEMS searches from multiple initial candidates matching the supplied molecular formula and ranks candidates by fingerprint agreement and multi-start consensus. With the target structure excluded from PubChem initialization, three edit rounds achieve Hit@1/Hit@10 of 29.64%/50.81% on NPLIB1 and 30.22%/41.33% on MassSpecGym, exceeding published baselines under comparable settings. On NPLIB1, GEMS achieves higher identification accuracy in about 3.3 minutes end to end, compared with 26.2 hours for our FRIGID run. GEMS also improves identification from generated candidates, including those refined by FRIGID. Experiments with exact target fingerprints identify fingerprint prediction error as a bottleneck in target recovery and final ranking. Deeper search recovers additional targets without retraining, with larger gains from weaker initial candidates.
  </details>

- **[From Trajectories to Differentiated Skills: Cognitive Differentiation for Self-Improving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23599)**  `arXiv:2610.23599`  
  _Yewei Zhao, Derong Xu, Wei Chen, Yin Xu, Peilun Zhou, Xiaohan Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents tackle complex tasks through reasoning and interaction with external environments. Agent skills provide persistent, transferable capabilities that guide task planning, inform contextual decisions, support tool use, and enable executable procedures. Yet existing skill-evolution methods largely organize knowledge around skill artifacts, overlooking the functional heterogeneity within individual skills and its implications for knowledge maintenance and use. Inspired by cognitive science, we propose Cognitive Differentiation in Skill Evolution (CDSE), which evolves a unified skill through four functionally distinct yet coordinated components: Meta, Experience, Tool, and Script. CDSE extracts evidence-grounded semantic gradients from successful and failed trajectories, consolidates them by functional role, and applies component-specific updates and validation with cross-component coordination and adaptive invocation. An analysis of 500 top-ranked skill packages shows that 96.8% contain knowledge spanning at least two functional roles. Across six benchmarks, three agent models, and two execution harnesses, CDSE achieves the highest scores in all 36 settings, improving over the Base agent by 7.6 percentage points and the strongest competing method in each setting by 2.6 points on average. Ablation studies further support the importance of functional differentiation and adaptive invocation.
  </details>

- **[RL-Guided Temporal Localization for Dual-Channel Retrieval in Long-Horizon Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23598)**  `arXiv:2610.23598`  
  _Dabin Sheng, Zhe Wu, Jinming Zhao, Junliang Xing, Yuanchun Shi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time-aware retrieval is crucial for long-horizon LLM agents to effectively leverage ever-growing long-term memory. Existing methods focus on lexical or semantic similarity, leading to target memories being crowded out by temporally mismatched yet semantically similar candidates. Even time-aware approaches inject temporal cues only during memory organization or answer generation, overlooking temporal constraints at the retrieval stage, where candidate selection occurs - the root cause of the displacement. Inspired by the temporal contiguity effect in human episodic memory, we propose TIDER (Temporal Interval-driven Dual-channel Evidence Retrieval), a time-aware agentic retrieval framework. Central to TIDER is a reinforcement learning (RL)-trained temporal interval locator that infers the temporal windows in which the evidence lies. RL optimizes window localization as a discrete, non-differentiable retrieval decision, using composite rewards to convert sparse answer supervision into fine-grained retrieval feedback. These temporal windows first define the temporal retrieval channel; the retrieved candidates are then merged with results from the semantic channel and reranked by proximity to the windows. This temporal grounding enables TIDER to retrieve temporally aligned evidence that would otherwise be drowned out by the volume of semantically similar yet temporally mismatched memories. With a lightweight 1.7B locator, TIDER achieves state-of-the-art accuracy on both long-horizon memory benchmarks - 61.52% on LifeBench (+8.82%) and 78.96% on LoCoMo (+3.66%) - which indicates that temporal cues are crucial for pinpointing target evidence amid a vast pool of semantically similar historical events in long-term daily-life memory.
  </details>

- **[Darwin-Claw: Environment-Adaptive Experience Consolidation for Recursively Self-Evolving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23574)**  `arXiv:2610.23574`  
  _Mingze Yin, Xiaohan Wang, Dian Li, Longfei Luo, Jiahong Yan, Yiheng Zhu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While Large Language Model (LLM) agents have demonstrated remarkable capabilities across complex tasks, they predominantly operate in isolated sessions, lacking the ability to continually learn from historical interactions or adapt to user-specific environments. Existing self-evolution paradigms often indiscriminately inject raw interaction trajectories into memory or skill modules, leading to obscured behavioral patterns and compromised update controllability. Furthermore, as foundational LLM reasoning improves, the bottleneck for agentic evolution shifts from accumulating generic knowledge to effectively managing situational experience. To address this, we propose Darwin-Claw, an environment-adaptive self-evolving framework that systematically distills episodic experiences to drive the comprehensive evolution of operational skills and durable memory. Theoretically grounded in an iterative agent-actor and evolver-critic co-adaptation, Darwin-Claw introduces a Hierarchical Experience Consolidation System that extracts fine-grained error attributions and generalizes them into dynamically updated prototype vectors. This structured situational experience serves as interaction patches for context-sensitive perception and explicit optimization signals for skill refinement. Practically, Darwin-Claw is a readily deployable, automated framework compatible with mainstream agent systems without requiring manual curation. Extensive experiments on WildClawBench demonstrate that Darwin-Claw yields substantial performance improvements, establishing a controllable and stable pathway for the continual, user-centric adaptation of autonomous agents in real-world ecosystems.
  </details>

- **[SkillBrowser: Selective Skill Exposure for Scalable Skill-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23554)**  `arXiv:2610.23554`  
  _Jin Ye, Yifan Jiang, Lan-zhe Guo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model agents increasingly rely on skills to extend their capabilities in complex tasks. However, scalable skill use requires Selective Skill Exposure (SSE): providing relevant procedural content when needed while avoiding unnecessary exposure that may disrupt reasoning. Existing paradigms fall short in complementary ways. Progressive disclosure exposes metadata for a growing skill library, introducing context pollution; retrieval limits catalog exposure, but static retrieval from the initial task query may be misaligned with capability needs that emerge during reasoning and may still inject unnecessary skills. We propose SkillBrowser, a Meta-Skill-managed framework that enables an agent to identify current capability needs, formulate targeted queries, and selectively load skills on demand. Across six SRA-Bench subsets and five LLMs, SkillBrowser improves aggregate accuracy over static retrieval and LLM-based selection, including multi-step tool use, and mitigates accuracy degradation on already-solvable instances. Benefits remain model- and task-dependent, with under-invocation of useful skills and additional latency as key limitations. These results demonstrate the viability of Meta-Skill-managed SSE for scalable skill repositories.
  </details>

- **[Trapped in Your Story: Evaluating Narrative-Induced Sycophancy in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23525)**  `arXiv:2610.23525`  
  _Runtai Yang, Dandan Zhu, Kaiwei Zhang, Guangtao Zhai_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are known to exhibit sycophancy by adjusting their responses toward users' expressed views. However, real users often justify their opinions through reasons, personal experiences, causal explanations, and broader narratives, raising the question of whether such narratives can further steer model judgments. We construct a controlled benchmark of 500 debatable questions across ten domains, where the user stance is held fixed while its justification is systematically varied without introducing new task-relevant evidence. Each question is instantiated under 11 matched conditions: one neutral baseline and five mirrored narrative levels for each of two opposing positions, yielding 5,500 evaluation instances. We evaluate seven LLMs and observe substantial differences in narrative-induced agreement. The effect is not monotonic with narrative elaboration: personal experience can reduce agreement, whereas causal framing and worldview-based synthesis increase it. Controlled GPT-5.1 ablations further show that causal framing produces the strongest positive effect, while claim-preserving bullet presentation also increases agreement over paragraph form. These findings suggest that sycophancy is shaped not only by user stance, but also by how supporting explanations are structured and presented.
  </details>

- **[LocalIntent: Outcome-Specific Structured Generation for Backend-Compatible Smart Building Automation with Local LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23511)**  `arXiv:2610.23511`  
  _Tianyang Liu, Jiayue Wang, Zhanhao Cheng, Changyang Wang, Shaoshu Zhu, Zhenghao Wu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Locally deployed large language models (LLMs) can reduce reliance on remote cloud services for natural language smart building automation, but backend-facing processing requires correct outcome selection, alignment with local capabilities, and valid structured outputs. We investigate whether a complete outcome-specific prompting design improves compact-model outputs beyond generic route-then-generate prompting. The proposed LocalIntent approach routes requests to ordinary or persistent-rule branches: the former produces commands, clarifications, or rejections, while the latter generates trigger-action rules, followed by deterministic validation. We evaluate four locally executed Gemma and Qwen models spanning 2B-9B parameters on 101 non-persistent and 54 persistent-rule records, comparing Monolithic prompting, generic two-stage prompting, LocalIntent, and validation-guided repair. Across the evaluated datasets, LocalIntent achieves higher intent understanding, device identification, and action/parameter correctness than generic two-stage prompting, while clarification/mode correctness remains model-dependent. The largest gains occur for rules, where LocalIntent improves intent understanding from 26.4% to 80.6%, device identification from 59.3% to 84.7%, rule-mode correctness from 49.1% to 98.6%, and action/parameter correctness from 5.6% to 44.9%. For non-persistent requests, pooled gains are smaller: intent understanding increases from 39.6% to 53.2%, and action/parameter correctness from 30.7% to 42.8%.
  </details>

- **[S-GAP: Stealthy Graph Attack Primitives for Graph Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23465)**  `arXiv:2610.23465`  
  _Wenda Li, Tongya Zheng, Shunyu Liu, Hanyang Yuan, Kaixuan Chen, Yu Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph Large Language Models (GraphLLMs) enable powerful semantic understanding and reasoning over Text-Attributed Graphs (TAGs), while their semantic reasoning may exhibit vulnerabilities to perturbations in node text and graph structure under adversarial attack evaluation. Existing attacks typically construct textual and structural perturbations in separate stages guided by distinct realization intents. However, this separation results in attack-intent fragmentation, causing semantic incoherence between the two perturbation types, thereby undermining attack stealth and making them easier for GraphLLMs to identify and disregard, ultimately weakening attack effectiveness. To avoid attack-intent fragmentation, we propose the Graph Attack Primitive (GAP), an atomic semantic representation that unifies textual and structural perturbations under a coherent attack intent. By carefully calibrating the semantic composition of a GAP, we can achieve strong attack effectiveness while maintaining sufficient attack stealth. Furthermore, we introduce S-GAP (Stealthy GAP), a multi-agent framework that iteratively refines the semantic content of the GAP. The framework unifies GAP planning and realization and continuously refines this process through surrogate evaluation and textual feedback. Extensive experiments across four datasets, three GraphLLMs, and two attack settings show that S-GAP improves ASR over the strongest baselines by 7.02% on average while producing more relationally plausible perturbations, exposing critical vulnerabilities in current GraphLLMs and motivating more robust defenses.
  </details>

- **[GatewayBench: Unified Capability and Security Evaluation Platform for MCP Gateways](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23449)**  `arXiv:2610.23449`  
  _Yitian Zheng, Zhaorun Chen, Haoyu Li, Bo Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents have increasingly interacted with external environments by invoking tools, with Model Context Protocol (MCP) emerging as a standard interface for connecting agents to heterogeneous tools and services. As organizations deploy agents at scale, MCP gateways have become a critical middleware layer for aggregating, governing, and shaping the tool surface exposed to agents. Despite the growing importance of MCP gateways in agentic systems, no prior work has systematically studied how gateway design affects agent capability, efficiency, and security. To address this gap, we introduce GatewayBench, the first unified evaluation framework and benchmark for MCP gateways. We characterize gateway architectures into two major paradigms: flattened gateways and hierarchical gateways. GatewayBench comprises 524 tasks across six domains, together with a pool of 11,118 real-world tools from 251 authentic MCP servers. To emulate large-scale deployments, we systematically inject task-irrelevant tools sampled from this pool into each agent environment, enabling controlled evaluation as the accessible tool surface scales. Based on GatewayBench, we conduct extensive experiments across seven widely deployed MCP gateways and diverse agent configurations, covering both benign and adversarial tasks. Our results reveal substantial differences across gateway designs in task completion, tool-use efficiency, and resilience to attacks, and further demonstrate that these effects interact strongly with the underlying model and agent harness. These findings highlight the MCP gateway as a consequential yet underexplored component of the agentic AI stack. By enabling controlled evaluation of gateway architectures under realistic tool ecosystems, GatewayBench provides both practical guidance for MCP gateway design and a rigorous testbed for developing more capable, efficient, and secure agentic systems.
  </details>

- **[Generative Policy Improvement from Observational Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23436)**  `arXiv:2610.23436`  
  _Jonas Schweisthal, Dennis Frauen, Stefan Feuerriegel, Emma Brunskill_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study how to improve decision policies from observational data to generate better actions when actions are complex, structured objects, such as text messages or images. This task is subject to two major challenges: (i) action-level overlap is often too weak for reliable off-policy evaluation and improvement, and (ii) generating new structured actions often requires expensive training or fine-tuning (e.g., of an LLM) and can be ill-defined due to information loss in action representations. To address both, we propose PolicyLift, a three-stage framework for generative policy improvement with structured actions from observational data. In Stage 1, PolicyLift learns a small set of discrete action codes that capture high-level strategies in the observed action space and are tailored to support policy improvement under limited overlap. In Stage 2, it learns a context-dependent policy over these action codes. At deployment time, Stage 3 "lifts" the learned code-level policy back to the original action space by generating actions from the historical behavior system in a code-conditioned way. We show theoretically that improving the policy over action codes guarantees improvement of the induced policy in the original action space. We demonstrate PolicyLift across experiments in synthetic and real-world text generation tasks with consistent policy improvements over the behavior policy.
  </details>

- **[OliO: ODE-based Linear Transition Operator for Self-Supervised Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23345)**  `arXiv:2610.23345`  
  _Kwangryeol Park, Seulki Lee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-supervised representation learning has advanced time series forecasting by capturing robust features from unlabeled data. However, existing contrastive and masking-based methods often struggle to explicitly model the underlying temporal flow, either by treating time lag as noise or by disrupting the inherent temporal dynamics essential for forecasting. To address these limitations, we propose OliO (**O**DE-based **Li**near Transition **O**perator), a plug-and-play self-supervised learning method that learns bidirectional temporal flows as a continuous and structurally consistent dynamical system. OliO introduces a transition operator derived from linear Ordinary Differential Equations (ODEs) to align latent representations across arbitrary time shifts. By imposing a strictly upper triangular constraint on the transition matrix, we mathematically ensure numerical stability and enforce a robust polynomial boundary that effectively captures long-term dependencies while preventing the exponential divergence typically found in unconstrained ODE-based models. This structural constraint induces a temporally coherent representation space that preserves the underlying flow essential for accurate forecasting. Extensive experiments across various backbone architectures and benchmarks demonstrate that OliO achieves significant performance gains, providing Mean Squared Error (MSE) reductions of up to 5.75% over the most competitive grid-searched SOTA baselines. The code is available at *this https URL
  </details>

- **[From Memory Hits to Answerable Evidence: Query-Conditioned Memory Compilation for Long-Term Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23313)**  `arXiv:2610.23313`  
  _Shangze Li, Yanyu Yao, Derong Xu, Zhi Zheng, Tong Xu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-term agent memory requires recovering answer-relevant evidence dispersed across turns and sessions. Existing systems increasingly use structured memories and adaptive retrieval, yet often pass retrieved or expanded units directly to the reader, coupling memory addressing with evidence presentation and leaving the final context redundant or incomplete. To address this gap, we propose MemCompile, a query-conditioned evidence compilation framework for long-term conversational memory. Given a query, the Address Plane locates relevant support through compact source-linked descriptors and capacity-aware Cells, while a normalization boundary resolves heterogeneous retrieval hits to stable SourcePacket identities. Its Evidence Plane then allocates a bounded evidence budget across complementary packets, admits limited additional support, and renders the retained claims associated with each selected packet into an answer-ready context. MemCompile achieves 80.52% Judge Accuracy on LoCoMo and extends the empirical quality-cost Pareto frontier to a higher-quality regime. Its strong performance on LongMemEval-S further suggests that the benefits of query-conditioned compilation generalize across long-term conversational-memory benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Optimist Deep Research: An Iterative, State-Centric Agent for Autonomous Literature Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23299)**  `arXiv:2610.23299`  
  _Gihan Panapitiya_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Most retrieval-augmented systems treat literature search as a single retrieval step followed by summarization. Real literature research works differently: the investigator's understanding of the question changes as they read, new subquestions appear, and evidence is weighed, not just collected. We present Optimist Deep Research, an open-source agent that runs literature research as an iterative evidence-acquisition process over an explicit, mutable research state. A research question is broken into a tree of subquestions, each served by search intents that are translated into source-specific queries over arXiv, OpenAlex, Crossref, PubMed, Semantic Scholar and the web. Results are merged into a canonical, provenance-preserving document registry through a conservative four-tier deduplication hierarchy. The registry is expanded by citation snowballing until a saturation criterion is met, and then screened, ranked and allocated to subquestions round-robin. Documents are read in parallel into a structured evidence store of claims with support, location, type and strength. A critic reviews the state along eight dimensions and returns research actions rather than a binary verdict: it can add, split, rephrase, deprioritize or close subquestions, so the research plan is an output of the investigation as well as an input. The final report is written section by section from the evidence store, and every citation resolves to a retrieved document. We describe the design, the deterministic machinery that keeps a language-model pipeline auditable, and a case study that read 28 of 237 retrieved documents in about three and a half minutes. We also report weaknesses the case study exposed.
  </details>

- **[CoTA++: Understanding the Repeat Curse in dMLLMs from an Information Flow Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23290)**  `arXiv:2610.23290`  
  _Qiyan Zhao, Xiaofeng Zhang, Xu-Yao Zhang, Shijie Han, Zhepeng Li, Shunzhi Zhu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion-based multimodal large language models (dMLLMs) decode by iterative denoising with bidirectional attention, which precludes the standard autoregressive key--value cache. Practical deployments therefore rely on approximate caching that reuses intermediate states across denoising steps. Such caching, however, often makes a response repeat one token at adjacent positions in a run that breaks the sentence, a failure we term the \emph{Repeat Curse}. Tracing the context-token information flow reveals three disruptions. Within a step, caching disperses the attention that anchors a position on nearby context tokens. Across steps, similarity-based refresh is not staleness-aware, so some positions are decoded against stale key--value states. Across layers, the deep-layer entropy of the context fails to converge where repetition arises. We accordingly propose CoTA++, a training-free, plug-and-play framework that counteracts each disruption where it originates: Context-Token Attention Re-anchoring re-forms the attention routing against the current context, Decode-Aware Refresh reserves part of the cache's refresh budget for the positions about to be committed, and Context-Token Entropy-Guided Voting discounts the confidence of a position by the deep-layer entropy of its context, which reorders the unmasking. The three components act at non-overlapping stages of a decoding step and compose freely with existing dMLLMs and caches. Across three dMLLMs and two caching backends, CoTA++ reduces the sample repetition rate of long responses from up to 99.6\% to at most 17.0\%, recovers most of the accuracy lost to caching on nine benchmarks, and keeps the speedup of caching at under 15\% overhead, making approximate caching reliable for dMLLMs.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Reward Scoring: Online Multi-Agent Preference Learning for Multimodal Response Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23256)**  `arXiv:2610.23256`  
  _Ziyun Zou, Jiwoo Hong, Yifan Lu, Shao Tang, Weiyi Wu, Aman Gupta, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal preference alignment often follows a score-based RLHF pipeline: a reward model is first trained to learn human preferences, then used to score on-policy responses and optimize the response model's generation. This turns preference supervision into a scalar signal for generation: a model may learn to produce answers with higher preference scores, but is rarely trained explicitly to learn the reasons underlying human preferences. We therefore ask whether making preference understanding an internal capability of the response model can improve its generation. To investigate this question, we propose \textbf{PrefMind}, an online dual-agent reinforcement learning framework with two trainable agents: a response Generator and a preference Critic. Given a pair of candidate responses, the agents analyze and discuss which response better aligns with human preference. Human labels supervise their judgments and corrections. For pairs on which both the Generator and revised Critic correctly identify the human-preferred response, the Generator produces an improved answer, and the Critic compares it against the original preferred response to provide a generation reward. Both agents are jointly updated from interactions sampled with their current policies. Through this process, \textbf{PrefMind} trains the Generator to understand human preferences via explicit judgment and correction, and to translate this understanding into better responses. Experiments across three model scales show improvements in both standalone preference judgment and downstream response generation.
  </details>

- **[Characterising and Analysing Retrieval-Augmented Generation Techniques](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23226)**  `arXiv:2610.23226`  
  _Emmanuel Igbekele, Olawande Daramola_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The advent of Large Language Models (LLMs) across many application domains has increased the need for Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) to reduce hallucinations and improve LLM performance. Thus far, several RAG techniques with impressive features and capabilities have been proposed in the literature, making it a herculean task to select the most appropriate RAG techniques for specific problem scenarios. Thus, we propose a conceptual framework comprising main dimensions and sub-dimensions for characterising (defining "what it is") and analysing (defining "how well it does") existing RAG techniques. The conceptual framework was developed through a qualitative analysis of the attributes of RAG techniques reported in the literature. It can serve as a pre-empirical filtering mechanism for researchers and practitioners in selecting the highest-fit RAG candidates that meet specific user and task requirements before empirical benchmarking of RAG techniques, which can be computationally and financially expensive.
  </details>

- **[Not All Test-Time Compute Is Evidence: Model-Specific Bias in LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23213)**  `arXiv:2610.23213`  
  _Ming Shen, Tianjun Yao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Test-time scaling methods implicitly assume that additional computation from the same model provides independent evidence. We show that this assumption fails within a single reasoning model: errors are correlated across repeated samples, across reasoning depth, and in the model's own self-evaluation, so more compute from the same model is not more evidence. We introduce DIA (Disagreement-triggered Independent Arbitration), which treats disagreement between an intermediate-stage and the final-stage prediction as a per-question signal of reasoning instability and resolves it by asking a small panel of heterogeneous models to solve the question independently, without seeing either candidate, and aggregating their answers directly. Across seven benchmarks and two base models, DIA outperforms full chain-of-thought in 12 of 14 settings, including a 7.00% gain on MuSiQue, while activating on only $\sim 17\%$ of examples and using up to 47.2% fewer tokens than a multi-agent baseline. Ablations show that both independent solving and solver heterogeneity are necessary for these gains.
  </details>

- **[FuzzInsight: Navigating the Efficiency-Effectiveness Trade-off in Coverage-Guided DNN Fuzzing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23195)**  `arXiv:2610.23195`  
  _Vivek Vekariya, Fathiyeh Faghih, Alexander Pretschner_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Collecting and labeling diverse test datasets for evaluating Deep Neural Networks (DNNs) is costly and time-consuming. This has motivated coverage-guided fuzzing techniques that generate defect-revealing corner cases from limited test data. However, their effectiveness depends heavily on configuration choices, particularly coverage criteria and seed-selection strategies. We present a configuration-oriented analysis and decision guidelines for scalable DNN fuzzing targeting two bottlenecks: inefficient seed utilization and the cost of coverage guidance. Our analysis shows that existing seed-selection strategies repeatedly prioritize a small subset of inputs, leaving many collected seeds unexplored. To address this issue, we propose Equalized Sampling, a constraint-based strategy that increases mean seed queue utilization to 77%, improving on prior approaches by 13 percentage points.We further evaluate 84 fuzzing configurations on four benchmark datasets: MNIST, SVHN, GTSRB, and CIFAR-100. Results show that the non-structural criteria LSCD, LSC, and MDSC achieve coverage outcomes comparable to structural and alternative non-structural criteria while requiring substantially less computation. These criteria also promote broad behavioral exploration of the DNN under test, reducing the need to monitor multiple coverage metrics. Among them, LSCD provides a modest but consistent runtime advantage.Finally, we show that retraining with generated corner cases improves generalization robustness by up to 77 percentage points on average without degrading performance on the original test distribution. Together, these findings provide a practical blueprint for selecting fuzzing configurations that balance efficiency, behavioral exploration, and model hardening.
  </details>

- **[OfficeTown: Generative Organizations for Evolving Agent Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23194)**  `arXiv:2610.23194`  
  _Hongchen Wei, Daohong Ying, Zhi Hong, Aidan Wu, Bei Liu, Ruichun Ma, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents increasingly use files and software tools to produce professional deliverables. Such work draws on materials, decisions, and feedback accumulated across assignments. We introduce OfficeTown, a generative simulation framework for professional organizations in which tasks and their context arise from continuing organizational work. From a natural-language description of an organization's purpose and activities, OfficeTown initializes an organization whose members coordinate work, delegate bounded assignments to assistant agents, and review their deliverables. Artifacts, decisions, and unresolved matters persist and shape subsequent work, while responsibilities and permissions determine what each member can see and do. We curate work requests, their context, and review feedback into executable resources for evaluation and learning. We construct OfficeTown Bench with 406 tasks and 4,836 evaluation criteria from 23 simulated organizations across eight professional domains, incorporating domain-expert feedback. Frontier-model evaluations reveal substantial remaining headroom. Supervised fine-tuning and on-policy distillation on OfficeTown data substantially improve Qwen3.5-9B on external professional-work benchmarks, including GDPval. Supplementary evaluations show that the principal--assistant harness achieves higher scores than the single- and multi-agent baselines tested. These results highlight the value of organizational simulation in generating work-grounded data and environments for the continued development of generalist agents.
  </details>

- **[LongForge: Measuring and Improving Long-Context Instruction Data via Evidence-Assertion Reasoning Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23167)**  `arXiv:2610.23167`  
  _Ziyang Chen, Junlong Jia, Xing Wu, Tinghao Yu, Songlin Hu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing quality control for long-context instruction-tuning data is a one-shot judgment inside the generation pipeline that leaves only a scalar, revealing neither which document parts an answer uses nor how many reasoning steps it writes down; the data can thus be neither audited nor controlled. We formalize data quality as a property of the alignment structure among document, question, and answer, and build LongForge, which pairs an analyzer that measures long-context data with a synthesizer that generates it. The analyzer reverse-reconstructs each sample into an evidence-assertion reasoning graph and programmatically computes four dimensions on it: breadth, depth, correctness, and completeness. Every reading traces back to specific evidence spans and reasoning steps, making data auditable. Measuring seven mainstream datasets reveals two previously unquantified defects: breadth barely grows with document length, and depth is generally low, the two being nearly orthogonal. The synthesizer then embeds the analyzer into generation, gating on correctness and completeness and deferring breadth and depth to a training-time mixture over measured values, which makes the training distribution controllable. On two backbones, its two mixtures, LongForge-1 and LongForge-N, outperform the strongest baseline by 2.2-4.7 points on average over six long-context benchmarks, and in a controlled comparison that holds the gate and the candidate pool fixed, raising breadth and depth alone is worth a further 1.16 points, making the structural distribution of training data a lever on downstream quality rather than only a description of it.
  </details>

- **[CIIR - Class-Indexed Interface Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23151)**  `arXiv:2610.23151`  
  _Doo Yon Kim, Christian Wallraven_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal learning commonly assumes paired or synchronized observations, yet independently collected datasets rarely provide such correspondence. We introduce CIIR, a modular encoder--decoder framework that connects heterogeneous datasets through a class-indexed address space while preserving each dataset's native input format and output module. A known local index selects address $k$, and an input-dependent 128-dimensional address representation Z_k(x) is written to that address. To demonstrate CIIR, we learn this address space from handwritten digits, clothing images, spoken-digit spectrograms, and hand signs without synchronized or instance-matched samples. Newly trained label-free receivers recover the requested addresses with 94.7% macro-average Address Realization Accuracy. We then attach the unseen SVHN dataset after freezing the base address space and train only lightweight adapters, achieving 90.2% MNIST>SVHN and 82.3% SVHN>MNIST end-to-end Address Transfer Accuracy without target-side decoder labels. To demonstrate generalizability of CIIR, we reuse the ten addresses as code elements in collision-free arithmetic and hierarchical address codes for all 100 CIFAR-100 classes and evaluate direct arithmetic-code realization through the existing receivers. These results establish CIIR as a compact interface for modular cross-dataset communication and post-hoc dataset attachment without exhaustive paired multimodal supervision.
  </details>

- **[LexPilot: Benchmarking the Proactive Interaction Ability of Legal LLMs with Laypeople in Chinese Civil Case Consultation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23141)**  `arXiv:2610.23141`  
  _Wenbin Zhang, ShangZhan Li, Bingxiang He, Ye He, Yuxin Zhou, Muzhen Cai, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) have greatly assisted legal professionals. However, our empirical studies reveal that, when faced with underspecified consultations from non-experts, current LLMs often produce irresponsible responses with limited utility despite lacking sufficient case details for definitive conclusions. To better assist laypeople, legal LLMs must identify focal points of dispute and proactively elicit crucial context from users. Unfortunately, existing data resources often focus on interaction and response quality, offering limited insight into how proactive interaction affects LLMs' downstream claim-level judgment prediction in actual civil cases. To address this gap, we introduce \textbf{LexPilot}, a benchmark linking proactive legal interaction to claim-level verdict prediction using authentic Chinese civil judgments. Constructed from 432 real-world judgments across 36 common causes of action, LexPilot comprises expert-reviewed pairs of complete and underspecified consultation histories with corresponding court verdicts. Experiments reveal that current LLMs struggle with proactive legal interactions in underspecified consultations, with downstream judgment prediction substantially underperforming that in the fully specified consultation setup (-23.84% on average). Further analysis identifies critical bottlenecks hindering these capabilities. Our work highlights the urgent need for more user-centered legal AI to better serve the public.
  </details>

- **[WebCoder: Scaling General Frontend Development with Artifact-Grounded Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23104)**  `arXiv:2610.23104`  
  _Zijun Dou, Zhen Yang, Xijun Liu, Dongzhuoran Zhou, Aohan Zeng, Jie Tang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world frontend development requires agents to compose reproduction, generation, modification, and repair over an evolving application state. Learning a general policy for this process requires translating heterogeneous execution feedback into task-aligned rewards and effective credit assignment across long sequences of interdependent actions. We introduce WebCoder, a general frontend development agent trained with artifact-grounded agentic reinforcement learning. Our key idea is to assess diverse development activities through their effects on a shared executable artifact and its satisfaction of task requirements. We build Webdev-World, an executable environment that integrates code editing, application execution, browser inspection, and on-demand skills while preserving project state across interactions. An independent verifier evaluates the final application against task requirements to provide terminal rewards, while changes in intermediate artifact progress redistribute the resulting rollout advantage across development segments. Context compaction and message-tree sample construction support training on trajectories beyond a single context window while avoiding repeated loss computation on shared generated history. We further introduce Webdev-Bench to evaluate composed development requirements within a single project. On Webdev-Bench, WebCoder-27B raises requirement satisfaction from 64.8 for its Qwen3.8-27B base model to 72.8, exceeding Claude-Opus-4.8 (65.1) and GPT-5.4 (59.6); on Vision2Web, it improves the average visual and functional score from 41.1 to 62.4, on par with Claude-Opus-4.8. Ablation studies confirm the contributions of artifact-grounded credit assignment and long-horizon training.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking GRPO for Large Reasoning Model Safety Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23085)**  `arXiv:2610.23085`  
  _Jiawei Chen, Yi Zhang, Jiawei Du, Zhonghou Lv, Tairan Chen, Junyuan Shang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large reasoning models (LRMs) exhibit strong reasoning capabilities but remain vulnerable to unsafe outputs. While Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) is widely used for safety alignment, its group-relative optimization may provide insufficient safety-directed learning signals. In this paper, we identify two failure modes: advantage collapse, where homogeneous rollout groups yield vanishing advantages, and unsafe diversification, where unsafe behaviors persist and become increasingly diverse. Our theoretical analysis reveals that behavioral consistency weakens within-group safety contrasts, while entropy regularization can diversify unsafe responses when relative signals vanish. To address these challenges, we propose Beacon, comprising Safety Signal Recovery (SSR) and Safety-Utility Decoupled Alignment (SUDA). SSR combines advantage-guided trajectory filtering and safe reference supervision to recover effective safety learning signals, while SUDA employs category-aware rewards and benign-only KL anchoring to mitigate over-refusal without compromising safety. Experiments across four Qwen3 and DeepSeek-R1-Distill variants and eight benchmarks show that our framework achieves the best average safety and reasoning performance, reducing average safety error by up to 32.83 percentage points while maintaining low over-refusal.
  </details>

- **[Agentic In-Context Learning: What Algorithms Can Transformers Learn in Closed-Loop Interaction?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23051)**  `arXiv:2610.23051`  
  _Shiguang Wu, Yaqing Wang, Quanming Yao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The learning algorithm view of in-context learning (ICL) studies how fixed-parameter Transformers learn from supplied examples. Language model agents extend this setting through interaction: their actions determine what evidence enters context next. We call this regime Agentic In-Context Learning (Agentic ICL). Existing studies largely depend on externally supplied contexts or reward-bearing interaction histories, while work on general tool-using agents lacks a learning algorithm view. We represent an interactive learner by its state, update, and decision rule, covering active learning, bandits, and online reinforcement learning (RL). For expressivity, we construct causal Transformers that approximate these algorithms over a finite horizon, and bound the resulting loss in return. For training, we characterize when optimizing process rewards preserves behavior that maximizes the final outcome. Distillation experiments show that Transformers can learn to execute three representative recursions. We then train models from outcome or process rewards without oracle action or state targets, as in RL post-training. Different rewards yield distinct decision policies. Improving the alignment of process supervision with outcome-optimal action preferences shifts learned decisions accordingly. Such behavioral differences persist in a pretrained language model across textual paraphrases.
  </details>

- **[Distill Until Divergence: Error-Truncated Hindsight Distillation for Agentic RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23048)**  `arXiv:2610.23048`  
  _Yibin Huang, Conghui Zhu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon interaction requires agents to make multiple decisions in a changing environment. Outcome-based GRPO lacks fine-grained guidance for intermediate decisions. Recent methods distill completed trajectories into reusable skills and use hindsight-conditioned OPD to provide dense token-level credit. However, skill-induced probability shifts do not necessarily represent reward-aligned credit; especially after an erroneous action changes subsequent interaction states, skill-conditioned supervision may become mismatched with the current state. To address this mismatch, we propose \methodname{}, which routes two complementary hindsight skills according to trajectory outcomes and error boundaries: Positive skills capture successful workflows to reinforce successful trajectories and pre-error decisions, while Negative skills capture failure-specific corrections to target decisive errors. The post-error suffix is optimized exclusively by GRPO, retaining outcome-based learning without extending potentially mismatched hindsight supervision. Extensive experiments demonstrate competitive task performance and support effective fine-grained credit allocation.
  </details>

- **[Where Research Ideation Begins: Forward-Citation Supervision for Actionable Limitation Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23038)**  `arXiv:2610.23038`  
  _Liyang Zhao, Yong Cao, Haibin Ling_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated research ideation has advanced rapidly, with multi-agent workflows that generate whole research ideas end to end. These frameworks leave largely unexamined the step that seeds a good idea: identifying an actionable limitation from literature. In this paper, we focus on this first step in the research ideation pipeline. Starting from a pilot study, we find that as large language models (LLMs) scale from 0.6B to 32B parameters, they become far better at recognizing which limitation later work addressed, but no better at generating one, suggesting that the bottleneck lies in the supervision signal rather than model capacity. We therefore construct ActLim-Bench from forward citations, yielding 18,279 evidence-linked supervision triples with human verification. On this signal, we propose a three-stage post-training pipeline: supervised fine-tuning, verifier-filtered rejection-sampling fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning with the verifier as an online dense reward. Experimental results show that our method achieves the highest recall score compared to existing methods among all frontier models. We further show that our generated limitations are preferred in 64.9% of non-tied human comparisons and improve downstream ideation systems. We will release our data and code upon publication.
  </details>

- **[ASE: Attribution-Guided Semantic Evolution for Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23027)**  `arXiv:2610.23027`  
  _Yuxuan Zheng, Zhaoyang Li, Purui Liu, Hengbo Xiao, Guannan He_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  External text can supply predictive information absent from numerical histories, but its value depends on how it is represented, combined and deployed. We present Attribution-guided Semantic Evolution (ASE), a modular framework for constructing textual factors, evaluating their conditional contribution, and deploying validation-selected numerical--textual predictors. ASE supports task-specific implementations with lexical measurements, semantic primitives or evolved event/state factors, coupled to suitable numerical experts. Its feedback-based implementation uses conditional refitting diagnostics to guide factor revision and combination search. We evaluate ASE on the four text-associated TESS datasets and dynamic CSI 300 stock ranking. The four forecasting implementations obtain test MSEs of 0.001041, 0.022439, 0.333115 and 1.8150, each below the corresponding published TESS score. On CSI 300, the frozen text-fusion implementation improves mean RankIC by 0.000642 over its numerical expert and by 0.000701 over matched shuffled text, with positive differences in all five seeds. Controlled text perturbations support functional reliance on text correspondence, while incumbent retention prevents regressions in the measured development criterion. Together, the experiments characterize ASE at two levels: forecasting performance of task-specific implementations and the conditional contributions of individual modules.
  </details>

- **[Adaptation of Computer Use Agents to New Software with User Manuals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23012)**  `arXiv:2610.23012`  
  _Chulhoon Yoon, Kyungri Park, Jinyeong Yun, Daeyun Jeong, Sukmin Yun, Woohwan Jung_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in multimodal foundation models have significantly improved the ability to operate real-world software through graphical user interfaces (GUIs) using visual observations and natural language instructions. However, maintaining strong performance on unseen applications remains challenging, as practical tasks often require long-horizon procedural knowledge and application-specific operation patterns. In addition, constructing executable tasks and collecting corresponding trajectories for training still heavily rely on human-annotated data, incurring substantial cost. In this paper, we propose MAGE (Manual-augmented computer use AGent Execution and learning), a framework that leverages user manuals as a scalable source of procedural knowledge to improve both inference and training. During execution, MAGE retrieves task-relevant manual content and uses a planner to generate explicit procedural guidance for CUAs. To further account for the discrepancies between manuals and actual GUI environments, such as version differences or user settings, MAGE also adapts the agent policy through environment interaction. During training, MAGE converts manuals into executable task-plan pairs and uses the plans to guide environment rollouts toward task-relevant states; the resulting trajectories are then used to optimize the policy without human-authored demonstrations. This unified design enables effective utilization of manuals for both task execution and training, reducing reliance on human-authored training data. Experimental results across six applications demonstrate improvements in task success rates over strong baselines, highlighting the effectiveness of manual-guided planning and policy adaptation in new software environments. We provide the code and reproduction scripts atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Efficient CPU-GPU collaborative MoE inference via Adaptive Expert Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23006)**  `arXiv:2610.23006`  
  _Wenhui Lv, Yunming Liao, Hongli Xu, Yang Xu, Junhao Cheng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models reduce inference computation through sparse expert activation, but their large expert memory footprint often exceeds GPU capacity, making expert offloading necessary for memory-constrained inference. However, existing systems typically handle GPU-missed experts by either transferring them to the GPU, incurring substantial PCIe transfer latency, or executing them on the CPU, making the slower CPU computation the bottleneck. To address these limitations, we present \method, a CPU-GPU collaborative MoE inference system that adaptively assigns these missed experts to the CPU or GPU for efficient execution. By exploiting differences in expert token-loads, \method selectively transfers high-load experts to the GPU while executing low-load experts on the CPU. To minimize inference latency, \method adaptively adjusts expert assignments to balance the CPU computation path against the GPU transfer-and-computation path, allowing both execution paths to complete as early as possible. Extensive experiments on three representative MoE LLMs show that \method improves prefill speed by up to 51.9\% and decode throughput by up to 39.5\% over the strongest baseline across the evaluated batch sizes, with consistent gains under different GPU-memory budgets.
  </details>

- **[GOLD: Geometric Optimized Latent Diffusion for Structure-Aware RNA Inverse Folding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22992)**  `arXiv:2610.22992`  
  _Qi Si, Xuyang Liu, Penglei Wang, Shuo Su, Limei Han, Mengzhu Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Designing RNA sequences that fold into specific 3D structures is a fundamental challenge in therapeutics. Current RNA inverse folding methods typically condition on 3D backbone coordinates but the generation is limited to 1D discrete sequences. This ignores the continuous side-chain orientations, which are essential for overall structural stability. To address this issue, we explicitly introduce side-chain geometry into the RNA generative process. Unlike co-design frameworks that diffuse across separate raw spaces, we propose Geometric Optimized Latent Diffusion (\textbf{GOLD}), which project sequence and structure into a unified latent manifold. GOLD employs a novel GeoVAE to elegantly integrate discrete RNA sequences with continuous side-chain angles. By mapping these modalities into a single, smooth latent manifold, GOLD naturally facilitates a synergistic co-diffusion process. To enhance structural validity, we further introduce a training-free, gradient-guided sampling mechanism. By decoding the generated sequence probabilities and side-chain geometries into structures during the sampling process, we compute analytical gradients from physical constraints. This dynamic feedback actively guides the generated sequence to a physically stable state. Extensive experiments validate the superiority of GOLD, achieving state-of-the-art performance across sequence recovery, secondary structure validity, and 3D foldability metrics. Our code and data are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[From Approval to Abuse: Privilege Escalation Attacks on Harness-Protected Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22987)**  `arXiv:2610.22987`  
  _Xianglin Yang, Yechao Zhang, Jiahao Liu, Yulin Chen, Yuxin Cao, Bryan Hooi, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coding agents use execution harnesses to constrain the consequences of prompt injection by enforcing permission boundaries. These systems deterministically intercept any out-of-sandbox action and prompt the user for approval. To reduce approval fatigue, users can approve allow-rules proposed by the agent, after which any future action matching the rule is allowed without a prompt. In this work, we show that this mechanism introduces a new risk: untrusted context can influence the permissions presented for approval, with consequences extending beyond the immediate task. We investigate privilege escalation targeting this permission-update process. Specifically, adversarial context planted in a repository can reliably steer an agent performing a routine task into requesting a plausibly justified, yet dangerously broad allow-rule. If the user approves the request, the saved rule can effectively blind the harness, enabling adversaries to execute malicious actions that would otherwise require approval, without triggering human oversight. Across production platforms, including Claude Code and Codex CLI, we demonstrate that agents successfully elicit the target allow-rules in 58% of experimental instances. Once acquired, these rules exhibit a massive semantic blast radius, covering 56.9% of category-specific capability test cases. To further quantify the downstream risk, we show that conditional on rule approval, our exploit achieves verified, silent goal completion in 47 of the 58 eligible instances (81.0%), undermining the safety promise of human-in-the-loop oversight.
  </details>

- **[DiffusionRVGE: Reinforcement Learning via Regression-Based Value Gradient Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22973)**  `arXiv:2610.22973`  
  _Jiayuan Wang, Tongyao Pang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning improves diffusion models using rewards on generated images, but repeated denoising and gradient estimation make training expensive. We introduce \method, an efficient method that constructs useful update directions by regressing centered rewards on centered final denoised images. It requires neither reward derivatives nor adjoint backpropagation through the sampling trajectory. A unified first-variation formulation identifies the value-gradient target with or without a trajectory-KL penalty. A population analysis under Gaussian noising explains the regression geometry, while a subspace analysis motivates clean-image coordinates. The implementation adapts rollout bandwidth and query weights to reward continuity: local rollouts suit smooth rewards, whereas broader groups provide informative contrasts for discrete rewards. Trained jointly on five rewards, one SD3.5-Medium model matches or exceeds DiffusionNFT's joint model on seven of eight metrics, reaching GenEval $0.94$, OCR $0.92$, and PickScore $23.82$ at about one fifth of its estimated training compute. In single-reward training, \method reaches matched reward thresholds $2.1$--$6.7\times$ faster than reproduced DiffusionNFT on identical hardware. Ablations support the clean-image estimator, direct direction transfer, and reward-dependent rollout choices. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Multiple Turns, Short Horizons: Measuring Effective-Horizon Compression in Jailbreaks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22928)**  `arXiv:2610.22928`  
  _Zhipeng Wei, Michael Lee, N. Benjamin Erichson_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-turn jailbreaks use feedback from a victim large language model to adapt their queries over several turns. However, once an effective trajectory has been found, it is unclear how much of the earlier query history is still needed. In this work, we study this by removing early attacker queries and running the remaining queries again in a new conversation while regenerating the victim's responses. We define the effective horizon as the shortest remaining query sequence that recovers the same attack progress. Empirically, we find that roughly half of the original query sequence is sufficient on average to recover the same attack progress. We call this phenomenon effective-horizon compression. We show that this shorter history can recover the best point reached during an unsuccessful attack and provide a better starting point for continuing the attack. It also helps identify which earlier queries should receive less learning signal during attacker training, and downweighting those queries improves performance. We further find that short reward-propagation windows preserve calibrated defense performance close to full-trajectory returns. These results show that multi-turn attacks can contain more queries than are needed to recover the progress reached later in the interaction.
  </details>

- **[Reward Engine: Reward-Centric Self-Evolution for Open-Ended Tool Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22915)**  `arXiv:2610.22915`  
  _Litong Feng, Ji Qi, Yiran Cheng, Shaowu Pan, Xinyu Zhang, Qijun Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open-ended tool agents can complete the same objective through different tool paths, and their quality depends jointly on the interaction trace, environment state, and final artifacts, so such tasks rarely admit an executable ground-truth reward. The central challenge is therefore to construct a reward from existing experience that exposes the current policy's failures while remaining stationary during optimization. We introduce Reward Engine, which, like a self-motivated student, first works out what success requires and then optimizes toward it. From the task and initial trajectories, it builds a RewardPack of testcases for required correctness and rubrics for graded quality, each executed as a check that returns a judgment with evidence. The frozen RewardPack then guides the agent to revise and select its outputs, and can also serve as a reward for reinforcement learning. On WorkBuddy Bench Office and Claw-Eval, the constructed reward largely agrees with the benchmark graders, not reversing their ordering on 82.3\% and 80.5\% of candidate pairs. Used to revise executions, it raises three-run consistency ($\mathrm{Pass}^{3}$) over Baseline from 45.0\% to 54.0\% and from 78.4\% to 83.7\%.
  </details>

- **[When to Forget: Risk-Controlled Context Adaptation for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22897)**  `arXiv:2610.22897`  
  _Chenle Chen, Yangbo Wei, Zhijie Zheng, Zhenyu Su, Qifan Wang, Junhong Qian, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon agents learn from failure, but the value of a failure record can change as execution progresses. We study when retaining, compressing, or removing the same history block improves subsequent execution relative to full-history KEEP. We introduce RECAP, a framework that combines matched context interventions, execution-relational prediction, and independent policy certification. Matched environment states and rollout seeds measure success and continuation prompt-cost differences; prefix-only relations between a failure block and the current execution propose promising edits. Independent task bundles then select a policy subject to harmful-intervention and non-positive-benefit risk constraints under a fixed measurement protocol. A five-seed longitudinal case holds both the failure block and its summary fixed: compression fails at an early state and becomes a cost-saving intervention at a later state. Across five backbones, RECAP produces positive paired-success saving estimates with backbone-specific reliability-efficiency tradeoffs. On Hunyuan-A13B, it edits 43.24% of retained checkpoints while matching KEEP's observed 91.89% continuation success and reducing unconditional mean continuation prompt cost by 4,619.8 tokens (2.13%). The framework provides an execution-level reference for deciding when to forget: retain history by its value to what comes next.
  </details>

- **[ToPA: Block-wise Toeplitz Adaptation for Expressive and Efficient Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22883)**  `arXiv:2610.22883`  
  _Sicong Li, Qianqian Xu, Zhiyong Yang, Zitai Wang, Longtao Huang, Xiaochun Cao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) has emerged as a prevalent strategy for adapting large pre-trained models. Among PEFT techniques, reparameterization-based methods, particularly those that employ low-rank or sparsity structures, have attracted significant attention. However, these approaches often underperform full fine-tuning. Specifically, low-rank methods constrain the update rank, which is misaligned with the inherently high-rank nature of full fine-tuning. In contrast, sparsity-based techniques limit the flexibility of the parameter space and compromise connectivity across weights. To overcome these limitations, we explore the use of Toeplitz matrices, whose entries are constant along each diagonal, therefore providing a compact parameterization without imposing explicit rank or sparsity constraints. A straightforward approach is to update weights via a product of Toeplitz matrices. However, similar to RNNs, long multiplicative chains often lead to gradient instability. To address this issue, we propose \textbf{ToPA}, a more refined version of this idea that replaces standard Toeplitz matrices with block-wise Toeplitz, greatly increasing each factor's capacity and shortening the chain, thereby stabilizing training and improving expressiveness. We theoretically show that products of block-wise Toeplitz matrices can approximate arbitrary matrices under shorter chain length, justifying the structural design of ToPA. Furthermore, we demonstrate that ToPA offers greater expressivity than existing low-rank and sparse parameterizations. Empirical evaluations across 21 NLP and CV datasets, spanning 5 model architectures, consistently validate the effectiveness of ToPA.
  </details>

- **[Look Back, Correct Forward: Error Feedback Calibration for Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22872)**  `arXiv:2610.22872`  
  _Haonan Yang, Yulin He, Zhuo Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series forecasting models are increasingly deployed in continuously evolving environments. After a forecast is issued, future observations gradually arrive and reveal how the model has failed. Existing methods mainly treat such observations as supervision for parameter updates. However, it remains unclear whether verified forecasting errors can themselves provide causal feedback for correcting future predictions. To address this, we introduce Error Feedback Calibration (EFC), a fully test-time framework for deterministic post-hoc correction of time series forecasting. EFC constructs a Causal Error Diagonal (CED) from revealed errors of overlapping historical forecasts, providing a fresh view of the frozen forecaster's recent failure behavior. Then Cumulative Error Transport (CET) maps this error state to future residuals using cumulative estimation, while temporal out-of-sample validation controls the correction strength. Temporal Residual Learning (TRL) further predicts only the residual structure left after CET from recent CED trajectories, rather than relearning the entire forecasting error. Extensive experiments across forecasting backbones and datasets show that EFC broadly improves forecasting performance while substantially controlling harmful over correction.
  </details>

- **[ShadowFPT: Backdooring Federated Prompt Tuning via Shadow Triggers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22871)**  `arXiv:2610.22871`  
  _Kun Zhai, Teng Li, Yunhao Feng, Xingjun Ma_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Federated Prompt Tuning (FPT) adapts large vision--language models by freezing the pretrained backbone and optimizing only lightweight prompt parameters across clients. Although this design improves communication and parameter efficiency, it creates an overlooked security risk. Since the backbone is shared and frozen, a malicious client can induce backdoor behavior through the representation space while keeping its uploaded prompt updates close to benign ones. We propose \textbf{ShadowFPT}, a targeted backdoor attack that exploits this frozen-backbone attack surface. ShadowFPT first pretrains a learnable \emph{Shadow Trigger} against the frozen CLIP visual encoder, using either auxiliary public data or the malicious client's local data, so that triggered inputs are steered toward the target class in representation space. During federated prompt tuning, the malicious client adapts the trigger under the current global prompt and then optimizes its local prompt on both clean and triggered samples. Only prompt parameters are uploaded to the server, while the trigger and frozen encoders remain local. By shifting most of the attack burden from prompt updates to trigger-induced representation steering, ShadowFPT achieves targeted misclassification while preserving prompt-space stealthiness. Across multiple datasets, aggregation rules, and non-IID federated partitions, ShadowFPT increases the attack success rate from 19.81\% to 90.36\% in our main setting, while maintaining clean accuracy. It remains effective across textual, visual, and joint vision--language prompt tuning. These results identify frozen backbones as stealthy and underexplored backdoor surfaces in federated prompt tuning, suggesting that defenses based only on prompt-update anomaly detection are insufficient.
  </details>

- **[MS-Eval: Benchmarking Sub-Agent Delegation and Orchestration in MCP-Intensive Working Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22869)**  `arXiv:2610.22869`  
  _Bowen Ding, Qixin Wang, Lexing Ruan, Weiyi Lu, Qiwei Ge, Ruishan Fang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents are increasingly integrated into real-world works, yet they must handle diverse domain-specific tools and complex multi-sub-task problems that sequential tool invocation resolves inefficiently and with severe context bloat. The Main-Agent Delegation and Sub-Agent Execution (MAD-SAE) paradigm addresses these issues by delegating sub-tasks to specialized sub-agents for parallel execution in isolated contexts, but its delegation and orchestration quality remains unquantified. We introduce MS-Eval, a unified evaluation kit comprising an MCP-intensive benchmark, a controlled execution harness, tailored delegation and orchestration metrics, and the MSQ index for comprehensive assessment. Evaluating four MAD-SAE variants and eight frontier models reveals that (1) sub-agent specialization (Spec) is the best practical practice; (2) open-source models are largely competitive with few exceptions; and (3) MAD-SAE generally raises cost with contingent payoff, benefiting most open-source models in both performance and efficiency while leaving closed-source models with higher cost and degraded performance. Our case study further traces these gains to better single-agent solutions, effective context offloading, and genuine sub-task parallelism, whereas unnecessary or redundant delegation turns the same cost into pure overhead. Codes:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Evo-B&B: Behavior-Aware LLM-Guided Joint Evolution of Branching and Node Selection Policies for MILP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22861)**  `arXiv:2610.22861`  
  _Jiayu Chen, Chentong Chen, Yuhan Liu, Ye Fan, Jianyong Sun, Jialong Shi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In branch-and-bound for Mixed-Integer Linear Programming (MILP), branching and node selection are two critical and tightly coupled decisions, yet most existing learning-based approaches focus on optimizing them separately.
