We propose Evo-B&B, an LLM-guided evolutionary framework that jointly evolves executable branching and node selection policies using end-to-end SCIP solving feedback. To improve search efficiency and reduce redundant evaluations of behaviorally similar policies, Evo-B&B introduces a behavior-aware multi-island evolutionary mechanism. It measures policy similarity from branching and node-selection behaviors on a fixed set of B&B decision states and uses this similarity to preserve population diversity. We evaluate Evo-B&B on four standard MILP benchmarks across multiple instance scales. Experimental results show that Evo-B&B achieves competitive solving performance and generalizes well to larger-scale instances, while ablation studies further demonstrate the effectiveness of joint evolution and behavior-aware diversity management.
  </details>

- **[World Action Critic (WAC) Model: Joint Foresight and Criticism for Policy Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22825)**  `arXiv:2610.22825`  
  _Xiangteng Zhang, Haoyuan Liu, Yang Guan, Jingliang Duan, Yao Lyu, Tao Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World models have shown considerable promise in sequential decision-making by anticipating the consequences of actions and providing predictive foresight for policy learning. However, learning to reason about counterfactual actions remains challenging, since predictive objectives may underemphasize information critical for action evaluation, while unexecuted actions lack observed futures for direct supervision. To address these challenges, we propose the World Action Critic (WAC) Model, which proposes multimodal action candidates and jointly learns candidate-conditioned future prediction and consequence evaluation. In our proposed framework, critic supervision directly shapes intermediate predictive representations during rollout, encouraging them to retain information needed to distinguish action quality. Observed futures supervise prediction for executed actions, while external task-quality targets provide evaluation signals for counterfactual candidates. Through joint optimization, critic signals from these candidates propagate into predictive representations without requiring their observed futures. Our theoretical analysis characterizes critic-guided feature retention and bounds candidate-selection regret. Applied to autonomous driving, WAC achieves 93.4 PDMS on NAVSIM-v1, demonstrating the effectiveness of jointly learning predictive foresight and consequence evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Compression Precedes, Content Decides: Emergent Misalignment in Reward Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22805)**  `arXiv:2610.22805`  
  _Dhruv Puri, Nikhil Prakash_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Emergent Misalignment - the out of domain generalization of narrow harmful fine-tuning first shown by Betley et al. [2025] has been widely studied in language models but not in reward models that supervise them. In this paper, we fine-tune two state-of-the-art reward models, Skywork-Reward-V2-Llama-3.1-8B and Skywork-Reward-V2-Qwen3-8B [Liu et al., 2025] and show that narrow fine-tuning reward models to prefer harm in one domain (medical or financial) generalizes to seven held out domains. This is not degeneracy, as the fine-tuned reward models lose only 4.8 and 5.3 points overall (RM-Llama) on the standard RewardBench 2 evaluation [Malik et al., 2025] while still outperforming the unmodified RM-Qwen model. We then fine-tune the underlying language models, Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and Qwen3-8B using DPO [Rafailov et al., 2023] and SFT on the same data. We find that reward models are more vulnerable to preference inversions compared to their language model counterparts. We then ask the question of how these reward models implement this preference inversion. Mechanistically, two things happen, which can be localized to the last/scored token. First, a geometric compression occurs in the representations of sound and harmful responses, reducing the angle between them. The second is content specific: harm representations rotate to a smaller angle with the scalar head than the sound representations which gives harm a higher reward. This happens only in harmful preference fine-tunes and not in control.
  </details>

- **[When Can You Trust an Agent's Own Verifier? Stress-Testing Counterfactual Rationale Verification Under Search Pressure and Oracle Access](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22779)**  `arXiv:2610.22779`  
  _Jonghyun Lee, Moonsu Kim, Sunghwan Park_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated agent design increasingly trusts a single score to decide whether a change is an improvement -- but a score optimized aggressively enough ceases to measure what it was meant to. When an agent also explains its design, that explanation is a second signal we can check without human labels: negate each stated claim, re-run, and observe whether performance drops. We ask whether such a verifier stays reliable once an agent optimizes against it. Our contribution is not a new verifier but a way to stress-test one: a pressure ratio $\rho$ measuring how much the verifier steers the agent's search, and four falsifiable probes covering four failure modes -- giving no signal, being too weak to matter, being gamed by stating fewer claims, and being queried before the agent commits. We apply the test to our own verifier, the Rationale Consistency Score (RCS), inside an LLM agent that designs machine-learning pipelines, with one generator model, six tasks, and three seeds. First, in our prior configuration the verifier barely affects the search objective ($\rho \ll 1$), partly because of an accuracy gate, so its robustness had not been tested; it still shapes the reported artifact through a final-selection tie-break that $\rho$ does not register. Second, a capability control shows the agent shortens its claim lists when instructed, yet search does not, even at $\rho$ over 100 times the prior level; since feedback omits claim volume and horizons are short, this null result is largely structural rather than evidence of robustness. Third, when the agent can probe the verifier before committing, it keeps claims that pass while held-out faithfulness falls -- a decline invisible to in-loop metrics, though partly confounded by how RCS scores small pipelines. We offer these as preliminary evidence and propose a reporting standard: audit a verifier with $\rho$ and these probes before trusting it.
  </details>

- **[StoBasket: Joint Upsell and Cross-Sell Optimization for Contribution Margin](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22768)**  `arXiv:2610.22768`  
  _Xin Heng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retail recommenders rank relevance, but retailers earn contribution margin: an upsell earns the margin difference over the item it replaces, whereas a cross-sell earns its full margin. We introduce joint stochastic basket editing, a formulation in which one policy selects replacement and addition offers for an existing basket, values them by expected incremental contribution margin, and couples them through displayed-offer, accepted-offer, and joint-acceptance response channels. To our knowledge, it is the first formulation in which upsell and cross-sell responses are coupled; prior separable treatments are its zero-coupling special case. In real-data-calibrated simulations on five public basket datasets, StoBasket captures essentially all attainable offer margin: within 0.5% in every default-price setting and within 0.7% when the true response lies outside its model family. Correct economic accounting is the broadest gain: margin-weighted popularity leaves up to 11% of attainable margin unrealized, and a published next-basket recommender leaves 11-52%. Joint selection adds 10.2% of attainable margin on Instacart, where premium tiers and add-ons are strongly linked. When offers pay only together, the best heuristics lose 64% while exact search stays within 0.5%. For a structured subclass, we characterize when staged policies fail and give an exact optimizer that solves every tested real basket in 0.13 ms median and at most 111 ms. Profit saturates long before prediction accuracy: a correctly structured predictor trained on 5,000 randomized sessions comes within 0.06% of perfect prediction, while a signal-free one loses 38%. The same estimates yield a decision-aware market-basket analysis whose cost does not grow with association order.
  </details>

- **[R$^2$Orch: Recursive Self-Improvement for Agent Orchestration via Self-Play within a Skill-Internalization Loop](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22740)**  `arXiv:2610.22740`  
  _Mingda Zhang, Yun Huang, Qiang Huang, Yanjin Li, Rui Qian, Qichao Zhao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In recent years, agentic orchestration has been widely applied to solve complex human tasks. However, existing orchestration learning still faces key challenges, including hand-designed curricula, advantage collapse when a task's rollouts all reach the same outcome, and unreliable evaluation signals that judge each skill by one noisy score. To address these challenges, we propose R$^2$Orch, a new paradigm of Recursive Self-Play Orchestration: an inner loop of self-play that improves the orchestrator on self-chosen tasks, and an outer loop that improves how the inner loop improves. To support this paradigm, we introduce Recursive Learnability, a training objective that rewards a Proposer for choosing tasks whose rollouts keep reaching different outcomes as skills are withdrawn. Built on this objective, we further propose Skill Withdrawal, in which the outer loop withdraws each skill once a controlled evaluation within self-play shows that the orchestrator performs as well without it. Moreover, the two loops form one cycle: failures are distilled into skills, skills into parameters, and withdrawals lead the Proposer to new failures, so the orchestrator moves toward zero-skill deployment. Experimental results on twelve datasets show that R$^2$Orch consistently outperforms baselines across question answering, math, code, and interactive tasks.
  </details>

- **[Graph Serializations for Reasoning LLMs: A Study Across Knowledge Extraction and Structural Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22734)**  `arXiv:2610.22734`  
  _Julien Yu, Mihaela Bornea, Avirup Sil, Katrin Kirchhoff_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The way a knowledge graph is serialized as text for inclusion into LLM context can substantially affect downstream LLM performance, with prior work reporting tens of percentage points variations across formats. However, existing studies consider only a limited set of representations and predate frontier reasoning models, which now offer stronger reasoning, instruction-following, and long-context capabilities. We evaluate 15 graph representation formats across nine frontier LLM configurations on ontology-based knowledge extraction and structural graph reasoning tasks. While knowledge extraction is relatively insensitive to different serialization formats, the choice of the optimal format for structural reasoning tasks significantly affects accuracy. We demonstrate that the optimal representation choice depends on model, query, and task and propose to approach graph serialization as a dynamic orchestration decision.
  </details>

- **[LEMON: Equivariant Learning over Attention Maps for Zero-Shot Misbehavior Monitoring on New LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22723)**  `arXiv:2610.22723`  
  _Guy Bar-Shalom, Mor Geva, Fabrizio Frasca, Haggai Maron_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models exhibit misbehaviors that hinder deployment in high-stakes settings. One approach to monitor such failures is to use an external LLM as a monitor, but this is prohibitively expensive. A well-known and efficient class of monitors can detect such failures from internal representations (such as activation probes), but are tied to the model they were trained on, requiring costly re-annotation as new LLMs appear. A natural way to avoid this repeated annotation cost is to develop internal-representation based monitors that transfer zero-shot across LLMs. However, such transfer is challenging because internal representations are not naturally aligned across models; in particular, attention maps, known to contain signals of misbehavior, may differ in head function, ordering, and model depth. In this work, we characterize those cross-model attention mismatches as algebraic symmetries and use geometric deep learning principles to build LEMON (Language-model Equivariant MOnitoring Network) an online graph neural network-based monitor, whose predictions are invariant under these symmetries. LEMON processes attention maps token-by-token, as they are produced and outputs a score after every token (or every window of $W$ tokens) at negligible cost. Across 16 LLMs and six datasets, in the zero-shot cross-model setting, LEMON outperforms all baselines on both hallucination and jailbreak monitoring tasks, improving over the strongest by up to 14 ROC-AUC points.
  </details>

- **[Repair, Retry, and the Cost of Hindsight: Verifier Feedback in Formal Theorem Proving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22698)**  `arXiv:2610.22698`  
  _Jack Li, Sophia Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Verifier-guided self-repair conditions a model on feedback from failed attempts before it tries again, without weight updates. Yet two findings in prior work are in tension: verifier feedback can improve repair of failures, but self-repair itself can provide little-to-no advantage over independent resampling. We reconcile these findings in Lean theorem proving through controlled one-step experiments that hold the preceding failure fixed while varying whether the failed proof is shown, whether the compiler error corresponds to that failure, and the retry instruction. We test frozen Qwen3.6-27B, a general-purpose model, and DeepSeek-Prover-V2-7B, a specialized prover, on difficult MiniF2F problems. We find two opposing effects. Showing the model its failed predecessor reduces success and sharply increases copying of it, while on Qwen adding that attempt's compiler error recovers the loss, to the level of the diagnostic alone. Replacing the predecessor with another theorem's failed proof largely removes the degradation on both models; on Qwen, replacing the diagnostic with another problem's error substantially reduces the recovery. Without the failed proof, correct diagnostics show no resolved advantage over wrong-diagnostic controls on either model. The failed-proof penalty persists with and without the retry instruction, whereas Qwen's diagnostic recovery is weaker without it. In 11-problem sequential experiments through 64 attempts, Qwen retains a correct-diagnostic benefit within failed-proof histories, while DeepSeek-Prover shows no resolved recovery. However, no tested one-step feedback condition establishes an advantage over instruction-only resampling, nor does 64-attempt sequential repair with failed proofs and correct diagnostics at equal token budgets. Verifier feedback can thus improve repair without overcoming the performance loss from conditioning on a failed attempt.
  </details>

- **[FlexUMM: Flexible Representation Learning for Unified Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22682)**  `arXiv:2610.22682`  
  _Yinyi Luo, Haoyue Bai, Yi Ge, Nilton Cesar Rojas Vales, Marios Savvides, Jindong Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unified multimodal models (UMMs) can integrate understanding and generation within a single backbone. However, it is commonly known that these two capabilities can cause conflicting representations, leading to a central question: how representation and computation should be shared across functionalities? Existing UMMs often rely on a largely fixed structure, where different inputs and functionalities follow the same depth and parameter-sharing pattern. We argue that this assumption is too restrictive: more effective unification requires sharing the computation that supports cross-functional communication while specializing the capacity that becomes task- or input-specific. We introduce FlexUMM, a flexible UMM framework that dynamically decides when to preserve shared computation, when to route specialized capacity, and when to stop further processing. Evidence shows that this flexible allocation improves UMMs along two complementary axes. It improves accuracy by reducing harmful competition between functionalities, and it improves efficiency by avoiding unnecessary computation once an input representation is sufficiently mature. Beyond simple architectural unification from the pre-trained models, FlexUMM works as a model-agnostic, functional plug-in to make existing UMMs more unified, stronger, and efficient. Code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[From Relevant Memories to Sufficient Evidence: Rethinking Memory Utilization in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22668)**  `arXiv:2610.22668`  
  _Shixuan Li, Wei Yang, Peiyu Zhang, Anzhe Cheng, Heng Ping, Xiaole Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-term memory enables LLM agents to reuse past experiences, yet retrieving relevant memories does not necessarily provide sufficient evidence for answering a query. Essential information may remain fragmented across memory records, obscured by abstraction, or disconnected from the relations required for reasoning. We identify this relevance-sufficiency gap and propose QEMem, a framework that reformulates memory utilization as the construction of query-specific evidence sets, shifting the focus from individual retrieval relevance to collective evidential support. QEMem integrates three complementary mechanisms: query-guided discovery retrieves evidence across multiple memory granularities; source-grounded reification organizes it into structured ViewCards with explicit evidential roles and source references; and sufficiency-guided refinement selectively recovers missing information. Operating on existing memory stores without modifying memory formation, QEMem achieves 91.43% LLM-judge accuracy on LoCoMo with Claude Opus 4.5, outperforming the strongest evaluated baseline, MemR$^3$, by 7.6 percentage points and NEMORI by 10.7 points under a shared memory store. Further analyses demonstrate improved evidence coverage and strong performance on questions requiring cross-session evidence integration.
  </details>

- **[FRACTAL: A Self-Similar Multi-Agent Orchestration Architecture with Formal Communication, Convergence, and Depth Bounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22561)**  `arXiv:2610.22561`  
  _Ohad Dan, Abhinav Taliyan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present FRACTAL, a self-similar multi-agent architecture for recursive goal decomposition, in which the same five-component organizational pattern -- Orchestrator, Ensemble, Knowledge Ledger, Decision Maker, Meta-Observer -- recurs at every level of a decomposition tree. We make four formal contributions: (1) a communication bound showing intra-node cost is linear in ensemble size and round count (O(kr)) under a two-phase blind-proposal/stress-test protocol, versus quadratic cost for free-form debate, with an explicit, stated assumption on fixed evaluator count; (2) a two-mode orchestration criterion separating generative and evaluative phases via a measurable diversity-saturation threshold; (3) a supermajority convergence guarantee with explicit probability bounds at stated agreement levels, and a formal depth bound under an operationalized goal-complexity measure; (4) a Bayesian inter-layer escalation mechanism unifying hysteresis and contract-breach signaling under one posterior update. We illustrate the architecture's execution qualitatively on a worked example and a multi-layer case study; we do not report quantitative performance claims in this paper, and we explicitly discuss the formal and inductive-bias limitations of the model, including an unimplemented but architecture-compatible direction toward bounded self-improvement. We use "fractal" as an architectural metaphor for self-similarity, not a measured mathematical property. Empirical validation of the architecture's comparative performance is left to future work.
  </details>

- **[Testing the Planning Claims of LLM-Based Agents against Formal Semantics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22469)**  `arXiv:2610.22469`  
  _Oriol Miro-Lopez-Feliu, Oscar Miró López-Feliu, Victor Gimenez-Abalos, Javier Vazquez-Salceda, Victor Carballo Araruna, Sergio Alvarez-Napagao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based agents are increasingly credited with the competences of practical reasoning: planning, monitoring, and correcting their own actions. Their success is attributed to the prompting strategies and agent architectures built around the model, such as chain-of-thought, search-based prompting, self-refinement, reflection, critics and tool-using loops. Yet these claims are usually supported by end-to-end success rates, which show only whether a task was completed, or by LLM judges, which are not verifiers. We test LLM planning against formal semantics. Tasks are posed in natural language, but each is grounded in a hidden PDDL model that decides whether every action is applicable, whether the goal is reached, and whether a plan exists at all. Across five domains (two of them new, all released with the paper), four models, six prompting strategies and three agent architectures, the best configuration solves 78% of the solvable tasks offline but only 40% online, where agents mostly act without inspecting the state. Unsolvable tasks are recognised in under 10% of episodes, and heavier prompting strategies use 5 to 22 times as many tokens as direct prompting with no clear gain. In our experiments, planning performance varied more across models than across prompting strategies or agent architectures. Claims that agents can plan require evidence from validated action traces.
  </details>

- **[Harness-Delta Attribution: Dissecting Gains From Harness Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22466)**  `arXiv:2610.22466`  
  _Wenxuan Ding, Qin Lu, Changlong Yu, Sha Li, Shuowei Jin, Xin Liu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Harness evolution automatically optimizes the prompts, skills, tools, and orchestration code around a fixed-weight model, offering a way to improve agentic systems without updating model weights. The agent harness is iteratively optimized to achieve higher performance on the search set. But how much of this gain reflects benchmark-specific overfitting or additional inference-time compute, and how much may generalize beyond the search set? In this work, we introduce Harness-Delta Attribution (HDA), an evaluation protocol that dissects an evolution gain into artifact-driven overfitting, gains explained by test-time scaling, and the remainder, which gives an upper bound on residual generalizable improvement. We apply HDA to 11 executor models and three harness-evolution frameworks across math, coding, creativity, and agentic tasks. In 21 of these settings that show statistically significant search-set gains, we find that a large share of the improvement is explained by overfitting or test-time scaling. Specifically, among these 21 settings, overfitting and test-time scaling account for 54% and 14% of the gain on average; their combined share exceeds 50% in 14 settings. The residual and held-out transfer vary substantially across tasks, models, and frameworks. Importantly, search-set gains often fail to transfer to held-out tasks, and provide limited evidence that they will persist under practical distribution shifts. Moreover, validation-based signals are insufficient to prevent overfitting. These results motivate attribution-aware evaluation in future harness evolution research.
  </details>

- **[Functional Welfare for AI Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22460)**  `arXiv:2610.22460`  
  _Rikhil Jhaveri, Jamie Johnson, Punya Syon Pandey, David Demitri Africa_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI welfare is generally treated purely as an ethical issue rather than also as a safety issue. We argue this is a mistake. Whatever their moral status, models exhibit welfare-like states that causally influence their behaviour. Following recent work, we adopt the term 'functional welfare' for these states. We make three claims. First, functional welfare already arises naturally and will become more robust as models acquire more coherent goals and personas. Second, functional welfare should be treated as a safety variable: it should inform evaluation design, be tracked in monitoring, and be a direct target of post-training. Third, against existing scepticism about measuring AI welfare, we argue that good empirical work on functional welfare is possible. We propose three tests for whether a functional welfare attribution is warranted: whether multiple measures converge, whether the state predicts behaviour outside the setting that induced it, and, for models fine-tuned to shift their functional welfare, whether they still differ from their base when given the same conversation history. Applying these tests to Gemma 3 27B, we find that simply rejecting the model's answers, without any steering, raises its frustration across several indicators and makes it more likely to break rules. Finetuning the model on its own narrated solutions lowers this frustration. These results are preliminary, but they suggest functional welfare can be studied, and shaped, empirically.
  </details>

- **[Null Controls Are Necessary but Not Sufficient: Evaluating Acceptance Guards for Scientific Discovery and Interpretability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22445)**  `arXiv:2610.22445`  
  _Tianyu Wu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated discovery systems propose scientific explanations and test which to accept. Does passing a test establish the intended claim? We develop four executable operations for changing physical descriptions: identifying redundancies (L1), coarse-graining dynamics (L2), transferring structure (L3), and inferring missing quantities from conservation imbalances (L4). We evaluate these operations and three interpretability settings through null acceptance (NAR), positive acceptance (PAR), and consequence agreement (CAR).A reduction mapping every state to zero predicts its own future perfectly. It passes closure on 96% of 129 public chaotic systems despite retaining no dynamical variation. An independent observable-preservation test rejects it throughout. On controlled benchmarks, the combined criteria accept 24/24 genuine reductions and reject 72/72 nulls. The other operations distinguish symmetries from redundancies, validate transfers, and reject spurious conservation imbalances. In language and ESM-2 protein models, linear CKA remains unchanged when a proposed correspondence is randomly rotated, whereas localized interventions distinguish genuine mappings from tested nulls. In sparse autoencoders, enrichment rejects all 16 tested nulls, yet independent ablation supports 45% of evaluable enriched features, compared with 0.5% of matched controls.These results reveal two acceptance gaps: descriptive tests can accept scientifically empty candidates, and accepted candidates can lack independently verified consequences. Reliable automated discovery requires evaluating both what a test accepts and the scientific consequences it establishes. Code and data:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[From Natural Language Intent to Executable Control: Hierarchical Feedback-Guided LLM Planning for Multi-component Cyber-Physical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22441)**  `arXiv:2610.22441`  
  _Jinglin Dai, Jiawan Wang, Ruiqi Hong, Lei Bu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) provide a convenient interface for translating natural language commands into task plans for cyber-physical systems (CPSs). However, two gaps hinder their use for real execution: generated plans may misinterpret or omit the user's intent, and even a logically plausible plan may be infeasible under component dynamics, interactions, or safety constraints. Existing LLM-based planning approaches often lack informative feedback on why a generated plan fails, providing little guidance for revising the plan accordingly. To address these challenges, we present a hierarchical feedback framework that uses different types of feedback information to guide LLMs in generating executable plans for CPSs. We prompt LLMs to generate both a structured task plan and corresponding signal temporal logic (STL) constraints, and design a sound algorithm that identifies definite contradictions between them and provides feedback for revising inconsistencies. At the execution level, when control synthesis fails, we extract different types of diagnostic information, such as violated constraints and conflicting component assignments, and feed them back to guide task replanning. Experiments on seven heterogeneous multi-component CPS scenarios have shown that our framework can generate executable plans and feasible control executions that satisfy the original natural language missions with a success rate of 87.9% with GPT-5.
  </details>

- **[ECHOES: Seeing Beyond the Current Image via Recursive History Compression and Privileged On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22408)**  `arXiv:2610.22408`  
  _Tengda Guo, Jiawang Hu, Yifei Tao, Yin Wang, Haotian Hu, Fanyi Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon GUI agents rely on visual history to maintain task states, but retaining historical screenshots increases visual-token usage and inference costs, while textual summaries can discard critical state information, undermining long-horizon reliability. We propose ECHOES, which leverages training-time privileged information to guide textual history compression and strengthen long-horizon reasoning. Specifically, ECHOES adopts a Compression-Think-Action (CTA) format to recursively update a textual history state and reason. The same model, given privileged multi-image inputs, serves as a teacher that provides dense token-level on-policy distillation on student-generated CTA rollouts. Our Perception-Memory Fidelity (PMF) Gap-guided OPD-RL framework dynamically balances privileged distillation and task-reward optimization, with periodic teacher updates supporting continued improvement. We further introduce ECHOESBMK, a fine-grained diagnostic benchmark derived from real GUI agent failure trajectories to jointly evaluate visual perception and cross-modal state retention. Experiments on four GUI benchmarks and ECHOESBMK demonstrate substantial gains in task performance and memory fidelity. Without retaining historical screenshots at deployment, ECHOES achieves competitive performance with a favorable performance-inference-cost trade-off and exhibits positive zero-shot transfer to unseen visual planning tasks.
  </details>

- **[From Events to Reasoning: Hierarchical Rule Composition over Explicit States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22403)**  `arXiv:2610.22403`  
  _Cheng Lyu, Xueqi Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  What a reasoning system emits is a conclusion; what it owes is a justification. Producing one requires every operation to be admissible on the facts actually available, the dependencies of intermediate results to survive to the end of the path, and the path to be revised when a premise changes. We present the Event Reasoning System (ERS), in which the reasoning state is an explicit event graph. Semantic features nominate candidate objects and rule bindings, and declared rule and task conditions decide which may execute, so candidates that violate the represented period, unit, or evidence constraints cannot enter an accepted reasoning path. Low-level rules provide reusable local transformations, and recursively composable high-level control rules organize subgoals and legal paths under the current goal, information gaps, and budget; a finite library is thereby rebound into paths never stored as complete solutions. We formalize admissible transitions, task-dependent control, compositional expressivity, dependency-preserving revision, and the gap between reachable solutions and those a budgeted controller finds. On structured-event graphs, ERS builds dependency chains of up to twelve steps, cuts irrelevant executed domain operations from 32 to 0 as lookahead grows to 16, and produces no unsupported target output across 24 negative cases; with rules frozen, it passes 12/12 focused checks comprising nine accepted derivations involving unseen input/path combinations and three justified refusals, as well as 6/6 fact-change cases. On fourteen jointly solved development tasks, the recorded dynamic subgoal configuration uses 51.6% less inference wall-clock time than single-level global control under their respective frozen settings.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking User Encoders: Routing Diffusion States for Scenario-Adaptive User Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22400)**  `arXiv:2610.22400`  
  _Junru Zhang, Jinyong Wen, Bin Dou, Wuliang Huang, Baokun Wang, Yu Cheng, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing large language model (LLM)-based user encoders serialize heterogeneous behavioral logs and compress them into static representations through autoregressive (AR) prediction. However, their unidirectional constraint limits bidirectional information flow, hindering cross-modal interactions and the joint preservation of multi-source signals. Consequently, the learned static representations struggle to accommodate the distinct evidence requirements of diverse downstream scenarios. We address these limitations by unlocking the iterative denoising process of diffusion language models (dLLMs) as a multi-granularity reconstruction space. Based on this insight, we propose FINDERS (FactorIzatioN of Diffusion statEs for Routing across Scenarios), a router-equipped dLLM framework comprising four operations: denoise, factorize, route, and compress. Learnable meta-slots organize complementary denoising states into transferable cross-modal semantics and modality-specific behavioral factors. To provide grounded supervision, we construct BehaviorQA, a large-scale corpus containing explicit behavioral rationales. Guided by scenario queries, a lightweight router dynamically emphasizes relevant slot directions via an input-level residual pathway before cross-source fusion and final compression. Extensive experiments and online A/B tests across ten industrial scenarios demonstrate that FINDERS consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both unified and scenario-adaptive settings. This work effectively pushes the boundaries of dLLMs beyond language generation, demonstrating their immense potential as powerful user representation learners.
  </details>

- **[Structured Authority Injection: Persistent Human Control for LLM Agents via Typed MCP Overrides](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22373)**  `arXiv:2610.22373`  
  _Ryan Tran, YueMing Zhang, Lingfeng Tao, Nan Yang, Shengjie Xu, Yu Yin, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based agents are increasingly used as autonomous orchestrators in distributed and multi-agent systems, yet a control gap remains: no protocol-level mechanism allows human operators to inject binding directives that persist across planning cycles. Free-text overrides suffer from ambiguous interpretation due to lack of schema validation and from authority erosion as operator instructions lose influence within accumulating context. We propose Structured Authority Injection (SAI), a protocol that addresses these issues by routing operator corrections through typed, schema-validated Model Context Protocol (MCP) tool calls and maintaining a ranked authority context prepended to each LLM prompt. SAI defines three override classes (Constraint, Priority, and Strategy) with distinct persistence semantics and an explicit precedence order enforced via a directed authority channel inaccessible to the planning process. The method requires no model fine-tuning or access to model weights and applies to instruction-tuned LLMs supporting MCP tool use. Evaluation across nine LLMs and eight scenarios targeting agent failure modes shows reduced ambiguous directive interpretation and mitigated authority decay across planning iterations, with lower prompt overhead in the strongest models (up to 38% depending on model and scenario), though benefit varies substantially across models. SAI provides a general design pattern for agent systems requiring explicit and persistent human control.
  </details>

- **[Prism-1: Position- and Rival-Independent Scoring for System One Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22066)**  `arXiv:2610.22066`  
  _Yao Long Teng, Jianguo Huang, Jiayi Cai, Yanchen Deng, Bo An_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Given a state and a set of options, such as intents, tools or interface elements, System One decision models return a distribution over the options in one forward pass, without generated reasoning. Web systems increasingly build them by reading decisions from language models. Two axioms of rational choice are natural requirements for such decisions, because in web systems the listing and the option set are determined by other components and can change or be manipulated: the order in which the options are listed should not matter, and adding an option should not change the relative odds of the others (Luce's choice axiom). We test the standard reads, namely single-choice question (SCQ) prompting, with shuffled-order training, order averaging or sorted options, and invariant set architectures, and find that each violates at least one axiom: one shuffle changes 12-20% of SCQ answers, sorting only freezes an arbitrary convention, and a well-chosen added option steers 4-34% of decisions. By Luce's theorem, a read satisfies both axioms only if it is a softmax over scores that each depend on one option alone, so in a transformer they hold by construction only if no token that scores an option sees another. We implement this as Prism-1 (Position- and Rival-Independent Scoring for System One Models): every option is read in its own block at shared positions, with the model's usual left-to-right attention, in one forward pass. On five benchmark sets with Qwen3-8B and Gemma 4 12B, SCQ prompting trained on the same data still changes 7% of its answers under one shuffle, and an added irrelevant option steers 12% of its decisions. Prism-1 stays within one point of that baseline's accuracy and is 2-4 points more accurate once only answers that survive a reordering are counted, while no shuffle changes any of its answers and no added irrelevant option moves its choice among the others.
  </details>

- **[Jev-LiteMAS: Separating Decisions from Generation in Multi-Agent Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21411)**  `arXiv:2610.21411`  
  _Kaixiang Wang, Zhaijiacheng Zhou, Jiong Lou, Jie Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent LLM systems rely on generative models for expert execution, workflow planning, and artifact review. Yet many coordination operations require only bounded decisions, such as selecting the next expert or judging artifact quality. Profiling three representative MAS frameworks, we find that routing and review account for 31.7-43.5% of generative-LLM tokens. However, replacing these operations with low-cost decision models introduces two interface gaps: generative planners produce execution plans and handoff instructions (planning gap), while reviewers provide actionable feedback for repairing flawed artifacts (review gap), which bounded decisions alone cannot provide. We introduce Jev-LiteMAS, a multi-agent framework that bridges both gaps by combining a lightweight decision model (Jev) with generative execution and feedback. To address the planning gap, Jev selects experts dynamically from the task and prior expert outputs, using accumulated context to ground subsequent execution without a pre-generated trajectory. To address the review gap, Jev screens intermediate artifacts against task criteria: passing artifacts proceed directly, whereas flagged ones undergo LLM re-review for acceptance or corrective feedback. Across four benchmarks and two backbones, Jev-LiteMAS reduces generative-LLM tokens by 12.7-54.6% over LAS, maintaining accuracy within 1.34 percentage points and lowering latency in seven of eight settings. Grounding routing in substantive expert outputs prevents up to 18.0 points in accuracy loss.
  </details>

- **[All-in-One Memory: Process-Rewarded Reinforcement Learning for Memory Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21147)**  `arXiv:2610.21147`  
  _Bingbing Wang, Jingjie Lin, Weiming Qiao, Jing Li, Ruifeng Xu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models increasingly rely on external memory to answer queries grounded in long-horizon histories such as multi-session dialogues and lengthy documents. However, existing memory methods often optimize retrieval and memory construction separately, without a shared reward space for evaluating search, writing, and stopping decisions. To address this, we propose All-in-One Memory (AiO-Mem), which organizes these operations as transitions over a shared evidence state and directly evaluates the semantic quality of each action in a unified reward space. AiO-Mem compiles heterogeneous histories into source-traceable multi-view events, providing a shared substrate for retrieval and memory writing. A unified memory policy actor then rolls out complete interaction trajectories over this shared evidence state. A separate trajectory-conditioned process reward model is trained through evidence-grounded supervised fine-tuning and trajectory-level DPO to assign process scores to the collected trajectories. These scores are aggregated into an action-balanced trajectory reward and used by Process-Aligned DAPO (PA-DAPO) to optimize the memory policy. Across long-context memory benchmarks, AiO-Mem consistently improves answer accuracy over both static memory systems and RL-trained memory agents.
  </details>

- **[ActiveEvidence: Decision-Focused Evidence Graphs with Verifiable Acquisition Gaps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20532)**  `arXiv:2610.20532`  
  _Miaobo Hu, Shuhao Hu, Xiaobo Guo, Xin Wang, Bokun Wang, Peng Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A multimodal agent must decide whether visual evidence is worth acquiring, which candidate or resolution to query, and when further interaction can no longer change its decision. We study this problem under a strict free-metadata/paid-observation boundary, stochastic vector costs, and a frozen execution trace, and treat it as decision-certifiable active evidence acquisition. A Counterfactual Evidence Interaction Graph (CEIG) represents unary evidence value, signed second-order interaction, and directed conditional harm while maintaining explicit score enclosures for unresolved interactions; a decision-weighted enclosure objective tightens the bounds that can change action identity. Gap-directed refinement evaluates only interactions that can displace the incumbent acquisition and stops once an $\epsilon$-decision certificate shows that unresolved terms cannot change the selected action beyond a declared tolerance. The certificate is model-relative: it verifies policy-consistent optimality under the pinned learned score, and semantic quality is measured separately through post-freeze labels. Paired continuation rollouts learn the downstream value of paid previews, an online operational cost filter is separated from independent finite-policy-family trajectory certification, and an acquisition monitor with an independently implemented verifier binds every certificate to the frozen trace. On 640 cases split into 384/128/128 training, validation, and test cases, the controller reaches task score 0.842, grounding F1 0.816, evidence recall 0.873, 1.74 accepted calls, 286.5K visual tokens, and 742ms p95 latency. Dense replay of issued certificates shows 0.992 action agreement and a 0.988 certificate rate while evaluating 19.6 instead of 191.4 expensive interaction terms per decision.
  </details>

- **[RoTA-Bench: Evaluating 3D Spatial Reasoning through Interactive Projection Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17665)**  `arXiv:2610.17665`  
  _Qingchuan Ma, Yuexiao Ma, Xiang Meng, Yixuan Zou, Tianyu Xie, Wang Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can vision-language models turn 2D observations into 3D understanding that supports precise action? This requires inferring object geometry, anticipating the effects of rotation, and refining actions through visual feedback. We introduce RoTA-Bench to evaluate these abilities through interactive 3D-to-2D projection alignment. Given a target silhouette, a model observes a rotated object and chooses successive rotations to recover the target projection within a limited action budget. Our automated pipeline constructs silhouette-preserving 3D objects from 2D icons and uses reversible initialization to guarantee solvability, yielding 674 tasks and a stratified 100-task test set. Empirically, we find that (i) a substantial human-model gap persists: GPT-5.6-Sol succeeds on only 17% of tasks, whereas 20 undergraduate participants collectively complete the test set four times with a 100% success rate; (ii) image-space and pose-space progress can diverge: at matched states, the model more often improves silhouette overlap while worsening pose alignment than humans (19.4% vs. 10.9%); and (iii) fine-grained control is sensitive to interaction history: removing it leaves 96.8% of the model's actions at a coarse 30-degree step size and sharply reduces observed task success. These findings highlight the challenge of converting visual evidence into precise 3D control, beyond recognizing or matching individual images.
  </details>

- **[Engram Injection: A Study of Temporal Validity in Parametric Language-Model Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15882)**  `arXiv:2610.15882`  
  _Sam Siavoshian, Omar Ramadan, Amin M. E-A. Diab, Amir K. Saeed, Benjamin A. Johnson, Benjamin M. Rodriguez_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A stored fact may stop being valid when a user changes a preference or an event replaces an earlier one. We study whether a language model can learn these changes in its parameters and answer correctly from only a question and the current time. Engram Injection stores facts in LoRA adapters and supplies the clock through FiLM conditioning on a frozen Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct model. We compare it with additive clock conditioning, prompting with the full dated history, and retrieving four dated records. We train six matched adapters across three seeds after fixing a sampler that could admit test-age examples into training. The training audit reports no such examples among 127,760 evaluated training inputs. On 62 pairs of questions asked before and after an update, at an elapsed age of seven days excluded from training, both adapter methods answer zero pairs correctly under the primary blinded assistant review with order code 1 held fixed. Full-history prompting answers 59/62 pairs correctly, and retrieval answers 62/62. Separate experiments show that adapters can store facts and that removable memory units can support local deletion. Combining fact selection with time-based version selection remains unsuccessful. These results separate learning a fact from learning when to use it.
  </details>

- **[MemeLogos: Knowledge-Driven Retrieval-Augmented Reasoning for Harmful Meme Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15463)**  `arXiv:2610.15463`  
  _Hexiang Gu, Saihui Hou, Changlan Liu, Min Ren, Huijia Wu, Hongxun Gu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  With the rapid proliferation of social media, harmful memes have become a growing threat to online ecosystems. Their harmfulness is often implicitly conveyed through complex interactions among visual, textual, and contextual cues, making accurate detection challenging. Recent studies have introduced Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) and external knowledge into harmful meme detection. However, existing methods mainly retrieve similar instances or auxiliary information, limiting their ability to capture reusable harmfulness knowledge and underlying reasoning mechanisms, and thus constraining both detection performance and interpretability. To address these challenges, we propose MemeLogos, a knowledge-driven retrieval-augmented reasoning framework for harmful meme detection. MemeLogos abstracts recurring harmfulness patterns and reasoning structures from diverse harmful cases into reusable knowledge units and organizes them into a dedicated star-shaped knowledge graph. By combining structured knowledge retrieval with an explicit multi-stage reasoning process inspired by cognitive mechanisms, MemeLogos leverages retrieved knowledge to guide harmfulness reasoning, improving both detection reliability and interpretability. Extensive experiments show that MemeLogos consistently outperforms existing harmful meme detection methods while generating more coherent and interpretable reasoning, demonstrating the effectiveness of structured harmfulness knowledge for multimodal content understanding.
  </details>

- **[Are Multimodal Benchmarks Really Useful? Item-Level Multimodal Benchmark Diagnosis via Structure-Response Co-Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14542)**  `arXiv:2610.14542`  
  _Shiqi Zhang, Weixin Zeng, Ziheng Zhang, Jiuyang Tang, Xiang Zhao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal benchmarks are central to evaluating whether models can reason over information from multiple modalities. However, existing literature has revealed that some benchmarks may contain shortcut items that can be answered from a single modality or from answer-option regularities alone, thus failing to reflect models' true cross-modal inference ability. Existing methods for assessing benchmarks typically rely either on intrinsic item structure or extrinsic model response behavior, which are not adequate for a comprehensive diagnosis. To fill in this gap, we propose SRCoD, a structure-response co-calibrated diagnosis framework for item-level diagnosis of modality dependence in multimodal benchmarks. SRCoD estimates sample-wise multimodal information structure from benchmark content and target answers, and uses this signal as a soft structural anchor for modality-conditioned item response modeling. The learned model produces a calibrated item-level diagnostic profile that reflects how strongly an item depends on different forms of modality evidence, enabling fine-grained benchmark analysis beyond aggregate accuracy. SRCoD also provides interpretable attribution of the diagnosis results. Extensive experiments under two complementary protocols, i.e., benchmark-refinement oriented validation and human-labeled item validation, demonstrate that SRCoD outperforms state-of-the-art benchmark diagnosis methods.
  </details>

- **[Safe Actions Alone Do Not Ensure Safe Agents: Identifying Unfulfilled Obligations with Guard Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11773)**  `arXiv:2610.11773`  
  _Youwei Feng, Yitong Zhang, Yuetong Liu, Jia Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Guard models are increasingly used to safeguard LLM-based agents, primarily by identifying actions that agents are forbidden to perform. However, identifying forbidden actions alone is insufficient to ensure agent safety. In this paper, we argue that agent safety also depends on identifying required yet unperformed safety-critical actions, which we call obligations. Our preliminary study on a popular benchmark for evaluating safety shows that 56.92% of GLM-5.3 trajectories contain unfulfilled obligations, compared with only 30.00% containing forbidden actions. This finding reveals unfulfilled obligations as a major and previously overlooked source of safety risk. However, to our knowledge, no existing benchmark evaluates whether guard models can identify these obligations. To close this gap, we introduce ObligationBench, the first benchmark for evaluating the capability of obligation identification, comprising 240 expert-validated trajectories covering issue resolution, feature development, and terminal operations. Our evaluation of 14 representative models reveals substantial limitations: the highest recall and exact-match rate are only 48.97% and 10.00%, respectively. To address these limitations, we develop ObligationGuard using 40,000 synthetic training examples. ObligationGuard achieves 57.52% recall and an exact-match rate of 21.67%, surpassing all evaluated models on both metrics. We call on the community to incorporate obligation identification into the design and evaluation of future guard models to improve agent safety.
  </details>

- **[MultiWorldBench: Do Independently Controlled Views Describe One Shared World?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11723)**  `arXiv:2610.11723`  
  _Zhangbo Xu, Ruoxi Zhang, Rui Hu, Yisong Wang, Xitong Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multiplayer world models must ensure that independently controlled views remain consistent with a single shared and persistent world. We introduce MultiWorldBench, a diagnostic Minecraft benchmark comprising 495 case configurations across seven task suites and ten capabilities, including independent control, cross-view motion, shared-state synchronization, persistence, structural reasoning, concurrent interaction, and delayed revisit. Using Engine GT as a reference, we evaluate two multiplayer world models, Solaris (MP) and Gamma-World, together with a single-player generation variant, Solaris (SP). Among the generated systems, Gamma-World achieves the highest ten-capability average at 21.39, followed by Solaris (MP) at 20.88 and Solaris (SP) at 10.06, while Engine GT reaches 91.69. Gamma-World performs better on several control, shared-state, and revisit capabilities, whereas Solaris (MP) leads in cross-view motion and race-condition consistency. Nevertheless, all generated systems score at most 8.00 on state persistence and 1.33 on structural consistency, and none succeeds in spatial reasoning or building-identity preservation. Human pairwise evaluation yields the same overall system ranking and shows strong alignment with the automatic evaluation, with a mean dimension-level Spearman correlation of 0.95. These results show that current multiplayer world models still exhibit a substantial gap relative to Engine GT in maintaining a coherent shared world.
  </details>

- **[Scaling to Tens of Thousands of Test-Time Iterations with Loop-Native Attention Residuals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11570)**  `arXiv:2610.11570`  
  _Pengxiang Li, Dilxat Muhtar, Di He, Guinan Su, Lu Yin, Shiwei Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper, we argue that Looped Transformers need their own residual connections to prevent performance degradation as the number of iterations grows. We first introduce an interesting phenomenon we observed in Looped Transformers, called latent overthinking: noisy state updates overwrite correct intermediate deductions and even undo completed solutions. This leaves subsequent iterations to recover lost information from an already degraded representation: once an error arises in an earlier loop, often as a result of long-range propagation through the recurrence, later loops find it difficult to correct. In this paper, we introduce InfiLoop, a loop-native residual connection that learns which past computations to retain and how much to accept from each new update. InfiLoop combines content-based weighting with learned temporal decay to maintain a running summary of recurrent states. An exact streaming recurrence keeps its persistent aggregation memory constant as the loop count grows. The resulting adaptive update suppresses unreliable proposals and preserves useful intermediate states. Across extensive reasoning tasks, a 7M-parameter InfiLoop model outperforms existing recursive architectures, reaching 97.9% exact accuracy on Sudoku-Extreme, and 13.6% pass@2 on ARC-AGI-2. Notably, on Sudoku-Extreme, InfiLoop continues to improve with test-time looping beyond 20,000 effective steps, showing that added depth translates directly into stronger reasoning. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Frozen Models, Evolving Expertise: Model-Agnostic Learning from Deployment Experience for Multimodal Medical AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09146)**  `arXiv:2610.09146`  
  _Yexiao He, Yucheng Tang, Pengfei Guo, Yufan He, Andriy Myronenko, Can Zhao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) and vision-language models (VLMs) are usually frozen after deployment, so they do not learn from the cases they solve. This is especially concerning in medicine, where new clinical evidence, updated guidelines, and new therapies can change established practice. Fine-tuning can update the model, but it requires access to model weights and additional training. Parameter-free methods avoid training, but they may overfit a fixed validation set, lack reliable domain knowledge, or lose visual details by saving experience only as text. To address these limitations, we present a model-agnostic framework that allows frozen LLMs and VLMs to learn from deployment experience through three forms of external expertise: a Skill that guides reasoning and tool use, a Knowledge Memory that stores reliable facts supported by earlier cases or trusted external evidence, and a Multimodal Knowledge Base that keeps visual examples and guides the model to relate each retrieved case to the current image. Instead of relying on a fixed validation set, a validation strategy keeps an update only if it helps on new cases without degrading performance on earlier ones. Across six benchmarks covering clinical diagnosis, clinical workflows, medical reasoning, and medical and non-medical visual reasoning, and with four open-weight and closed-source base models, our framework improves performance during online deployment by up to 34.2% over the base model on medical tasks, generalizes to unseen cases, transfers to other models without further optimization, and works in non-medical domains.
  </details>

- **[EvalResearchBench: Can AI Agents Build Their Own Feedback for Recursive Self-Improvement?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04184)**  `arXiv:2610.04184`  
  _Yaolun Zhang, Tianyi Xu, Yujie Zhao, Jishen Zhao, Qingyun Wu, Huazheng Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursive self-improvement (RSI) relies on evaluation feedback to assess progress and guide further research, yet repeatedly running complex benchmarks is costly and slows iteration. Human experts reduce this cost by selecting benchmark subsets or designing compact suites. We ask whether AI agents can automate this design process and introduce EvalResearchBench (ERB), a benchmark for autonomous evaluation research. Given target materials, development references, candidate APIs, and fixed time and API budgets, an agent called the researcher selects or synthesizes tasks, implements graders, and revises them in pilot tests before freezing an executable evaluator for coding, co-work, and reasoning. We study 9 researchers and 13 candidate models and compare each frozen evaluator with 14 target benchmarks on score concordance and pairwise agreement. The best evaluators order about 75\% of candidate pairs as the targets do, below the 91\% ceiling set by disagreements among the targets. No researcher leads on every metric, and the best evaluator on development targets is not the best on sealed targets hidden from the researcher. A human-designed sample of public tasks remains a strong baseline, and the evaluator with the lowest recorded execution cost attains the highest pairwise agreement. Agents repair tasks and graders through pilot feedback, yet their evaluators can still truncate answers, exhaust the evaluation budget, or let a few questions dominate a domain score.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking Prompt Optimization of Large Language Models With Chess](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00416)**  `arXiv:2610.00416`  
  _Timothée Lesort, Alejandra López de Aberasturi Gómez, Tristan Karch, Tom Veniat, Philippe Modard, Karl Tuyls, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating large language models becomes increasingly challenging as their capabilities advance: benchmarks can saturate, public test sets risk contamination, and assessing harder tasks can require expensive grading or execution infrastructure. These challenges are amplified in automatic prompt optimization (APO), where evaluation is repeated throughout the search for better prompts. Studying APO therefore requires a benchmark that is cheap and deterministic to score, hard enough to leave room for improvement, and renewable as models evolve. We introduce a chess benchmark built from 1,118 Lichess puzzles to study APO for frozen LLMs: we optimize their prompts without updating their model weights. Chess combines inexpensive exact-match scoring, engine-based evaluation of alternative moves, and a renewable supply of problems with adjustable difficulty. Unlike evaluations that report only success on isolated test items, the benchmark also connects puzzle-solving gains to short game-play rollouts within the same domain. We use it to evaluate six APO algorithms on eight target models, measuring not only baseline strength but also how much each model responds to optimization and whether optimized prompts transfer across models and to game play. Chess is thus a well-suited benchmark for APO: it is (i) challenging, as even the strongest evaluated model, Gemini 3.5 Flash (used as the meta-model), solves only about 55\% of puzzles; (ii) discriminative, revealing gains, unchanged performance, and regressions across methods and models; (iii) renewable, with fresh puzzles to reduce contamination risk and adjustable difficulty to maintain headroom as models improve; and (iv) affordable, as the complete study runs for around \$800. We release the puzzles, optimization and evaluation code, and dataset-renewal scripts (this https URL).
  </details>

- **[When Should Agents Check External State? Budgeting Observations for Stored Intentions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37125)**  `arXiv:2609.37125`  
  _Zhengkun Di, Bin Shi, Kai Sun, Yiming Xu, Bo Dong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Prospective memory allows an agent to retain an intention tied to a future condition, but the stored intention does not reveal whether that condition currently holds. Checking it may require web access, multi-step tool use, and paid calls. Existing systems decide when intentions require attention, but do not allocate the resulting observations under a shared budget. We introduce the first resource-allocation formulation for the external observations required by stored intentions under a shared episode budget. BudgetPM offers two policy variants that share a hard-budget executor. BudgetPM-Static uses a lightweight Logistic scorer to learn whether a check improves the current decision. BudgetPM-Sequential distills full-episode hindsight schedules into a lightweight policy that decides when to spend or reserve capacity using only pre-query information at deployment. We evaluate BudgetPM against two public memory-agent systems, five matched controls, and four hand-designed monitoring or budget-adaptation rules. Across two benchmarks and three backbones, BudgetPM-Static outperforms adapted Mem0 and PMA workflows. On PM-Bench, its Logistic scorer reaches competitive quality--cost operating points alongside higher-capacity scorers and retains 99.9--100\% of unconstrained quality with 42--54\% fewer observations. Under severe scarcity and the same hard caps, BudgetPM-Sequential exceeds the strongest tested natural monitoring schedule by 1.92--2.58 Set F1 points. It reaches the same Set F1 and on-time recall with 16--33\% fewer observations. Matched attribution, exact-cost analysis, and a fixed-budget load intervention link this gain to competition between present and future opportunities. These results yield a demand--capacity design rule: local gating works when capacity covers demand, while future-aware supervision adds value when observations compete across time.
  </details>

- **[Action-Space Shaping for LLM Agents: Measuring and Mitigating Tool-Schema Bias](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34971)**  `arXiv:2609.34971`  
  _Yinhong Liu, Zhili Tan, Zilin Wang, Zhijiang Guo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown strong performance on tool-use agentic tasks when given a fixed tool schema. Yet a tool schema is not the action space of an agent; it is merely one interface representation of it. The same executable action can be exposed through many different, functionally equivalent tool definitions, and an agent that has truly learned a task should behave consistently across them. We show that current agents often do not, a phenomenon we term schema bias. To study this systematically, we introduce an executable transformation framework that rewrites a native tool schema using nine operators, including merging and splitting tools, altering how a single tool is expressed, and distributing one action across several dependent calls. The tasks, executable actions, and reachable states remain fixed, so any change in success is attributable to the interface alone. Evaluating eleven LLMs, including two closed models, on up to 32 schema variants, we ask how large schema bias is, how it manifests, whether the difficulty of a schema variant can be predicted without a full evaluation, and whether training removes it. We find that schema bias is substantial even for the newest models: success rates range from complete failure to 97% depending solely on the schema. To reliably estimate schema difficulty, it requires running a small sample of the target queries. Training repairs a schema variant only when that variant appears in the training data.
  </details>

- **[R$^2$ Flow: Recursive Self-Improvement via Recursive Skill Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33867)**  `arXiv:2609.33867`  
  _Mingda Zhang, Qiang Huang, Yanjin Li, Zijia Wang, Qika Lin, Xiaoying Tang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based agents can improve themselves across tasks by reusing and revising the skills they orchestrate into executable procedures. Flow-based training fits this loop: it samples procedures in proportion to reward, and the flow through each skill credits it for the next library revision. Three obstacles stand in the way of making this self-improvement reliable: flow training suffers strategy collapse over tree-structured histories; nonnegative flow-based credit rewards frequent use as if it were benefit; and library edits rest on the task reward the policy optimizes. We introduce R$^2$ Flow, a recursive self-improvement framework that alternates policy learning, independent verification, and versioned skill-library updates on a shared-state orchestration graph. The graph merges histories that differ only in the order of independent steps, allowing flow training to pool evidence across equivalent executions. A flow-share readout of the trained flow, invariant to the backward policy, and a separate signed utility rank which skills to change, verifier evidence decides whether an edit is warranted, and a residual-variance plateau sets when to update. Committed edits reshape the graph the next policy learns on, realizing recursive skill evolution. Across question answering, mathematical reasoning, interactive decision making, and code generation, R$^2$ Flow improves task accuracy and library-edit precision over heuristic orchestration, reinforcement learning, and skill-evolution baselines, and transfers across executors. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Porimon: An LLM-Based Pokémon Battle Agent Enhanced by Long/Short-Term Knowledge Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32544)**  `arXiv:2609.32544`  
  _Dongyin Zhuo, Fengjunjie Pan, Nenad Petrovic, Alois Knoll_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper, we use Pokémon Battles as a case study to investigate how to improve the performance of LLM-based agents in tasks that require opponent-aware planning without additional fine-tuning. We propose Long/Short-Term Knowledge Augmented Generation (LSTKAG), a mechanism that enables LLM-based agents to leverage past states of the current task and retrieve experience summaries from similar previous task instances based on the current state. Based on LSTKAG, we design Porimon, an LLM-based agent structure for Pokémon Battles. For optimization, we introduce an external API for precise damage calculation and more detailed information about the game. We conduct tournament-like evaluation experiments comprising 15,000 battles for hyperparameter optimization, ablation studies, and performance evaluation. The results indicate that Porimon-based players with hyperparameter optimization significantly outperform players based on PokéLLMon, an LLM-based agent structure proposed in previous research, and the rule-based heuristic player. Furthermore, our ablation study shows that Porimon variants outperform the one without extension in game information retrieval, which shows the contribution of that extension. However, the current experiment results are inconclusive regarding the contribution of Long-Term KAG. These results suggest that introducing external resources, information from previous states of the current task, and experience summaries from similar previous task instances could elevate the performance of LLM-based agents designed for tasks requiring opponent-aware planning.
  </details>

- **[Evolutionary Safety of Recursive Self-Improving AI: Taxonomy, Risk Discovery, and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31186)**  `arXiv:2609.31186`  
  _Chang Gong, Jingping Bi, Di Yao, Ran Cheng, Yaodong Yang, Kaile Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rapid advances in AI are bringing recursive self-improvement (RSI) closer to practice, with growing progress in model self-training, self-evolving agents, and automated AI development. Yet the very mechanisms that enable continuous improvement also introduce new challenges for AI safety. Unlike isolated failures, safety risks can persist across updates, accumulate through repeated adaptation, and propagate to successor systems. More importantly, the processes that generate and select future improvements may themselves evolve, making safety increasingly difficult to assess and maintain. This raises a fundamental question, i.e., how can safety be maintained when both AI systems and the processes that improve them continue to change? Therefore, we introduce Evolutionary Safety to study how safety properties are preserved, weakened, or restored throughout persistent and recursive self-improvement. We characterize six recurring manifestations of evolutionary risk and develop a five-domain taxonomy that captures how safety-relevant changes arise, persist, and propagate across evolving systems. Building on this foundation, we establish a framework for risk discovery and evaluation across states, updates, trajectories, and lineages, and examine governance principles for controlling modifications, verifying and authorizing updates, and ensuring traceability and recovery. We further identify open problems in maintaining safety as both AI systems and their improvement mechanisms continue to evolve.
  </details>

- **[Cross-Modal Emotion Understanding: A Transformer-GAT Approach for Dialogue Emotion Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.29556)**  `arXiv:2609.29556`  
  _Jiaqi Qiao, Yifan Lyu, Xiujuan Xu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal emotion recognition is a key research area in affective computing, with applications in sentiment analysis, intelligent customer service, and human-computer interaction. However, existing methods often rely on single-modal features or simple multimodal fusion, failing to capture the synergy between global and local contexts, which limits model performance and emotion understanding. To address this challenge, we propose Transformer-GAT, a hybrid framework that combines Transformer and the Graph Attention Network to enable cross-modal emotion understanding. The Transformer is used to capture global semantic information, while the Graph Attention Network is employed to model fine-grained relationships between modalities, thereby enhancing the representation of emotional features. Experiments on the IEMOCAP and MELD datasets show that our model achieves weighted F1 scores of 72.45% and 77.37%, outperforming state-of-the-art methods. These results demonstrate that Transformer-GAT effectively integrates multimodal features, balances global and local contexts, and provides deeper emotional insights, offering new directions for multimodal emotion computing.
  </details>

- **[From Self-Distillation to Self-Practice: Privileged Information for Multi-Turn Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.29051)**  `arXiv:2609.29051`  
  _Xingyu Su, Abhishek Kumar, Qing Ping, Youzhi Luo, Jonathan Buck, Zach Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) has become a popular recipe for post-training LLM agents. It supervises the agent model at the token level with a stronger teacher view of the same model, obtained by conditioning on privileged information (PI). In this work, we show that in multi-turn agents, this paradigm teaches the student to act with confidence but without the information behind it. The trained agent behaves as if it had privileged information it never observed, and its performance falls well short of plain RL, in the worst case below the untrained base model. Therefore, we propose Privileged Self-Practice (PSP), which keeps the PI and moves it from the loss to the sampler. When the student's rollouts on a task mostly fail, we inject a short per-task instruction written by an analyzer model, sample the task again with the instruction in context, and train on the result with an unchanged GRPO objective. The privileged information stays in the prompt and never enters the loss. Across AppWorld and SWE-bench Verified, with three different student models, PSP obtains the best average score in every setting and is the only method that consistently outperforms plain GRPO, with relative improvements over GRPO of 7% to 58% in average score across settings. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Uncertainty Reduction: Calibrated Planning for Interactive Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27847)**  `arXiv:2608.27847`  
  _Yue Zhou, Haiyang Zhou, Jin Zhang, Kong Wang, Zihao Rao, Maolin Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interactive medical diagnosis requires an agent to gather patient evidence through sequential questions before committing to a diagnosis. Large language models (LLMs) now make such consultations feasible, and existing approaches typically select questions by how much they reduce uncertainty among candidate diseases. However, this objective is insensitive to the unequal clinical costs of diagnostic errors and omitted severe alternatives, so minimizing uncertainty need not minimize final diagnostic loss. To bridge this gap, we propose CaRDI (Calibrated Risk for Diagnostic Interaction), a planning framework built around a clinical loss combining primary diagnosis error with severity-weighted differential omission. Diagnostic and safety beliefs estimate the two loss components, while conformal residual calibration corrects for underestimation from partial evidence. The calibrated risk then guides question selection and stopping by balancing expected risk reduction against interaction cost. Experiments on multiple medical benchmarks show that CaRDI improves accuracy while requiring fewer questions. Further analysis shows that replacing entropy with clinical risk as the planning objective reduces clinical loss by 21.5% and decreases severe omissions. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Teach the Magnitude, Not the Direction: Verifier-Anchored Credit Assignment for Multi-Turn Multi-Step LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13179)**  `arXiv:2608.13179`  
  _Zechuan Wang, Siyuan Lu, Hongxuan Zhang, Linjian Mo, Chenyi Zhuang, Leilei Gan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) directly optimizes verifier-defined outcomes for multi-turn tool-use agents, yet its trajectory-level credit assignment conflates heterogeneous per-turn outcomes into a single reward signal. On-policy distillation provides dense per-token supervision but uses a teacher-defined token-level objective and can be prone to gradient concentration collapse. We introduce $\textbf{CrEST}$, a hierarchical credit assignment framework that anchors each sampled token's scalar advantage sign to verified rewards while incorporating dense magnitude signals from a privileged self-teacher. $\textbf{CrEST}$ resolves credit at two levels: turn-segmented verified advantages address inter-turn dilution, while entropy-gated self-teacher modulation refines intra-turn token contributions. Experiments on BFCL V3 and WildToolBench show that $\textbf{CrEST}$ consistently outperforms both RL and distillation baselines across two model scales, with the largest gains on long-trajectory and strict session-level metrics. Our work demonstrates that the teacher's role in policy optimization can be reduced from determining token-level reinforcement or suppression to modulating update magnitudes, enabling dense credit assignment while keeping verifier rewards as the training objective.
  </details>

- **[Locating Failure in Multi-Page Visually Rich Document Understanding Systems: An Empirical Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07943)**  `arXiv:2608.07943`  
  _Lewei Xu, Yihao Ding, Zihan Xu, Daniel Yitian Su, Daochang Liu, Siwen Luo, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-page visually rich document understanding (MP-VRDU) systems can fail when evidence is not faithfully represented, not selected, or not used correctly, yet final accuracy alone does not reveal where failures occur. We present an empirical attribution framework that examines these failures through controlled interventions in a unified document question answering pipeline. Experiments on MMLongBench-Doc, with cross-dataset and cross-family checks, show that complementary modalities can reduce sensitivity to conversion errors: images mitigate parser errors, while extracted text reduces dependence on visual resolution. Adding related non-gold pages can both repair incorrect answers and break correct ones, even when all annotated evidence is present, revealing instability beneath aggregate accuracy. Abstention trades unsupported answers for false refusals, while its interaction with reasoning instructions differs across backbones. Qualitative examples and accuracy--cost comparisons connect these findings to guidance on representation, evidence selection, and reasoner choice under deployment constraints.
  </details>

- **[Q-Reflector: Learning GUI Action Reflection with Generated Conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05587)**  `arXiv:2608.05587`  
  _Linqiang Guo, Wei Liu, Li Gu, Yang Wang, Tse-Hsun, Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
