- **[Deconstructing Multi-Task Active Learning: The Paradox of Gradient Conflict and Orthogonal Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23623)**  `arXiv:2610.23623`  
  _Juho Jung, Seongjae Kang, Jinyoung Han_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing multi-task active learning (MTL-AL) methods predominantly rely on linear scalarization of heterogeneous objectives such as uncertainty, diversity, and gradient conflict. We argue that this entangled formulation is fundamentally limited, as linear combination fails to capture Pareto-optimal trade-offs under conflicting objectives. We propose a shift from combination to deconstruction and introduce a principled framework that rethinks MTL-AL along three orthogonal dimensions. First, we identify the Conflict Paradox: although the optimization literature treats gradient conflict as interference to be suppressed, penalizing conflict when ranking candidates is measurably worse than not scoring it at all. We therefore decouple the two concerns: the acquisition function does not score conflict, and the resulting interference is resolved by gradient surgery during training. Second, we introduce Orthogonal Decomposition, separating acquisition into Informativeness and Exploration. This two-stage process, informativeness filtering followed by coverage maximization, prevents "diversity dilution" by excluding uninformative outliers before enforcing diversity. Third, we propose Cross-Task Mutual Information (CTMI), a covariance-based statistic of cross-task dependency computed from MC-dropout task entropies. Extensive experiments on COCO, Cityscapes, and NYUv2, including comparisons against recent MTL-AL and data-selection baselines, demonstrate consistent improvements over state-of-the-art methods, highlighting that structural rethinking of acquisition yields larger gains than incremental heuristic design. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ThetreStageAI: Multi-View Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Structured Bengali Theatre Production Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23618)**  `arXiv:2610.23618`  
  _Md. Mehedi Hasan Naeem, Mst. Kamrunnahar Ruma, Shakila Sultana, Nafiza Anjum, Nasreen Jannatun Nemu, Md. Sujan Ali_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Staging a theatre performance requires coordinating dialogue, actor movement, stage directions, lighting focus, cue timing, and equipment constraints within the same production plan. This work presents ThetreStageAI, a retrieval augmented framework for structured Bengali theatre production planning. Rather than retrieving from one mixed corpus, the system maintains separate scene, actor-blocking, and lighting views. Bengali text is embedded with BGE-M3 and indexed in view-specific Qdrant collections. Retrieved references are combined with a production brief and passed to Gemini, which generates schema-constrained JSON containing characters, scenes, dialogue, stage directions, blocking, lighting, and optional sound cues. Structural and semantic checks are applied before acceptance, with at most one constrained repair attempt. We also construct a human-guided, LLM-assisted synthetic dataset of 500 short Bengali theatre records that expands to 1,500 retrieval documents. Dataset diagnostics confirm consistent identifiers and valid lighting fields while also revealing substantial dialogue repetition. A paired camera-ready evaluation compares single-combined and full multi-view retrieval under matched settings. The source code, dataset, and evaluation materials are publicly available.
  </details>

- **[The Sign Code: The Hidden Binary Nature of Deep Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23616)**  `arXiv:2610.23616`  
  _Niclas Göring, Shuofeng Zhang, Roi Holtzman, Yoonsoo Nam, Ard A. Louis_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Every neuron in a trained network outputs a number that can be separated into its sign (positive or negative) and its magnitude (absolute size). We call the set of all neuron signs the \textit{sign code}. This raises a key question: does classification depend more on the sign code or on the magnitudes? For overparameterized vision classifiers with sign-preserving activations, the answer is overwhelmingly the sign code. A $k$-nearest neighbor classifier operating only on this binary sign code achieves accuracy comparable to the full model across different architectures, activation functions, and datasets. Moreover, replacing all magnitudes in every layer with random noise while keeping the original signs barely changes residual network performance. In contrast, flipping just 20\% of the signs reduces accuracy to chance. Models generalize well when same-class inputs share similar sign codes. When this overlap is disrupted by a regularizer, training accuracy remains high, but test performance collapses. A margin decomposition helps explain these results: the sign-only contribution to each logit grows as $\sqrt{d}$ in network width, dominating the magnitude residual. The natural unit of analysis for these networks may not be through their continuous weight space but instead through their discrete sign codes.
  </details>

- **[How Many, but How Sure? Class Competition as a Confidence Proxy in Multi-Class Density-Based Counting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23611)**  `arXiv:2610.23611`  
  _Villanelle O'Reilly, Georgios Leontidis, Marc Hanheide_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-class density estimators can count objects in crowded scenes, but their class-wise outputs offer no indication of how reliably density mass has been allocated. We introduce a training-free confidence proxy based on local competition between the strongest class channels. The resulting prominence measure requires only the predicted density maps, with no retraining or access to model internals. We test whether residual competition is diagnostic of cross-class leakage by attenuating contested density at inference time. On DSACA predictions for the Hicks et al. dataset, this reduces overall MAE from 0.920 to 0.846, suggesting that class competition contains useful information about count reliability.
  </details>

- **[BMuon: Budgeting Muon's Spectral Geometry for Structured Compressibility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23571)**  `arXiv:2610.23571`  
  _Rida Assalouh, Tom Jacobs, Rebekka Burkholz, Yani Ioannou, Umut Şimşekli_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Muon has emerged as a strong optimizer for neural network training and admits an interpretation as steepest descent under a spectral-norm geometry. Yet we find that Muon-trained Transformers can degrade sharply when their MLP hidden channels are pruned after training. To reconcile Muon's optimization performance with structured compressibility, we introduce Budget Muon (BMuon), which modifies Muon's spectral geometry so that hidden channels across blocks compete for a shared allocation budget. The resulting oracle is exactly normalized steepest descent under a family of norms that interpolate between Muon's geometry and structured sparse geometries. Our principal sparsity control is an interpretable global channel-allocation budget, rather than a setting-dependent sparsity-penalty coefficient. For linear factorization models, we show that Muon admits minimum-cost exact representations with redundant hidden width, whereas under the BMuon oracle geometry and a sufficiently scarce budget, every minimum-cost representation is width-minimal. For practical training, we derive a minorization-maximization surrogate for the exact allocation problem. On ImageNet-1K with ViT-S/16, BMuon improves over Muon under gradual structured MLP pruning, and its learned allocations provide a more effective pruning criterion than weight magnitude. On a 1B-parameter LLaMA-style model trained on FineWeb, BMuon improves over Muon under post-training MLP channel pruning.
  </details>

- **[Out of Support, Not Out of Reach: In-Context Learning with Unsupported Instructions and Composed Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23528)**  `arXiv:2610.23528`  
  _Xuhan Tong, Yuchen Zeng, Jiawei Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In-Context Learning (ICL) enables pretrained LLMs to adapt to downstream tasks from a small set of input-output demonstrations, without parameter updates. Yet existing theory primarily explains ICL as identifying a latent task already represented in pretraining, leaving unclear whether ICL can succeed when either the instruction or the target task falls outside the pretraining support. We develop a theoretical framework under mild assumptions that separates these two forms of distribution shift. For target tasks represented in pretraining, we show that demonstrations can identify the task and reduce sensitivity to pretrain-supported prompt templates as their number increases. For an unsupported instruction, we show that under a fixed model-induced reference extension, demonstration evidence can dominate an $N$-independent instruction shift while preserving the exponential identification rate. We then use the same ICL framework as an interpretive lens for long-horizon target tasks that are not represented in pretraining but admit a plan that decomposes them into pretraining-supported subtasks. Applying the supported-task analysis step by step yields a standard accumulated-error bound and clarifies how invoking familiar subtasks along an intermediate-state trajectory can avoid directly selecting the unsupported complete task. Together, our results characterize when demonstrations, instructions, and task decomposition can support ICL beyond the corresponding pretraining supports.
  </details>

- **[Uncertainty-aware AI-enabled framework for predicting hydrogen dispersion in confined spaces to enhance operator situational awareness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23515)**  `arXiv:2610.23515`  
  _Niclas Flehmig, Mary Ann Lundteigen, Shen Yin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present a two-stage AI-enabled framework for enhancing operator situational awareness during compressed hydrogen bunkering in maritime confined spaces. In the event of a leak, the framework first estimates the unknown leakage rate from early sensor readings and then forecasts how the hydrogen cloud will disperse over the next time steps, providing operators with a predicted concentration map together with confidence bounds. The framework is based on two independent AI models building one AI system. The AI system is designed as a decision-support tool. It augments sensor readings, and thus enhances operator situational awareness and supports decision-making. This enables preventive safety barriers to be deployed before hazardous concentrations are reached, while an independent emergency shutdown system remains the ultimate safety layer. The system design incorporates safeguards to ensure the AI system operates within its competence envelope. Trained and tested on an open dataset of confined-space hydrogen releases, the framework achieves a mean absolute error of $0.052$~g/s in leakage-rate estimation and $\approx0.018$ in hydrogen-concentration prediction, approaching the sensor precision bound of $\pm0.01$. The hydrogen-concentration prediction provides well-calibrated uncertainty estimates that allow operators to judge the reliability of each forecast. These results suggest that the framework may provide early information about hazardous concentration build-up, potentially supporting preventive actions by operators.
  </details>

- **[A Topological Signature of Learning under Noisy Labels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23491)**  `arXiv:2610.23491`  
  _Zhongtian Sun, Fan Mo, Prayag Tiwari, Kelin Xia_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  How does noisy supervision change the representations learned by a neural network? Training accuracy measures agreement with training labels but provides little information about representation geometry. We use persistent homology to track loop structure across distance thresholds in neural activations. Experiments span image classification, text classification, and modular arithmetic. On a fixed CIFAR-10/ResNet-18 training probe, equally sized clean-label and corrupted-label subsets show no detectable persistence difference at epoch 10, whereas corrupted-label subsets have higher persistence at epoch 50. The contrast survives uniform-scale controls, even though predictions match the assigned noisy labels for only 7-11% of corrupted probe examples at that checkpoint. Normalisation preserves the crossover between clean and noisy training trajectories on CIFAR-10/ResNet-18, whereas CIFAR-100/ResNet-18 shows no crossover under any of the five evaluated constructions. In a separate noise-rate calibration comparison across five pipelines, mean activation norm achieves a higher mean in-sample R2 than raw persistence (0.936 versus 0.908). These findings show that noisy supervision leaves a measurable geometric signature even when agreement with corrupted labels is low. Comparing persistence under scale controls with label-fitting measurements offers a way to study how representations respond to label corruption beyond what training accuracy reveals.
  </details>

- **[Predictable Scaling: Efficient Model Exploration via Scaling-Law Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23453)**  `arXiv:2610.23453`  
  _Aishwarya Wesanekar, Sheng-Tao Yang, Jiawei Zhang, Flavio Sales Truzzi, Chen Chen, Nader Beigiparast, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating candidate changes (hypotheses) to recommendation models requires full-scale training runs consuming thousands of GPU-hours, severely limiting the hypothesis space engineers can feasibly explore. We introduce Predictable Scaling (PS), a systematic framework that exploits empirically verified regularity conditions to predict full-scale model performance from small-scale proxies. PS constructs scaled-down variants via composable scaling functions along distinct resource axes, and projects metrics to the target scale via curve fitting with calibrated uncertainty estimates. We compare three prediction strategies of increasing expressiveness: even the simplest (raw proxy) achieves prediction error within the empirical noise floor of repeated-run variance; the most expressive (Gaussian Process) provides an indicative 15% relative MAE improvement and calibrated uncertainty. Evaluation on two ranking models of increasing complexity---a task-specific model and a foundation model---demonstrates that PS correctly ranks actionable hypotheses across diverse hypothesis types at 79-88% compute reductions.
  </details>

- **[Regularized free deconvolution for spectral repair of resampling distortion in high-dimensional particle filters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23448)**  `arXiv:2610.23448`  
  _Deming Song, Chi-Kin Lam, Kefeng Li, Hui Yu, Xilin Li, Huagang Lin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Resampling is the standard remedy for weight degeneracy in particle filters, but in high dimensions it distorts the spectrum of the ensemble covariance in a way that is computable from the importance weights. This paper studies the inverse problem: recovering the pre-resampling spectrum from the observed weights and the empirical spectrum alone, and using it without biasing the filter. We first show that the forward spectral law extends to random importance weights once one conditions on the weights together with the realized offspring counts, rather than on the weights alone. We then construct a regularized free-deconvolution estimator, prove its consistency with an explicit error budget at a fixed resolution scale, and certify model mismatch through a computable forward residual. The repaired spectrum enters the filter only as the preconditioner of a Metropolis-adjusted rejuvenation move, so that spectral error affects mixing efficiency but not the invariant target. For the full filtering cycle, we derive a geometrically summable error recursion under stable scalar dynamics and an explicit law for the cost of abstaining from repair. Pre-registered simulations show that the inversion reduces the spectral error by an order of magnitude, that the corrected preconditioner recovers about 90% of the oracle's sampling efficiency in an anisotropic regime, and that the cost of abstention depends on when abstentions occur rather than on how many. The repair recovers eigenvalue laws, not eigenvectors, and is certified only within an explicitly stated exogenous-weight regime.
  </details>

- **[RECREG: Recovery-based Shielding for Regular Safety Properties in Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23445)**  `arXiv:2610.23445`  
  _Alexander W. Goodall, Francesco Belardinelli_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In many real-world scenarios we need to consider formal requirements, such as safety and constraint compliance, to deploy Reinforcement Learning (RL) agents. In this paper we introduce RecReg, a safe RL framework capable of enforcing non-Markovian regular safety properties via recovery-based shielding. RecReg is a unified approach that supports different levels of a priori knowledge and safety guarantees. The paper primarily focuses on building the theoretical foundations for RecReg in the tabular setting, providing rigorous safety and convergence guarantees that are not present in related work. We provide an extensive set of empirical results using the MASA-Safe-RL library, and we outline an extension of RecReg to deep RL domains using SOTA model-based RL. Our empirical results demonstrate that RecReg achieves efficient learning and matches the theory in this paper.
  </details>

- **[Bridging Compressed Communication and Local Steps via Spectral Method in Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23434)**  `arXiv:2610.23434`  
  _Dmitriy Bystrov, George Chirkov, Daniil Medyakov, Dmitry Bylinkin, Aleksandr Beznosikov_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Federated Learning (FL) enables collaborative training of complex models across large-scale networks of edge devices while keeping data localized. However, data heterogeneity and high communication overhead remain fundamental bottlenecks. Recently, the spectral optimizer Muon has demonstrated remarkable advantages over Adam-like methods in centralized training. While recent efforts have attempted to transition Muon to FL, naive integrations into traditional architectures fail short. Crucially, existing approaches do not support the the communication compression and local steps --- the two foundational techniques of efficient FL. To address this limitation, we introduce a novel spectral FL method that utilizes both local updates and compression. We establish rigorous convergence guarantees under mild assumptions, notably eliminating the restrictive bounded heterogeneity condition. Empirical evaluation on standard image classification benchmarks validates the efficiency and robustness of our approach.
  </details>

- **[Residual Stochastic Concept Bottleneck Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23421)**  `arXiv:2610.23421`  
  _Stephen Marcou, Mateo Espinosa Zarlenga, Sonia Laguna, Moritz Vandenhirtz, Julia E. Vogt, Mateja Jamnik_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Concept-based models (CMs) learn human-interpretable intermediate representations that enable users to understand the model's reasoning and steer its predictions through test-time concept interventions. However, in realistic settings, their performance can be limited by concept incompleteness, where the training concepts fail to capture all task-relevant information. Adding an unconstrained residual pathway to the model's predictive process can overcome the consequences of incompleteness. However, this introduces an inscrutable, leak-prone prediction pathway that may weaken concept interventions, as these often leave residual variables unchanged. To address this limitation, we introduce Residual Stochastic Concept Bottleneck Models (Res-SCBMs), a family of concept-based models that jointly model concept and residual variables through a multivariate Gaussian distribution. Res-SCBMs learn a covariance structure that explicitly captures dependencies between the known concepts and learnt residual variables, allowing interventions to propagate to statistically dependent residual variables through Gaussian conditioning. This extends the intervention capabilities of previous CMs without requiring concept completeness during training. Our results show that, across multiple concept-incomplete benchmarks, Res-SCBMs improve predictive performance over competing baselines while retaining effective test-time concept interventions.
  </details>

- **[Forecasting Voltage Time Series under Anomalies Using a WGAN Model With Lipschitz Control and Progressive Kernel Critic Strategy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23394)**  `arXiv:2610.23394`  
  _Najmieh Sadat Safarabadi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In time-series forecasting tasks, the use of recursive forecasting on high-dimensional data substantially increases the complexity of the prediction process. The main challenge is that long-term dynamics becomes harder to model, since the sequences show greater temporal variability. To counteract this limitation and improve the modeling of long-term dynamics, and more importantly, address high-dimensional recursive forecasting, this paper uses a Wasserstein GAN equipped with a progressive Lipschitz penalty and an adaptive kernel. This model is capable of taking a window of the data stream and forecasting the next values, subsequently reconstructing the time-series stream and dynamically adapting its kernel to each new window. The proopsed model is a deep learning framework based on a Wasserstein GAN (WGAN-CLP) with an adaptive kernel to model alterations in streaming voltage time series. To establish the methodological foundation, we review the WGAN-LP formulation another variant model in this class and extend its framework through an adaptive kernel critic model. We then evaluate this extended model using power ratios, correlation coefficient (CC), and relative root mean squared error (RRMSE). The experimental results demonstrate that the proposed model outperforms benchmark methods in both accuracy and robustness. The evaluation shows promising performance of the WGAN-CLP with an adaptive kernel framework in forecasting sequence alterations across the voltage and energy time-series datasets.
  </details>

- **[RTSM: Adapting Recursive Models to Multivariate Time-Series Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23353)**  `arXiv:2610.23353`  
  _Georg Tenzing, Anna Tegon, Luca Benini, Thorir Mar Ingolfsson_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate multivariate time-series classification requires extracting temporal features and integrating information across a sequence. Weight-tied refinement offers a way to repeatedly process these features without allocating new parameters at each stage. Applying this principle to time series requires choosing both a temporal representation and a latent-state organization suited to classification. We introduce the Recursive Time-Series Model (RTSM), which combines a learned convolutional temporal tokenizer, single-state weight-tied refinement, and a pooled classification readout. Across all 30 UEA datasets, RTSM achieves 72.81% mean accuracy under validation-based configuration and checkpoint selection, the highest observed mean among the 17 evaluated deep-learning models and 1.60 percentage points above MambaSL. Ablations on eight datasets favor the proposed tokenizer over a simple convolutional embedding and the single-state model over the tested two-state variants. The complete classifier reaches 86.10% mean accuracy versus 84.79% for a separately trained tokenizer-only classifier. These findings identify temporal encoding and state organization as key design choices for adapting recursive architectures to time series. The resulting classifier combines competitive accuracy with a shared refinement module whose parameter count is independent of computational depth.
  </details>

- **[How Finite-Rank Bottlenecks Shape the Low-Rank Adaptation Landscape](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23343)**  `arXiv:2610.23343`  
  _Long Nguyen-Chi, Quynh Nguyen, Thanh Nguyen-Cung, Binh T. Nguyen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) has become the standard method for parameter-efficient fine-tuning of large pretrained models, yet theoretical explanations for why low-rank updates suffice remain incomplete. Most existing analyses rely on the Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK) regime, which linearizes the network around the pretrained weights. In this work, we take a different approach that requires no linearization: our key observation is that, once the backbone is frozen, the loss sees each adapted weight matrix only through a finite-rank linear map induced by the frozen activations. Trace-norm regularization then forces the optimizer to pick a low-rank matrix among all matrices that produce the same observed output. This yields global minimizers whose ranks are controlled by the rank of this map, which we call the \emph{bottleneck rank}, rather than the sample size. For multi-head attention, we obtain tighter rank upper bounds by exploiting two additional invariances: row-wise softmax is insensitive to additive row constants in the score matrix, and value updates are observable only after attention weighting and frozen output projection. For the factorized LoRA objective with weight decay, we prove a complementary result on the nonconvex side: after an arbitrarily small generic positive semidefinite perturbation, every first-order stationary point is rank-deficient once the LoRA rank exceeds a threshold determined by the bottleneck rank. Finally, we show that global minimizers of the perturbed objective are near-optimal for the original trace-norm problem, and we provide uniform generalization bounds governed by the spectral structure of the frozen features.
  </details>

- **[SETAREH Transform: A Sorting-Assisted Haar-Wavelet Sparsification Method for Approximate Inner-Product Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23338)**  `arXiv:2610.23338`  
  _Seyed Kamaledin Setarehdan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern deep learning systems increasingly rely on high-dimensional vector operations, in which large input and parameter vectors are repeatedly stored, transferred, and processed. Although substantial research has focused on reducing neural-network model size through pruning, quantization, knowledge distillation, and efficient architectures, the storage, memory-traffic, and communication costs associated with repeatedly presented high-dimensional input vectors remain important practical considerations, particularly in distributed and edge-computing environments. This paper proposes the SETAREH (Sorting-assisted Energy-concentrating Transform for Approximate Representation using Haar wavelets) transform, a Haar-wavelet-based sparsification approach for approximating the inner product between a fixed high-dimensional coefficient vector and successive input vectors. By applying the same sequence of permutations and Haar transforms to both vectors, SETAREH preserves the exact inner product before truncation. It then retains only a subset of the transformed coefficient-input pairs, reducing their representation cost while introducing a controllable approximation error. To evaluate SETAREH, we consider two complementary experimental settings: a synthetic Gaussian-vector baseline and a real apparent-age estimation task using a convolutional neural network on the APPA-REAL dataset. The results show that SETAREH can approximate the evaluated inner products using substantially fewer transformed coefficient-input pairs than the original vector dimension. The findings support its use as a potential structured data-representation method for repeated inner products with fixed weights, while the practical benefit depends on approximation accuracy and the cost of storing or transmitting permutation metadata.
  </details>

- **[HiPredict: Predicting the missing HiRISE RED4 spectral channel with machine learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23335)**  `arXiv:2610.23335`  
  _Ardan Suphi, Ben Moseley, Gareth S Collins, Valentin T Bickel, Vidhya G Rangarajan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The High Resolution Imaging Science Experiment (HiRISE) onboard the Mars Reconnaissance Orbiter (MRO) acquires imagery of Mars at 25--30 cm/pixel, supporting studies of martian geology, climate history, and landing-site characterization. Its 14-detector focal plane assembly includes several overlapping detectors that enable the acquisition of three-channel colour-infrared images, which are important for identifying compositional variations and studying active surface processes. In 2023, a detector in the center of the focal plane assembly (RED4) began failing intermittently, creating a gap in acquired imagery and reducing the instrument's colour-imaging capability by half. The HiRISE processing pipeline provides a synthetic red (SYN4) channel obtained by linearly scaling the co-located infrared (IR10) channel. However, this approach cannot capture observed non-linear relationships between the colour channels, limiting its accuracy and applicability. Here, we present HiPredict, a machine-learning framework that reconstructs RED4 from co-located IR10 and blue-green (BG12) imagery, neighbouring red-channel observations, and acquisition metadata. The model employs a U-Net architecture to learn non-linear spatiotemporal-spectral relationships between these inputs and is trained on 81,717 training samples, representing 13,625 observations. Evaluated on 23,348 test samples, representing 3,892 pre-failure observations, HiPredict reconstructs RED4 with a Normalised Mean Absolute Error of 1.1%, substantially outperforming SYN4 (3.1%). The reconstructed products improve colour image recovery and more closely represent features of interest such as icy craters, stratigraphy exposed along ridge crests and chloride deposits. We release HiPredict as an open software tool. This work demonstrates the potential of machine learning to mitigate data loss as planetary missions age and naturally degrade.
  </details>

- **[Data-Efficient Classifier Pretraining Through Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23289)**  `arXiv:2610.23289`  
  _Qinyu Li, Yee Whye Teh, Chris Holmes, Joel Jaskari_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine learning with limited data is susceptible to overfitting, especially with modern deep neural networks. Prior to the introduction of AlexNet, reconstruction-based pretraining with auto-encoder and denoising auto-encoder were found to be beneficial, partially due to the limited size of the training sets at the time. Motivated by these traditional approaches, we propose DiffPT, a simple diffusion modeling approach to pretrain a given classifier backbone for limited domain-specific training data. We experiment on time-series data, standard natural-image benchmarks, and medical imaging datasets, and contrast the diffusion approach to a multitude of modern and traditional un/self-supervised methods. While contrastive pretraining performs better on CIFAR-10 and Tiny ImageNet in low-data regimes, DiffPT is particularly effective on medical imaging and time-series tasks, where it outperforms self-supervised baselines across a range of low-data regimes. Our results suggest that DiffPT is a simple and effective approach for data-efficient classifier pretraining on different modalities.
  </details>

- **[DeDiCa: A Scalable Deconfounding Discrete Causal Generative Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23272)**  `arXiv:2610.23272`  
  _Kunchangtai Liang, Adrián Javaloy, Antonio Vergari, Isabel Valera_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce DeDiCa, a practical framework for efficient causal inference with discrete variables and hidden confounding that revisits classical probabilistic modeling through a tensorized parameterization of causal Bayesian networks, enabling tractable inference. Given a causal graph, DeDiCa is trained once on observational data by maximum likelihood, exactly marginalizing over hidden confounders, and can then estimate any (proxy-)identifiable interventional query. Despite this deliberately simple parameterization, DeDiCa matches state-of-the-art neural causal generative models in ATE estimation, while supporting memory-efficient CPU training and exact interventional inference in milliseconds. Compared with causal foundation models, DeDiCa scales more effectively with additional observational samples and achieves competitive accuracy at substantially lower inference time and memory. DeDiCa provides a tractable, accurate, and scalable approach to causal inference in discrete settings with hidden confounding.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Marginal Influence: Interaction-Aware Memory Selection for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23266)**  `arXiv:2610.23266`  
  _BHVSP Subrahmanyam, Neeraj Choudhary_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-agent memory selection often uses retrieval rankings based on record relevance or individual influence. Such rankings cannot identify sufficient subsets under redundant or jointly necessary evidence. We study per-decision memory sufficiency: selecting a small subset that preserves full-memory behavior. To separate search error from model error, we introduce JointCoreBench, a benchmark with nine interaction families, six workflow domains, natural-language decisions, and exact sufficient-set oracles, and JointCore, a relevance cost-ordered group-deletion baseline that tests blocks before individual records. On 6,912 exact conditions, cost-ordered JointCore recovers a minimum-cost sufficient set in 100.0% of cases, reduces subset evaluations from 30.00 to 11.13 (62.9%), and achieves 87.8% active-memory savings. Across four 12B-70B model families and 432 tasks per model, its unweighted mean is 10.28 versus 16.00 model queries and 50.6% versus 43.1% minimum-cost recovery relative to cost aware singleton deletion. Both methods preserve full-memory behavior under the deterministic primary interface. Tests with physical deletion, UNKNOWN masks, PENDING sentinels, counterfactual values, and alternative labels identify interface dependent behavior. JointCore is an efficient benchmark baseline; the study does not establish deployment-ready memory compression.
  </details>

- **[ADT: All-Atom Diffusion Tokenizer for 3D Molecular Structures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23265)**  `arXiv:2610.23265`  
  _Xuantuo Huang, Encheng Su, Aoran Wang, Pan Tan, Shengyong Xu, Yizhou Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Discrete structural tokenization provides a promising route to autoregressive 3D molecular generation. Yet, existing tokenizers face a trade-off between compact representation and all-atom reconstruction: atom-level tokens preserve atomic details but produce long sequences, while compact residue-level protein tokens typically omit side-chain geometry. To overcome this limitation, we propose All-atom Diffusion Tokenizer (ADT), which decouples token granularity from reconstruction resolution. ADT uses one structural token per atom for small molecules and one token per protein residue that jointly represents backbone and side-chain geometry. To mitigate geometric distortion introduced by quantization, we leverage the generative capacity of diffusion models to recover fine-grained atomic geometry beyond deterministic decoding from discrete tokens. Extensive experiments across small molecules and proteins demonstrate that ADT achieves strong reconstruction from compact discrete representations while enabling high-quality autoregressive generation of both small molecules and all-atom protein structures. These results establish compact structural tokens as an effective discrete interface for all-atom molecular reconstruction and generation.
  </details>

- **[FLARE: Physics-Grounded Evaluation of Language-Model Agents for Feeder Restoration with Degraded Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23251)**  `arXiv:2610.23251`  
  _BHVSP Subrahmanyam, Neeraj Choudhary_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Restoring service after distribution-grid faults requires decisions from incomplete and conflicting information while respecting electrical constraints. Addressing this need, we introduce FLARE (Feeder-Level Agent Restoration Evaluation), a physics-grounded benchmark for post-isolation feeder restoration. FLARE con- tains 800 AC-feasible cases across five observation settings and allows agents to gather evidence, evaluate switches, and perform restoration actions under fixed operational limits. We evaluate five instruction-tuned models over 78,750 episodes, including topology, distributed-generation, and loading shifts. Mean normalized regret ranges from 0.082 to 0.148. Although every model has zero median regret, P95 regret ranges from 0.814 to 1.000, revealing an important failure tail. Shadow replay shows that 96.4% of 15,706 guard-rejected actions would have caused a physical violation. FLARE enables reproducible evaluation of language-model based restoration support while emphasizing the need for external safety checks.
  </details>

- **[Anisotropic Representation Dynamics in Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23242)**  `arXiv:2610.23242`  
  _Nguyen Do, My T. Thai_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Small differences between internal representations can shrink or grow as they pass through a Transformer layer, affecting how information is preserved and how sensitive the model is to perturbations. Yet it remains unclear when changes in attention weights can turn a representation difference that would otherwise shrink into one that grows. We develop a \textbf{Mixture-Gated Theory of Anisotropic Representation Dynamics} that separates self-attention into value transport, query gating, and key gating. Our analysis reveals how the geometry of attended representations constrains gating and how interactions between these mechanisms can reverse contraction induced by value transport. We formalize this phenomenon as gating-induced regime switching, where a representation difference contracts under fixed attention routing but expands under full attention. We derive a necessary and sufficient spectral criterion that certifies the existence or absence of switching and identifies switching directions without exhaustive search. Beyond existence, our theory characterizes maximum relative switching strength, certifies the severity of contraction that gating can overcome, and determines the largest dimension of a subspace in which every nonzero direction switches. Experiments across four Transformer architectures validate these predictions through analytical and finite-forward comparisons, revealing distinct and joint query--key gating mechanisms and nontrivial switching subspaces. Our results provide a mechanistic and spectral understanding of how attention routing can alter local representation dynamics.
  </details>

- **[Response-Informed Value Estimation for Cost-Aware Multi-Turn LLM Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23239)**  `arXiv:2610.23239`  
  _Haotian Yan, Jiaqi Liu, Bingyi Jing, Tianjun Yao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-turn LLM routing selects a model at every turn before its response is available, yet task success is revealed only when the interaction ends, leaving each intermediate choice without a label of its own. Logged trajectories, however, retain the response each call produced, which exposes what each turn required and how well the chosen model served it, evidence that outcomes alone hide. We introduce RIVET (Response-Informed Value Estimation for Turn-Level Routing), which exploits this post-call evidence during training while keeping routing strictly pre-call at deployment. RIVET jointly trains two value estimators over a shared state-model representation: a deployable Routing Value Estimator that scores candidate models from pre-call information, and a training-only Response-Informed Value Estimator that additionally observes the realized response. Conditioning on the response turns response-induced return variation from residual noise into an observed covariate, and we show that this reduces the leading asymptotic estimation error of the shared representation. On the MINT benchmark, RIVET solves more held-out tasks than the strongest multi-turn baseline while using 74.7% less total call cost.
  </details>

- **[Process Reward Modeling with Reliability- and Conservatism-Aware Bayesian Belief](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23223)**  `arXiv:2610.23223`  
  _Zhongjian Qiao, Zhixiang Zhou, Jiafei Lyu, Peisong Wang, Qiaosheng Zhang, Siyang Gao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Process Reward Models (PRMs) play a central role in test-time scaling for large language model (LLM) reasoning. However, PRMs may provide inaccurate process rewards for test-time scaling, which we identify as arising from two sources of uncertainty: (1) \textit{PRM uncertainty} that arises from insufficient supervision data and imperfect PRM training; and (2) \textit{policy uncertainty} that results from the mismatch between the policy to collect supervision data and the policy for test-time scaling, leading to systematic reward overestimation. Previous studies fail to jointly consider both uncertainties. To bridge this gap, we propose BayesianPRM, which treats the process reward as a random variable within a reward hypothesis space and maintains a Bayesian belief over it. Since exact Bayesian inference over the continuous hypothesis space is generally intractable, we discretize the reward hypothesis space with a reward ensemble and introduce two complementary belief updates, each targeting one source of uncertainty: (1) to address PRM uncertainty, we introduce \textit{reliability-aware belief updating} that measures how well each ensemble member explains the supervision data and updates the belief with an amortized belief network optimized by the evidence lower bound (ELBO); and (2) to tackle policy uncertainty, we propose \textit{conservatism-aware belief calibration} which further reweights the belief toward lower rewards to suppress overestimation. These two updates yield a reliability- and conservatism-aware Bayesian belief, whose expectation determines the process reward. Experiments on multimodal reasoning benchmarks indicate that BayesianPRM consistently yields more accurate process reward estimates while enhancing test-time scaling performance.
  </details>

- **[Non-Parametric Optimization for Scalable Learning in Stochastic Decision Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23204)**  `arXiv:2610.23204`  
  _Mohsen Amidzadeh, Lauri Viitasaari, Mario Di Francesco_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Stochastic optimization (SO) plays a central role in decision-making under uncertainty. Among SO problems, time-varying stochastic optimization (TV-SO) is particularly important due to its applications in adaptive control and machine learning. Non-parametric approaches have been proposed for time-varying deterministic optimization; however, they have not been developed for their stochastic counterparts. This work addresses that gap by developing a stochastic variational framework based on Malliavin calculus. This framework yields non-parametric optimality conditions for SO problems with stochastic decisions and supports the design of a scalable deep-learning algorithm that is insensitive to the parameterization dimension. This algorithm, called the Stochastic Path Follower (SPF), is applied to two important problems under distribution drift, namely least-squares recovery and logistic regression. Experimental results show that the proposed approach outperforms state-of-the-art learning-based and gradient-based methods in both performance and scalability.
  </details>

- **[LangNCO: Compiling Natural-Language Constraints into Inference-Time Interventions for Neural Combinatorial Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23190)**  `arXiv:2610.23190`  
  _Hironori Ohigashi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural combinatorial optimization (NCO) rapidly produces high-quality solutions for problem structures encountered during training, but operational requirements may change after deployment and be communicated in natural language. We propose LangNCO, a framework that compiles instance-specific requirements into structured inference-time interventions for a frozen NCO solver, without changing the pretrained policy's architecture, learned input representation, or parameters. A pretrained LLM serves as a replaceable semantic front end, translating each requirement into a schema-validated domain-specific language (DSL) rule set. A deterministic Rule Executor applies the rules as nonexpansive hard action masks. Because local masking prevents immediate violations but does not ensure that a complete feasible continuation remains reachable, Critical-Offset Decoding extracts rule-referenced critical nodes and reallocates part of a fixed rollout budget to multi-strength biased rollouts. Using controlled task-specific language to isolate solver adaptation from open-ended language robustness, we evaluate a CVRP policy on road closures, time windows, vehicle assignments, visit-order requirements, all pairwise combinations, and twelve additional constraint types. LangNCO achieves 100% feasibility on the four primary datasets with mean reference gaps of 1.63--3.26%, 97.33--100% feasibility when separately generated rules are composed for pairwise constraints, and 98.67% feasibility across the additional constraints. These results demonstrate modular post-training adaptation of a frozen NCO solver through inspectable language-derived rules, without retraining.
  </details>

- **[A Stronger Teacher Within: Strengthening MoE Models via On-Policy Bidirectional Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23185)**  `arXiv:2610.23185`  
  _Haoyu Chen, Jun Bai, Yang Liu, Jianfei Zhang, Wenge Rong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has substantially improved Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs, but its outcome-level rewards provide only sparse supervision. On-Policy Distillation offers dense token-level feedback, yet requires a stronger compatible teacher, while on-policy self-distillation relies on hand-designed privileged information. We observe that the MoE architecture itself offers a way to resolve this dilemma, as increasing the activation budget per token exposes latent expert capacity and yields a potentially stronger teacher within the model. However, naive expansion degrades performance because newly co-activated experts are not optimized to work together. We therefore propose MoEvolve, a bidirectional distillation framework following a recovery-then-improvement strategy: we first restore the activation-expanded high-$k$ model and further strengthen it by exploiting its additional active capacity, then reverse the direction of distillation to transfer the enhanced capabilities back into the original low-$k$ configuration, retaining its inference-time activation budget. Across diverse MoE models and benchmarks, MoEvolve improves the initial models by up to $22.2$ domain-average points and outperforms same-budget RLVR baselines by up to $6.5$ points. Notably, the final low-$k$ models often surpass their activation-expanded teachers.
  </details>

- **[Nearly Linear Private Learning of Littlestone Classes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23160)**  `arXiv:2610.23160`  
  _Shay Moran, Chao Yan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Differentially private learning aims to learn from data while protecting individual training examples. For binary classification, finite Littlestone dimension characterizes approximate private PAC learnability (Alon et al., JACM 2022). Understanding how this dimension controls private sample complexity remains a central question. For classes of Littlestone dimension $d$, the first general upper bound was doubly exponential in $d$ (Bun, Livni, and Moran, FOCS 2020). Subsequent work improved this to $\widetilde O(d^6)$ (Ghazi et al., STOC 2021) and then to $\widetilde O(d^5)$ (Lyu, STOC 2026). We prove a nearly linear upper bound of $O(d\log d)$.Our main algorithmic contribution is a structural exponential mechanism for data-dependent candidate sets equipped with a rank satisfying suitable stability conditions. For natural Littlestone classes such as subspace indicators, this rank has a simple geometric interpretation: it is the codimension. This yields a private PAC learner for subspaces of dimension at most $d$ over any field, with tight linear dependence on $d$ and no dependence on the ambient dimension, improving the quadratic dependence of Kaplan et al. (STOC 2025). Our second contribution is combinatorial: drawing on model-theoretic ideas, we construct a suitable rank for general Littlestone classes and establish the counting bounds required by the mechanism.
  </details>

- **[Grounded Credit Assignment in Reinforcement Learning for Multi-Turn Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23148)**  `arXiv:2610.23148`  
  _Qirui Liu, Hao Chen, Yi Wen, Weijie Shi, Yao Zhao, Xianhe Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Outcome-reward reinforcement learning such as GRPO gives every token of a search-agent rollout the same advantage, although the rollout exercises two capabilities: finding evidence and using it. We show that accuracy improves without grounding: Search-R1 raises accuracy on all seven QA benchmarks, yet on Bamboogle the evidence-supported share of correct answers falls from 84.0% to 42.9%. We attribute this to GRPO's credit assignment: rollouts that retrieve answer-bearing evidence are favoured in aggregate only in proportion to their accuracy gain over the rest, a grounding gap that answering from memory or misusing evidence shrinks or reverses. To address this, we propose Grounded Credit Assignment (GCA), which uses verifiable sub-outcomes (evidence retrieval and answer correctness) to correct search and answer credit separately: a wrong answer is blamed more than the search that found the evidence, and a correct answer that bypasses retrievable evidence is credited less. Never negative on evidence-finding search, the correction does not shrink with the grounding gap; capped at 10% of each token's advantage, it never overrides the outcome signal. Without extra models, rollouts, or training stages, GCA improves average EM over Search-R1 by 5.7 (Qwen2.5-3B) and 6.3 points (Qwen3-30B-A3B) and raises this share from 76.9% to 93.4%.
  </details>

- **[Local proxies for memory-efficient training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23145)**  `arXiv:2610.23145`  
  _Wu S. Zihan, Brian A. Yuan, Guillaume Bellec_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Local learning algorithms can reshape resource requirements of artificial intelligence. The memory bottleneck of standard end-to-end backpropagation lies in the need to store all network activations until the loss gradient has been propagated back through. Local learning avoids this, but typically at a cost in accuracy. We study how to achieve accurate and memory efficient local learning in transformers. For each transformer block, we train a local proxy network to predict the global network output via distillation. Gradients can be independently propagated through this local proxy while estimating well the backpropagation gradients. In theory, this approach is exact for deep linear networks; in non-linear residual models, the gradient error can be bounded when using the appropriate residual proxy. We verify this numerically and demonstrate that optimizing the distillation loss improves gradient estimation. Evaluating the performance of proxy propagation on CIFAR-100 in vision transformers, we find that it achieves a better accuracy/memory trade-off compared to other local algorithms. Finally, we show that the method is further relevant for fine-tuning large language models on a small memory budget. Our method trains faster and achieves higher performance than memory-efficient zeroth-order learning algorithms with comparable memory cost.
  </details>

- **[MetaCurate: Evolving Curation Filters for High-Quality Agentic Coding Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23134)**  `arXiv:2610.23134`  
  _Donghoon Kim, Seunghyuk Oh, Kevin Galim, Minseo Kim, Minjae Lee, Younghyun Kim, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Supervised fine-tuning on agent trajectories is an effective way to improve coding agents, but selecting useful training examples requires more than identifying successful runs. A resolved patch may follow inefficient reasoning or unnecessary tool use, while a failed run may still contain useful intermediate steps. Training pipelines therefore rely on rules and qualitative assessments to filter trajectories, yet designing these filters typically requires substantial human expertise, and validating improvements through repeated training and evaluation is expensive. We introduce MetaCurate, a training-free framework that evolves executable trajectory curation filters using pairwise LLM judgments. Disagreements between the filter's rankings and the judge's preferences provide targeted feedback for jointly revising its rules, rubric, and aggregation policy. The resulting filter scores trajectories independently, so pairwise comparisons are required only during search. Starting from a blank filter, MetaCurate autonomously discovers a curation strategy that achieves competitive downstream performance without task-specific handwritten criteria. We validate downstream utility through controlled SFT experiments on our uncurated trajectory pool and the Nemotron v3 SWE dataset, with random selection and hand-authored filtering as baselines. In our experiments, MetaCurate achieves the highest mean SWE-Verified score among the compared methods, including hand-authored rule-based filtering and a training-loop baseline whose search costs 3.5x as much.
  </details>

- **[Domain-specific language alignment of multimodal embeddings towards explainable predictions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23110)**  `arXiv:2610.23110`  
  _Gabrielė Tijūnaitytė, Thijs L. van der Plas, Mohamed-Bachir Belaid, Rob Knapen, Maciej J. Soja, Ioannis N. Athanasiadis_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal data are commonly fused into latent representations that improve downstream task performance but are not interpretable, limiting their application in domains that require explainable model decisions. Vision-language alignment has made image embeddings interpretable through language, but requires vast image-caption training data. However, captioned data are typically not available for multimodal embeddings, as relevant features cannot directly be observed and annotated from embeddings alone. Here, we propose a framework to language-align any embedding space that can be linked to interpretable auxiliary data. Captioning functions convert auxiliary data into partial captions; a soft contrastive loss weights pairs by the similarity of their properties that each caption describes; and a rank-based concept retrieval task evaluates alignment against the auxiliary data rather than the captions. We apply this framework to multimodal Earth embeddings to facilitate explainable predictions for two species distribution modelling tasks: S2BMS and SatBird. AlphaEarth embeddings beat previous state-of-the-art models on 6/7 metrics, while our framework language-aligns domain-specific concepts of the embedding space, outperforming previous Earth observation vision-language models on average concept retrieval for both datasets. Finally, aligned concepts recover species-habitat associations ($\rho$=0.44) and relate habitat descriptions to species predictions, showing that abstract embeddings can be interrogated in the language of domain experts.
  </details>

- **[Towards a GraphNARX model for structural dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23103)**  `arXiv:2610.23103`  
  _George Tsialiamanis, Keith Worden_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The current paper introduces the GraphNARX model, a graph-based nonlinear autoregressive exogenous model for structural dynamics. The model is built to account for the geometry of structures and to model dynamic behaviour as the interaction between elements of the structure. Similar to other types of NARX models, GraphNARX is used for system identification. Nodal attributes of the graph encode past displacements and nodal forces, and the output of the model is the next-timestep displacement. The graph formulation of the model allows it to generalise to structures of different dimensions and degrees of freedom. Moreover, because graph-learning is performed locally between the nodes, the GraphNARX model can also learn to infer displacements of nodes for which data are not available, i.e.\ to perform virtual sensing. The proposed model's performance is evaluated in a system-identification framework and a virtual-sensing framework, and achieves good accuracy and exhibits generalisation capabilities to structures of different sizes.
  </details>

- **[Where Latents Belong and How They Flow: Joint Identification of Structural Roles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23074)**  `arXiv:2610.23074`  
  _Xintong Li, Kun Zhang, Yujia Zheng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Latent factors in dynamical systems can play different roles across observations and time. For example, an idea in a group meeting may be expressed by only some participants, inherit information from earlier discussions, and shape what is said next. We formalize this notion as the structural role of a latent factor, describing where it is expressed, whether it receives influence from the past, and whether it transmits influence to the future. The main challenge is correspondence, since the three structures may be identified under different permutations, while a structural role is defined only when they are aligned to the same latent coordinate. We show that, under suitable structural and variability conditions, all three roles are jointly identifiable under a common permutation at each time point. Guided by this theory, we develop RoleTrace, a framework that traces structural roles in a shared latent coordinate system. Synthetic experiments validate structural recovery, while experiments on multiparty meetings examine the identified roles in real sequential interactions.
  </details>

- **[Path to Prior-Data Fitted Relational Models via Learned Latent Multi-table Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23073)**  `arXiv:2610.23073`  
  _Mohammad Sadeq Abolhasani, Viswanath Ganapathy_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Prior-data fitted networks (PFNs) show that pretraining on large populations of synthetic tasks can enable generalization to unseen datasets with little or no downstream fine-tuning. This paradigm is effective for transformer-based tabular and relational foundation models such as RDB-PFN, but remains largely unexplored for graph-based relational models built on message passing. We pretrain the graph-based relational foundation model Griffin on large collections of synthetic relational databases and ask whether curriculum learning, which benefits transformer-based relational learners, also improves message-passing representations. Rather than fine-tune on downstream tasks, we use the pretrained encoder as a general-purpose relational representation learner and pass its latent representations to a frozen in-context predictor (\ticl{}); no component is trained on the target task. On 16 real-world relational classification tasks, evaluated over five seeds against raw-feature and untrained-encoder baselines, a purely synthetic curriculum that begins with schema-guided data and continues with schema-agnostic data gives the strongest frozen in-context performance\rev{, outperforming real-data-only pretraining and every unordered single-stage mixture of the corpora; when appended after real-data pretraining, it yields the best encoders overall}. These results provide initial evidence that PFN-style synthetic pretraining transfers to graph-based relational foundation models.
  </details>

- **[GR-OPD: Group-Relative Sequence Credit for On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23042)**  `arXiv:2610.23042`  
  _Yushu Wang, Renkai Yang, Jingcao Xu, Zili Geng, Haihong Tang, Yaohui Jin, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) provides token-level teacher feedback, yet common sampled-token updates assign credit locally and omit the effects of earlier actions on subsequent feedback. We introduce \method{} (Group-Relative On-Policy Distillation), which decouples feedback computation from credit assignment by aggregating token feedback into calibrated sequence-level credit. We establish a theoretical basis for this approach by showing that, under ideal on-policy assumptions, broadcasting raw summed feedback to every response token yields an unbiased estimate of the negative gradient of the sequence-level reverse-KL objective. To support effective learning in practice, \method{} discounts later feedback that may become less reliable under distribution shift and uses same-prompt rollout groups to calibrate teacher guidance. It preserves relative response preferences while retaining a controlled fraction of the group mean, which we term collective teacher pressure. Across five Qwen3 teacher--student settings and six mathematical reasoning benchmarks, \method{} outperforms vanilla OPD in macro Avg@K in every setting, including gains of 10.9 and 11.5 percentage points in two settings with matched thinking patterns. It also maintains stable training under mismatched thinking patterns, where vanilla OPD collapses. Ablations show that retaining an appropriate amount of collective teacher pressure can improve learning, while excessive collective teacher pressure or an unrestricted feedback horizon can destabilize training.
  </details>

- **[Towards Looped Models Done Right, Part I: Topology, Input Injection, Recurrent-State Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23036)**  `arXiv:2610.23036`  
  _Benhao Huang, Chufan Shi, Junlin Chen, Shicheng Wen, Zhengzhong Liu, Eric Xing, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped language models reuse physical Transformer blocks across logical depth, but established architectures entangle recurrence placement, input injection, and recurrent state organization. We isolate these axes in models trained from scratch at matched parameter scale, logical depth, and token budgets, and then test whether the resulting design principles transfer to resident- and active-parameter-matched Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models. The prelude-recurrent block-coda sandwich architecture consistently improves performance on context extraction and reasoning-intensive tasks. Input injection primarily enhances knowledge retrieval and context utilization, whereas random initialization and separate H/L recurrent states provide mixed or negative returns. Taken together, these results favor the Huginn-style design: at the 730M-parameter and 336B-token scale, the complete Huginn architecture outperforms Ouro across all ten benchmarks. The advantages of Huginn over Ouro transfer to MoE models. At the 8BA0.8B (32BA3B when unrolled) scale trained on 500B tokens, Huginn outperforms Ouro on nearly all benchmarks spanning knowledge, reasoning, and coding, while also exhibiting more balanced expert routing. Under matched training and inference FLOPs, the Huginn-style model uses 75% fewer resident parameters than a 112-layer feedforward MoE baseline. Despite its smaller physical parameters, it outperforms the feedforward baseline on DROP and GSM8K, matches it on MATH500, and remains competitive on the other benchmarks.
  </details>

- **["Deep lifts": Neural networks can generalize even with the dumbest optimizer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23009)**  `arXiv:2610.23009`  
  _Sunil Madhow, Yu-Xiang Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Given a labeled dataset $\{(x_i, y_i)\}_{i = 1}^n$, consider drawing a parameter vector $\hat{\theta}$ uniformly at random from those that interpolate the data within a width-$W$ depth-$D$ ReLU architecture with continuous weights bounded within $[-M, M]$. We study the generalization properties of $\hat{\theta}$ in the case where the dataset can, in fact, be explained by a smaller $w$-by-$d$ ReLU network. Despite the obliviousness of the selection rule for $\hat{\theta}$, it adaptively generalizes at the rate $\tilde O\left(\frac{wdWD}{n}\right)$, rather than being governed by the VC dimension of the full $W$-by-$D$ hypothesis class. The reason is due to a particular inductive bias in ReLU networks: the $w$-by-$d$ parameter *witnesses* an exponential volume of $W$-by-$D$ interpolating parameters, which dwarfs the volume of poorly generalizing parameters (conditioned on interpolation). We explicitly construct this set of near-equivalent $W$-by-$D$ parameters by a proof technique called *deep lifting*, and then tie the achievable volume to stability properties of the $w$-by-$d$ witness. Examples show that these techniques allow us to convert approximation-theoretic results into generalization guarantees for nonparametric classes. We further show that generalization of uniform-random interpolators can survive label corruptions.
  </details>

- **[A Minimal ReLU Classifier on Non-Linearly Separable Data: Convergence and Implicit Bias at and beyond the Edge of Stability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23007)**  `arXiv:2610.23007`  
  _Quan Nguyen, Robin Yadav, Param Mody, Mark Schmidt_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Gradient descent (GD) with large learning rates can successfully optimize neural networks even though classical smoothness-based learning rate conditions are violated. Yet theory for this regime largely covers linear models or linearly separable data. We analyze a minimal setting outside both regimes. Specifically, a two-neuron ReLU network trained with logistic loss on 1D non-linearly separable data. The objective is nonconvex and nonsmooth, and GD exhibits edge-of-stability (EoS) behavior. We identify an active cone in parameter space that contains every perfect classifier, where the problem becomes piecewise logistic regression with a varying effective dataset. Within this cone, we prove a finite-time loss bound for constant learning rates and show that GD converges in direction to an explicit max-margin vector, while the orthogonal component converges to the unique minimizer of a non-strongly convex potential. We further establish catapult results where exponentially small loss is reached after a single loss spike. Furthermore, we propose an adaptive schedule that lets learning rates grow to infinity without leaving the active cone. We prove that this schedule achieves a loss rate of $O(1/(T\log{T})$, which is faster than the standard $O(1/T)$ rate. Our theoretical results extend to local convergence on more general network and are validated on MNIST dataset. Overall, we show that this setup provides a tractable neural-network model for studying large and adaptive step sizes beyond linear predictors and separable data.
  </details>

- **[Large Diffusion Models Need More Than EMA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22945)**  `arXiv:2610.22945`  
  _Chuyu Hu, Huanran Chen, Shitong Shao, Yinpeng Dong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large-scale diffusion models are commonly trained with a constant learning rate (LR) and exponential moving average (EMA), despite the strong performance of LR decay and Schedule-Free optimization in training large language models. We study whether EMA is uniquely suited to diffusion training, or whether stronger alternatives have been underexplored. Our theory shows that averaging a constant-step trajectory has an inherent optimization limitation, whereas LR decay and Schedule-Free optimization admit vanishing stationarity guarantees under standard nonconvex assumptions. Empirically, LR decay and EMA provide complementary benefits: combining decay with EMA further improves the standard baseline at most tested starting points, although the gain remains sensitive to when decay begins. Schedule-Free avoids this timing requirement and consistently outperforms EMA with a constant LR across training stages, achieving both lower fixed-evaluation objectives and better generation quality. Our analysis further shows that all three strategies substantially reduce parameter variability, yet stronger stabilization is not always better: EMA with a constant LR retains comparatively higher parameter variability, while aggressive decay combined with EMA achieves the lowest checkpoint variability but also weakens continued optimization. Schedule-Free substantially reduces parameter variability while preserving more late-stage progress, providing a better balance between parameter stability and continued optimization. Finally, the benefits of Schedule-Free extend beyond pretraining to ReFL and generator-side DMD, demonstrating its transferability across distinct diffusion optimization settings. Overall, our results suggest that large diffusion models need more than parameter averaging alone: effective optimization requires balancing variance reduction with continued optimization.
  </details>

- **[A Feature Selection Layer Approach for Multi-Class Classification on Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22898)**  `arXiv:2610.22898`  
  _João Pedro Silveira e Silva, Márcio Dorn, Bruno Iochins Grisci_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In the age of data-driven decision-making, deep learning models have become powerful tools for uncovering patterns and making predictions that influence many aspects of society. However, their broader adoption in scientific and business applications is often limited by a lack of interpretability. Feature weighting methods, which assess the relevance of individual features, are among the most widely studied approaches for improving interpretability. While post hoc techniques have advanced significantly, especially in modeling nuanced relationships between features and labels in multiclass problems, embedded methods typically fall short in this regard. Embedded techniques integrate feature relevance learning directly into the model training process, but they are often unable to capture label-specific relationships. To address the limitation, we propose multiple extensions to the Feature Selection Layer, an embedded feature weighting mechanism for neural networks. Through extensive experimentation, we identify the most effective extension and benchmark it against established feature weighting methods. Our results show that the proposed method matches existing techniques in identifying globally relevant features and outperforms them in capturing label-specific relevance. By leveraging the internal dynamics of the trained neural network, it reveals how individual features contribute to the prediction of specific labels, requiring no post-processing beyond the training process.
  </details>

- **[What Accuracy Hides In Learned Transformer Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22891)**  `arXiv:2610.22891`  
  _Jonathan Lee, Narmeen Oozeer, Philip Quirke_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Transformer Programs (TP) (Friedman et al., 2023) constrain a Transformer's architecture so that its learned computation can be extracted as readable Python code. TP expose learned computations, but token accuracy can hide incorrect complete answers. In this work, we examine how program inspection provides insights beyond accuracy scores and helps guide training. We first evaluated TP on nine algorithmic tasks. We compared token accuracy with accuracy on complete output sequences and checked for failures missed by sampled tests. Further, we assessed training data improvements informed by program inspection and failure analysis, finding gains in sequence accuracy on several tasks. We examined a counting task where a program achieved 100% accuracy on sampled test sequences but failed on another input using the symbols and lengths allowed during training. The program counted correctly, but the required answer was missing from its output labels. After adding training examples with larger counts, the retrained models passed exhaustive evaluation over the bounded domain. In the frequency ranking task, inspection showed how correct counts contributed to incorrect answers in programs trained before and after changes to the training data. Finally, we studied constraints imposed by TP training and operations. The TP training process can lead to accuracy losses late in training. Our reference constructions also show how composing the restricted TP operations can use many layers or large lookup tables.
  </details>

- **[Learning to Be Guided: Shaping Latent Geometry for Classifier-Free Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22885)**  `arXiv:2610.22885`  
  _Haoming Liu, Junhao Zhu, Shaoyi Zheng, Yuanhe Guo, Jinnuo Liu, Shenji Wan, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Classifier-free guidance (CFG) is a standard component of conditional diffusion models, and guided generation performance has become the \textit{de facto} measure of state-of-the-art models. Recent work on latent diffusion models seeks to learn latent spaces that are easier to model with diffusion. However, under CFG, generation additionally depends on how conditions are arranged in the latent space. We study this arrangement under discrete class conditions, termed \emph{class geometry}, and investigate what class geometry allows a latent space to work well with CFG. Under a Gaussian class model, we show that shifting variance from intra- to inter-class at fixed total variance makes classes more distinguishable under noise, and derive a noisy posterior objective whose per-sample descent direction is proportional to the oracle CFG correction. Based on the analysis, we propose Prototype Regularization (PR), a lightweight surrogate for the intractable noisy posterior objective, which applies a cosine prototype classifier to spatially pooled latents. With a controlled training budget (20 epochs for VAE, 80 epochs for DiT), adding PR to an LDM baseline reduces guided gFID on ImageNet from 3.31 to 2.28, surpassing a DINOv3-aligned VA-VAE (2.44) with negligible overhead. We further demonstrate that PR generalizes to pseudo-labels derived from clustering, continuous text-embedding conditions, and video latents. Together, our findings highlight class geometry as a critical factor in guided generation and take a first step toward CFG-compatible representation learning. Our project page is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking and Enhancing Agentic Causal Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22846)**  `arXiv:2610.22846`  
  _Pingchuan Ma, Rui Ding, Shuai Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents can now run causal discovery algorithms on a dataset and report what comes out. Whether they do more than that, such as noticing where an algorithm went wrong and fixing it with further tests, is hard to tell from current benchmarks. Most of them give the variables meaningful names, and an LLM shown only the names of the Tübingen cause-effect pairs already gets 99% of the directions right. With the names removed, the data are usually clean enough that picking a better algorithm than PC closes most of the gap. We introduce Heuresis, a benchmark of anonymous simulated datasets whose mechanisms range from linear Gaussian to heavy-tailed. For each family of datasets it separates what a better existing algorithm can gain from what only a closer look at the data can gain, and it traces every edge an agent changes. Every family leaves a gap that none of the algorithms, the router and the two causal-discovery foundation models we ran can close, so a score above the best chosen algorithm has to come from using the data. We instantiate the benchmark with an agent harness and three LLMs, GPT-6.1 Sol, Claude Opus 5.5 and Qwen 3.8 Flash. GPT-6.1 Sol surpasses the best chosen algorithm on all six families, by 14% adjacency F1 on the most difficult one, and Claude Opus 5.5 does so by 7.4% there. A third of the true edges the two agents restore on the two hardest families appear in no algorithm's output. The code and data will be publicly released to facilitate future work.
  </details>

- **[Partial-Revelation Panel Forecasting: Predictive Information Gain, Allocation, and Coarsening-Consistent Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22831)**  `arXiv:2610.22831`  
  _Bin Wen, Dai-Qiao Zhang, Tien-Ping Tan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study partial-revelation panel forecasting: given $k$ of $n$ annotator votes on the previous dialogue turn, predict the full count distribution of the next turn's panel. This cross-turn predictive information differs from same-item annotation value. Under any nested revelation mechanism, the population decrement satisfies $V(k)-V(k+1)=I(Y;R_{k+1}\mid X_k)$; a fitted model also contributes adjacent excess-risk differences. Coarsened-completely-at-random (CCAR) masking supplies the exchangeable random-rater interpretation used in our operators. We define coarsening-consistent panel operators with a closed-form Dirichlet-multinomial realization, learned completion, and rater-count transfer. Against per-level lookup, cold-start count negative log-likelihood differences are -0.422/-0.166/-0.014 nats on IEMOCAP/M3ED/EmoWOZ. A held-out rater experiment measures same-item value: first and second additional votes reduce NLL by 0.583/0.221, 0.578/0.184, and 0.180/0.069 nats, respectively. Cross-turn and same-item gains are not interchangeable, and CCAR masks are controlled interventions rather than observed arrival processes. Consistency is auditable but does not guarantee predictive improvement; per-level marginalization can outperform a shared table. Target-free allocation beats uniform only where row-level signal is predictable: at budget one it recovers 83--93% of retrospective feature class headroom on IEMOCAP/M3ED but does not improve EmoWOZ. Gains are modest and corpus-dependent, with several small or null partial-revelation differences. Code and aggregate result artifacts accompany the preprint.
  </details>

- **[Ori: Drift-Aware Streaming $k$-PCA for Very Low-Rank LLM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22794)**  `arXiv:2610.22794`  
  _Xinyu Luo, Jincheng Zhou, Mengbo Wang, Zilin Shen, Nadia Atallah Lanman, Petros Drineas, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A prominent family of low-rank LLM optimizers, pioneered by GaLore, performs gradient-subspace projection: at each step, they project the gradient into a low-dimensional subspace and map the update back to the full model. Most existing work either recomputes the gradient subspace at certain intervals, or performs post-hoc adaptation to catch up to the moving subspace. In this work, we explicitly identify in practice the slow, stable manner in which LLM gradient subspaces drift across time. To this end, we propose Ori, a novel low-rank optimizer for training LLMs that captures the shifting principal gradient directions over time to achieve state-of-the-art accuracy under stringent low-rank constraints. Ori employs a streaming $k$-PCA algorithm inspired by Oja++, whose low per-step cost enables high-frequency, on-the-fly subspace tracking.
