In practice, Ori reduces the performance gap of the state-of-the-art low-rank optimizers to full-rank optimizers by up to 69.3\% on a 1B LLaMA pretraining task, and achieves the best performance on 10 out of 16 task scenarios in Roberta-Base fine-tuning. Together, these contributions allow Ori to further push the performance and efficiency frontiers of gradient-projecting optimizers, narrowing the gap to full-rank training for LLMs. Code can be found atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Acquisition Function-Guided Generation for Bayesian Optimization over Discrete Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22763)**  `arXiv:2610.22763`  
  _Tommaso Ancilli, Thomas Wedenig, Henry Moss, Robert Peharz_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Bayesian optimization (BO) over discrete spaces is challenging due to missing gradients and the sparsity of valid solutions. Latent-space BO sidesteps both issues by fitting a surrogate in the latent space of a generative model, which however substantially complicates the direct incorporation of domain knowledge that is naturally expressed in the original discrete space. In this work, we instead combine a pretrained generative model with a surrogate defined directly in data space, where inductive biases such as domain-specific kernels can be readily incorporated. Rather than maximizing the acquisition function over the discrete design space, our method samples candidates from the generative model under a distribution tilted by the acquisition function. To recover useful gradients for sampling, we embed this tilted distribution into a diffusion bridge that yields a smooth, posterior-averaged acquisition function. This leads to \emph{acquisition function guided generation} (AFGG), which we instantiate with a categorical diffusion model and an autoregressive normalizing flow. Across a broad suite of molecular benchmarks, the diffusion model variant performs on par with leading BO methods, while the flow model variant outperforms state-of-the-art methods in most cases and remains highly competitive otherwise. Our implementation is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Spectral Limits of Polynomial Graph Filters on the Long-Range Ising Model Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22713)**  `arXiv:2610.22713`  
  _Amin Najafgholizadeh, Mohsen Afsharchi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The Long-Range Ising Model benchmark (LRIM) guarantees long-range dependence by construction: each label is the node's spin times the spin field convolved with a power-law coupling, so any model's distance from the target is measurable. We prove that a readout whose filter is a polynomial in the adjacency, the linear core of isotropic message passing and spectral graph networks, leaves an error floor that no degree lowers, because the target varies along a level set of the spectrum on which every such filter is constant. Computed exactly, the floor and the best filter of each degree predict two spectral networks adapted from published architectures to within a tenth of a decade. A filter escapes the floor only if it weighs separately the offsets the adjacency lumps together, and must still resolve the coupling across the whole lattice. A strided filter of about fifty coefficients does both: fitted to the training labels, its test error is 52 times below the best published network's. A dilated convolutional network beats the best published result at 256 nodes, matches it at 1,024, and gives the first in-domain results at the three larger sizes, while a Fourier neural operator with its standard modes falls further behind as the lattice grows. At its standard budget the dilated network stays two to four orders of magnitude short of a larger strided filter fitted to the labels. The analysis carries over to the benchmark's second coupling.
  </details>

- **[Normalizing Flow Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22711)**  `arXiv:2610.22711`  
  _Francesco M. Ruscio, Timothy James Hitge, Fred Zhangzhi Peng, Danyal Rehman, Zander W. Blasingame, Nicholas M. Boffi, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scalable approaches to learning neural dynamical systems have placed diffusion, flow matching, and few-step generators in flow maps at the forefront of generative modeling. Such state-of-the-art models have limited access to explicit model densities despite evolving from classical generative modeling approaches that leveraged tractable likelihoods, e.g., Normalizing Flows and Autoregressive models. In this paper, we combine these threads of few-step generation and tractable sample likelihoods and introduce Normalizing Flow Maps (NFM), a class of exactly invertible pushforward maps that generalizes normalizing flows and flow maps. More precisely, NFMs learn the integrator of an underlying dynamical system, but crucially, they provide efficient exact likelihood evaluation by construction. To enable scalable training of NFMs, we derive two complementary axes of consistency inherent to NFMs that yield (self) distillation objectives: (1) pointwise matching, which unlocks $\ell_2$-based flow-map style regression losses, and (2) distributional matching, which matches pushforward distributions to the marginals of a prescribed forward process. On conditional image generation on CIFAR-10 and ImageNet-64, NFMs achieve state-of-the-art sample quality among parameter-matched normalizing flows, while supporting likelihood-based RLHF. For equilibrium sampling in Boltzmann Generators, NFMs outperform prior state-of-the-art normalizing flows on both single-system and transferable peptide benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[AlphaBet: Reinforcement Learning for LLM Event Forecasting Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22684)**  `arXiv:2610.22684`  
  _Liqin Ye, Rongzhi Zhang, Xin Liu, Zheng Li, Yuan He, Qingyu Yin, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Forecasting an unresolved event requires finding relevant evidence and deciding how strongly it supports each outcome. Yet outcome-based training of Large Language Model (LLM) forecasters often begins with preassembled evidence at a single prediction date, leaving evidence acquisition outside the learning loop. We introduce AlphaBet, an agentic reinforcement learning (RL) framework that trains a single LLM policy to search, reason, and report probabilities across multiple pre-resolution dates. Each dated forecast is scored by a proper scoring rule against the eventual outcome, allowing one resolved event to supervise decisions under different information states. We decompose forecasting regret into an evidence-acquisition gap and a probability-reporting gap, and show that Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO)'s standard-deviation normalization can distort the reporting target under multi-class proper scores. On GLM-4.5-Air, AlphaBet reduces the Brier score from 0.731 to 0.626 on Forecast-Dojo and from 0.612 to 0.528 on FutureX. Controlled ablations attribute these gains to multi-date training and show that they come from improvements in both information acquisition and probability reporting. Our empirical results validate that GRPO's std normalization degrades proper-scoring-rule performance while leaving top-outcome accuracy largely unchanged.
  </details>

- **[Edit Discovery in Human-AI Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22661)**  `arXiv:2610.22661`  
  _Alireza Mirrokni, Shayan Kiyani, George Pappas, Hamed Hassani_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human-AI interactions are becoming a rich source of data for improving AI systems: while many model responses are already satisfactory, others expose failures that could become valuable supervision if identified and corrected. We study this setting through the notion of an editor: a more capable evaluation process that can inspect a response and either confirm it or modify it, but whose invocation is costly. The editor may be a human expert, a stronger AI system, or the same model equipped with additional computation, context, tools, or information. The key challenge is to decide which interactions to send to the editor before knowing whether an edit will actually be made, since invoking the editor itself consumes limited resources such as expert time, inference cost, latency, or tool-use overhead. We formulate this problem as Edit Discovery Optimization (EDO), which seeks to minimize edit opportunities missed among skipped interactions while controlling unnecessary confirmations and respecting an editor-cost budget. We characterize the population-optimal policy and show that it takes the form of a cost-adjusted threshold on the posterior probability of an edit. Building on this structure, we develop EDIT (Edit Discovery via Iterative Thresholding), an online algorithm for the partial-feedback setting in which edit labels are revealed only for selected interactions. EDIT combines an adaptive edit score, a confirmation-control threshold, and a cost-aware selection rule; it satisfies the editor budget at every round and controls the average confirmation rate up to a vanishing finite-horizon term, without distributional assumptions on the interaction stream. Across four reasoning and factual question-answering benchmarks, EDIT achieves a better trade-off between editor cost, Type-I error, and Type-II error than direct LLM-based batch selection.
  </details>

- **[The Ephemeral Cost of Switching Optimizers Across LLM Training Stages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22650)**  `arXiv:2610.22650`  
  _Xinyu Zhao, Davis Wertheimer, Ashutosh Sharma, Rameswar Panda, Paul Pu Liang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work suggests that switching optimizers between pretraining and subsequent training stages incurs a performance cost, but systematic investigation of this phenomenon is lacking. We study the mismatch between AdamW and Muon optimizers by pretraining 1.2B Llama-3 models from scratch while varying the proportion of updates taken with each optimizer, and similarly interpolating between the two optimizers during continued pretraining and supervised fine-tuning (SFT). Surprisingly, we find little evidence of a meaningful optimizer mismatch penalty: final performance is largely explained by the independent effects of the pretraining and post-training optimizers, with downstream performance generally improving as the proportion of Muon updates during SFT increases. Although matching the pretraining optimizer can reduce forgetting during SFT, this does not translate into improved final benchmark performance. We further evaluate AdamW and Muon for task-general SFT of open-weight models, across the Llama and Qwen families up to 14B parameters, where Muon remains competitive with AdamW, despite these models having been pretrained with AdamW. Together, our results suggest that the choice of optimizer for post-training can be made largely independently of the optimizer used during pretraining.
  </details>

- **[Network-Wide Freeway Speed Nowcasting and Forecasting from Sparse Fixed Sensors with a Probe-Supervised Graph Convolutional Recurrent Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22630)**  `arXiv:2610.22630`  
  _Ran Tu, Zihao Li, Arash Mirzaei_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Traffic agencies need current and near-future speeds on every link of their network, but their fixed sensors cover only part of it. We study joint nowcasting and next-hour forecasting of hourly speeds on all 4{,}127 directional freeway links of the Dallas--Fort Worth region from sensors on 643 links (15.6%). Historical probe-vehicle data supervise every link in training; only sensor readings are used at inference. We call this setting probe-supervised sparse sensing. We propose GCRN+, a graph convolutional recurrent network that predicts deviations from a historical speed profile. It combines a parameter-free, direction-aware kernel interpolation of sensor anomalies, used as input features and as an output baseline, with a diffusion-convolutional GRU on the directed road graph, a low-rank adaptive graph and learnable link embeddings. It scales linearly with network size and trains on a CPU. Sensor readings are emulated from probe speeds, which isolates spatio-temporal inference from detector calibration. On four held-out test days, GCRN+ attains an RMSE of 2.21~mph for the current hour and 2.39~mph for the next hour on unsensored links, against 3.23~mph for the historical average. The best baselines that use the same interpolation features reach 2.53 and 2.75~mph. Adapted recent models without these features (STID, STAEformer, ImputeFormer, MoGERNN) reach at best 2.54 and 2.53~mph; this comparison is between complete pipelines rather than architectures. GCRN+ is best in every distance band except links not connected to any sensor through the road graph. Ablations within one recurrent backbone attribute most of the gain to the link embeddings and the adaptive graph, which can be trained because probe labels exist for every link. For congestion detection on unsensored links, recall rises from 77\% (historical average) and 86% (best competitor) to 89% at aprecision of 0.94.
  </details>

- **[Understanding Stability of Outer Nesterov Momentum on Stochastic Pseudo-Gradients](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22623)**  `arXiv:2610.22623`  
  _Nishant Sharma, Laura Gomezjurado Gonzalez, Ganesh Talluri, Sarath Chandar, Hiroki Naganuma_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large neural networks are commonly trained across many workers, and communication between them is a major cost. Workers therefore take several local optimization steps before sending their net parameter displacements, or pseudo-gradients, to an outer optimizer, which often applies Nesterov momentum. We study how the outer momentum, the number of local steps and the averaging over workers control the training dynamics, and in particular their stability. For random quadratics, we derive an exact mean-square stability criterion in any dimension, which reveals that the inner batch size determines whether increasing the number of local steps eventually stabilizes or destabilizes the outer iteration. Moreover, while averaging the pseudo-gradients of independent workers can make larger outer momentum values admissible, the number of workers required for stability can grow exponentially with the number of local steps. Finally, we show that the mean-square dynamics governing stability can be probed directly at intermediate neural-network checkpoints using shared-minibatch perturbations. We test our theoretical findings across networks, and measure their consequences for training loss.
  </details>

- **[Noise-shaped Sampling and Annealing for Scalable On-the-edge Training of Binary and Ternary AI Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22621)**  `arXiv:2610.22621`  
  _Zihao Chen, Zhili Xiao, Rui-Jie Zhu, Yipeng Wang, Jason Eshraghian, Shantanu Chakrabartty_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The training loss of binary and ternary neural networks, expanded as a multilinear polynomial over the discrete weights, is structurally equivalent to the energy of a hierarchical high-order Ising model. We exploit this equivalence to apply Ising solvers to discrete-weight network training, while accounting for a fundamental difference in objectives: an Ising solver seeks the ground state of a fixed energy function, whereas training seeks solutions that generalize to unseen data. Our key contribution is $\Sigma\Delta$-Sampler, an optimizer that reconciles these objectives by embedding a stochastic sampling-and-annealing step as a noisy quantizer inside a first-order $\Sigma\Delta$ noise-shaping loop. The loop pushes noise from weight quantization, high-order frustration, and mini-batch sampling out of the low-frequency adaptation band, so that discrete weight updates follow the smooth descent trajectories embedded in the rugged loss landscape. We validate the framework on a planted quadratic unconstrained binary optimization (QUBO) benchmark with a known minimizer; on CIFAR-10, where a $13$M-parameter ternary model comes within $1\%$ of full-precision accuracy; on ImageNet-1k, where a ternary ResNet-18 comes within $0.4\%$ Top-5 accuracy of a ternary baseline; on class-incremental continual learning, where prior-task accuracy is preserved; and on MatMul-free language models up to $2.7$B parameters, where it outperforms a straight-through-estimator baseline under matched continued pre-training. Compared with standard quantization-aware training, $\Sigma\Delta$-Sampler reduces the training memory footprint by over $50\%$, delivers an order-of-magnitude speed-up, and reduces weight-update memory traffic by four orders of magnitude, making it attractive for on-device training and fine-tuning of large binary and ternary models.
  </details>

- **[Rarity of Singletons Matters & Supplementary Materials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22604)**  `arXiv:2610.22604`  
  _Pragatheeswaran Vipulanandan, Kamal Premaratne, Dilip Sarkar_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Estimating missing-mass and unseen-species counts from finite samples relies heavily on rare-event occupancy statistics, particularly singletons. In small-sample regimes, these statistics can be highly variable, making classical estimators sensitive to sampling fluctuations and potentially unreliable. We propose a fragility-aware (FA) framework that explicitly quantifies this sensitivity and adaptively regularizes conventional missing-mass and unseen-species estimators. Using singleton-based fragility measures derived from probability mass and occupancy counts, we formulate an entropy--Kullback--Leibler (KL) optimization that adjusts the reliance on base estimates according to their fragility. The resulting objectives are strictly concave, admit unique closed-form solutions, and require no Poissonization or asymptotic approximations. The framework provides a lightweight correction to classical estimators, including Good--Turing and Good--Toulmin, while reducing reliance on potentially unstable rare-event statistics. Across 360 experimental configurations spanning synthetic distributions and real-world language corpora, our method remains competitive in stable regimes while improving estimation accuracy and stability in fragmented, long-tailed, and small-sample settings. The findings demonstrate that accounting for the fragility of rare observations can improve the reliability of inference about unobserved species and their associated probability mass.
  </details>

- **[Diagnosing GNN Failure Modes with Layer-Wise Probe Entropy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22595)**  `arXiv:2610.22595`  
  _Shanzi Bao, Mar Gonzàlez I Català, Pietro Liò_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph neural networks (GNNs) are typically evaluated only at their final layer, obscuring how task-relevant information evolves during message passing. We introduce a post-hoc diagnostic that freezes a trained GNN, fits an independent linear probe to each layer, and tracks probe accuracy alongside normalized predictive entropy. Across real and synthetic graph settings, layer-wise probe entropy distinguishes beneficial, neutral, and harmful smoothing, a capability not demonstrated by existing geometric measures. Moreover, by comparing shallow and deep models, we show that the onset of entropy degradation separates the two main GNN failure modes: structural mismatch under heterophily, which appears from the earliest layers even in shallow models, and excessive-depth oversmoothing, which emerges only as propagation depth increases. These results establish probe-entropy trajectories as a tool for analyzing information flow in GNNs and motivate future work on adaptive depth selection and early-exit mechanisms.
  </details>

- **[Noise-Robust Forced Alignment for Spontaneous Speech](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22583)**  `arXiv:2610.22583`  
  _Laurin Wagner, Mario Zusag_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We show that forced aligners degrade on spontaneous, noisy real-world speech, and that fillers, laughter, and disfluencies corrupt word boundaries, impacting many downstream applications. We revisit the classical GMM-HMM aligner with upsampled WavLM features instead of MFCCs, explicit phone units for fillers and non-lexical vocal events trained on verbatim-annotated spontaneous speech, and a discriminative projection fitted on clean and noise-corrupted feature pairs to improve robustness. The pipeline is self-bootstrapped from 149h of curated audio without manual or external boundary targets. On conversational (Buckeye) and disfluent (FluencyBank) speech it reaches word-boundary MAEs of 20.6 and 76.9ms, 19% and 17% lower than the strongest of ten baselines. Catastrophic misalignments (>200ms) are 2.7 times rarer than Montreal Forced Aligners (MFA) best model even after widening MFA's search beam, which we show removes a large pruning artifact from its conversational error; event units halve the error next to laughter, while being meaningfully more robust than existing aligners.
  </details>

- **[Structure-Aware Spatial Kernels for Gaussian Process Distributed Port-Hamiltonian Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22575)**  `arXiv:2610.22575`  
  _Tianyi Yang, Thomas Beckers_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Gaussian process distributed port-Hamiltonian systems (GP-dPHS) enable data-driven modeling of distributed dynamical systems while preserving their physical structure. However, standard stationary kernels can be inefficient for spatially heterogeneous systems because they neither exploit known input invariances nor reflect structured spatial dependencies. We address this limitation with a structure-aware kernel design that removes irrelevant state coordinates and decouples state and spatial dependencies through a tensor-product covariance. This modular construction allows qualitative knowledge of spatial heterogeneity to be incorporated by selecting an appropriate spatial kernel without requiring an analytical description of the underlying profile. Numerical experiments on a nonlinear string with periodic, smoothly varying, and semi-piecewise stiffness profiles demonstrate that structural decoupling consistently improves reconstruction accuracy, while profile-matched spatial kernels yield substantially larger gains in force-field and trajectory reconstruction.
  </details>

- **[Coverage Cliffs in Off-Policy Quantile Certification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22565)**  `arXiv:2610.22565`  
  _Pauline Bourigault, Emmanuelle Bourigault_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study when logged contextual-bandit data can certify an upper tail quantile for a policy selected from the same sample. We construct a PAC-Bayes Bernstein lower-confidence envelope for the target cumulative distribution function (CDF), simultaneous over a predeclared threshold grid and all data-dependent posteriors. The resulting procedure, TailCert, uses the envelope's first crossing of level p to return an atom-safe upper confidence bound on the selected posterior's pooled p-quantile (or inconclusive). It can also be used to select a policy under a certified tail constraint. The concentration ingredients are standard; the main theoretical contribution is a matched finite-class certification boundary comparing full logs with their joint weighted CDF indicators at a fixed threshold. On an explicit rare-action class, the required sample size grows linearly with the overlap factor W, logarithmically with policy-search and confidence complexity, and quadratically with the inverse CDF safety margin; the upper and lower bounds agree up to constants in the stated regime. On this class, the CDF reduction hides which actions exceeded the budget; retaining action counts restores the full-log rate. Simulations verify the predicted search-complexity shift, winner's-curse failure of naive reuse, and the power cost of splitting.
  </details>

- **[On the SPOT: Enhancing Speculative Decoding for Diffusion Language Models via Commit-Order Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22543)**  `arXiv:2610.22543`  
  _Eliezer Shlomi, Eliron Rahimi, Avi Mendelson, Chaim Baskin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion language models (DLMs) promise parallel text generation, but in practice face an unresolved speed-quality trade-off. Exact decoding fills in only one masked position per model pass, while faster multi-token decoding schemes are lossy. Speculative decoding is the standard lossless acceleration for autoregressive models. However, it does not transfer directly to DLMs, which must choose both what to generate and where. We show that this order is the missing piece: the model's own predictions from the previous pass already give the right token at 92-100% of positions, but not which position comes next. Based on this observation, we introduce Speculating Positions Order Trajectory (SPOT), which learns to predict this order. A 0.62M-parameter model reads only the target's output distributions and proposes a tree of candidate continuations, and the target verifies all of them in a single pass by checking whether each equals its own next state. On the verification side, our analysis shows that prior block-diffusion verification approaches either assume $T=0$ or a sequential proposal interface. To support tree-based speculation at any temperature, we propose using coupled-noise match verification for diffusion blocks, following shared-Gumbel-noise coupling approaches. Our comprehensive experiments show that SPOT cuts target passes by up to 6.8x and reaches up to 3.2x the throughput of standard decoding, beating the calibrated and training-free drafting baselines in every task and budget at $T=0$ and $T=1$. In addition, we show that the learned drafter transfers across models, demonstrating successful transfer across six different DLMs. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Don't Retrain, Remix: Adaptation of Web Agents using Mixture Hypernetworks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22539)**  `arXiv:2610.22539`  
  _Md Farhan Ishmam, Minh Pham Dinh, Kenneth Marino_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The web is not static or uniform. Websites that serve the same purpose can look and navigate very differently. Even within a single site, successive redesigns can change how familiar features are presented and accessed. Humans adapt to this by recognizing familiar interaction patterns and reusing what they already know. We give web agents a similar ability with WebMix, a mixture hypernetwork that adapts them to unseen interfaces by composing previously trained low-rank adapters. WebMix begins with a bank of adapters trained on source interfaces. At deployment, a scout agent explores the target interface and summarizes its observations into a compact manual. A lightweight router uses this manual to predict mixing coefficients over the adapter bank, which combine the existing adapters into a new one for inference. Adapting to a new interface takes only a scouting run and a forward pass, without gradient updates on the target. With Qwen and Llama models, we evaluate domain transfer on TimeWarp and WebArena-Lite-v2, and version transfer on TimeWarp. In all three settings, WebMix matches or outperforms in-context learning and LoRA baselines, often matching the average success of specialists trained directly on those interfaces. On TimeWarp domain transfer, it gains about 8 percentage points on average over the best baseline with both models. These gains do not come at the cost of seen interfaces, where WebMix keeps its lead, and extend to version transfer. Adapting to a new interface can therefore rely on exploration and composition instead of retraining.
  </details>

- **[Predicting Privileged Information Effectiveness for On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22512)**  `arXiv:2610.22512`  
  _Kaijian Zou, Emiliano Penaloza, MohammadHossein Rezaei, Anisha Gunjal, Mohit Raghavendra, Lu Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) enables large language models (LLMs) to learn from privileged information (PI) without a separate teacher model. However, prior work largely treats PI as a fixed design choice, leaving the effects of its content and construction poorly understood. This challenge is particularly important in multi-turn agentic settings, where trajectories contain actions, observations, and reasoning that can be transformed into PI in many ways. We systematically evaluate 16 PI configurations across three multi-turn agentic benchmarks and three student models that vary in scale and reasoning mode. We find that PI effectiveness depends on its content, generation source, and the student model. Information-dense trajectories and PI produced by more capable generators generally yield larger gains, whereas thinking models benefit less consistently. To assess PI before training, we compare PI utility with two KL-based metrics: sampled-token reverse KL ($D$) and error-step KL contrast ($\Delta d^{-}_{\mathrm{err}}$). The KL-based metrics track post-training performance more consistently than PI utility, which can be distorted by information leakage. Empirically, selecting each task's PI by $\Delta d^{-}_{\mathrm{err}}$ also improves post-training performance over random selection, most when the PI candidates differ widely. Finally, effective PI remains beneficial when combined with GRPO, allowing the combined objective to outperform a strong GRPO baseline. Overall, our results provide practical guidance for constructing and evaluating PI in OPSD training.
  </details>

- **[Generative Value Classifier](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22497)**  `arXiv:2610.22497`  
  _Alexander Swerdlow, Perry Dong, Chelsea Finn_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Q-learning is premised on learning a function to predict the value of a state-action pair, with the goal of using such a function to guide policy improvement. Most approaches learn this function from scratch using scalar or distributional return targets. This discriminative approach leaves another source of supervision for learning representations unused: the actions themselves. Rather than directly predict the return of a state-action pair, we ask the reverse question: how likely is the action under each possible return? This perspective provides a critic whose generative component is a return-conditioned policy, which can share the actor's architecture. Training this policy supervises the critic with every action rather than with returns alone, and a squared-error loss anchors the critic's Q-value to the bootstrapped target. We evaluate our approach on both offline and online RL tasks and show gains over leading discriminative methods, including distributional RL methods.
  </details>

- **[Lean4AgenticRL: Advancing Agentic RL with Verifiable Multi-level Process Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22448)**  `arXiv:2610.22448`  
  _Ruida Wang, Gerald Friedland, Tong Zhang, Chris Kong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Enhancing the agentic capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) to tackle complex software engineering (SWE) and general terminal-usage problems is a pressing challenge. Current state-of-the-art LLMs often go through large-scale agentic Reinforcement Learning (RL). However, they mostly rely on high-level outcome rewards, with minimal consideration of low-level toolcall and mid-level workflow-stage rewards, which makes the training less effective. To overcome this limitation, we propose **Lean4AgenticRL**, which, to the best of our knowledge, is the first framework that grounds multi-level process rewards in the unified Lean4 formalization schema for open-ended agentic RL. **Lean4AgenticRL** first builds a *problem-specific workflow* system that balances the agent's own capability and controllability during execution, laying the foundation for further verification. Subsequently, our workflow formalization and multi-level reward annotation methods enable agent behavior verification within a general harness system. It provides multi-level rewards to support more effective agentic RL with the workflow-stage-normalized GRPO algorithm. Using **Lean4AgenticRL**, we train **LAR-9B/27B**, a family of models with enhanced agentic capability. Extensive experiments indicate that our model outperforms the leading model of similar size. **LAR-27B** achieves **77.3%** on TerminalBench-2.1. On average, our models outperform the base model by **9.89%** and surpass the second-best model of comparable size we know by **5.07%**. These results indicate the potential of integrating formal methods in enhancing open-ended agentic RL.
  </details>

- **[Sparse Autoencoders Can Induce Local Geometry for Mitigating Catastrophic Forgetting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22439)**  `arXiv:2610.22439`  
  _Kamil Faber, Katarzyna Filus, Łukasz Pawela_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Catastrophic forgetting remains a challenge in supervised continual learning, as adapting to new tasks causes representation drift and loss of previously learned information. This raises the question of which representational changes should be constrained while preserving plasticity. We therefore introduce Sparse Autoencoder Geometric Anchoring (SAGA), which converts a Sparse Autoencoder (SAE) trained at each task boundary into local, low-rank constraints on representation drift. Replay examples specify where the constraints apply, while leading right singular directions of the encoder Jacobian specify which changes are penalized. The resulting geometry is input dependent and can be retained without keeping the SAE for subsequent tasks' training. We establish its local relation to latent code matching, define its spectral truncation error, and derive a conditional bound on frozen-readout drift. With replay and snapshot history, the method achieves the highest mean Class-IL accuracy and lowest mean forgetting among the evaluated ER, DER++, ER-ACE, and SGD baselines on three pretrained ViT-B/16 benchmarks, with accuracy gains of 12.15, 4.79, and 3.22 percentage points over the strongest conventional baseline on CIFAR-100, CUB-200, and ImageNet-R, respectively. From scratch results are more mixed, while rank ablations and geometry controls show that SAE-induced low-rank geometry offers useful stability-plasticity trade-offs.
  </details>

- **[Rank Measures of Fine-Tuning Updates Need a Noise Baseline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22438)**  `arXiv:2610.22438`  
  _Muhammad Usman Altaf_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Studies of what fine-tuning does to a language model often summarise the weight update $\Delta W$ by its rank. We compare the rank measures used for this against the same measures on a random matrix of the same shape, and against two harder references: the pretrained weights, and matched fine-tunes without alignment content. Measures that count directions put our fine-tuning updates only a little below noise; stable rank, computed from the same singular values, puts them far below. Both are right: an update has a few strong directions on top of a long tail. The pattern holds for thirteen released pairs up to 8B parameters, and it is not a mark of alignment: with training held fixed, instruction data does not stand out from Wikipedia or code, DPO gives the same update whether its preference labels are real or half swapped at random (one model, modest preference strength), and scrambled text gives the most concentrated update of all. Applied to a recent claim that post-training is superficial because the effective rank of the weights barely changes, the baselines show that a random update of the same size leaves that rank almost unchanged while wrecking the model, and that an edit built on the effective rank of the update has no measurable effect in our tests. Rank measures of weight updates need a noise baseline, and claims about alignment need a control without alignment content.
  </details>

- **[CF-NODE: Counterfactual Spatial Node Intervention for Test-Time Adaptation Under Domain Shift, Evaluated Across Five Diabetic Retinopathy Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22437)**  `arXiv:2610.22437`  
  _Sabarish G, Deepak Parashar_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated fundus-based diabetic retinopathy (DR) grading can substantially expand screening capacity, but source-trained models degrade sharply across different cameras, clinics, and patient populations. Existing test-time adaptation methods depend on entropy minimization or target information accumulated from image batches and streams. These mechanisms can reinforce confidently incorrect predictions and break down when clinical images arrive independently. We introduce CF-NODE, a novel counterfactual test-time adaptation framework for cross- domain DR grading. Instead of trusting prediction confidence or estimating a target distribution, CF-NODE asks how each prediction responds under a controlled intervention on the evidence supporting it. CF-NODE ranks internal spatial nodes using model-derived attention, feature magnitude, and node-set similarity, progressively attenuates the highest-ranked nodes, and uses the stability of the resulting expected-grade response to apply a bounded calibration of the predicted grade distribution. The framework is fully frozen and adapts every image independently using only geometric views and a fixed source-derived prior, requiring no target-derived information or model updates. We evaluate CF-NODE on five external DR datasets under repeated-seed evaluation against TENT, EATA, SAR, and T3A. CF-NODE outperforms all four methods with statistically significant improvements over the second-best method, improves AUC on every target domain, and attains the highest average AUC and macro-F1, delivering stronger grade discrimination together with more balanced recognition across DR severity levels. These results demonstrate that counterfactual intervention sensitivity provides consistent and deployment-ready domain robustness for single-image clinical inference.
  </details>

- **[Trust but Verify: Designing Region-Inhomogeneous Forward Dynamics for Discrete Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22380)**  `arXiv:2610.22380`  
  _Gunmay Jhingran_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continuous diffusion treats the forward process as a design space; discrete language diffusion often uses absorbing corruption, leaving context fully trusted throughout generation. This makes trust binary: corrupted context is copied as ground truth, propagating errors into generated spans. We reopen this design space by combining absorbing and uniform-mixing kernels with region-dependent rates. Missing positions follow absorbing dynamics, while context undergoes low-rate random substitution, exposing the model to observed but uncertain tokens during training. At inference, a heuristic sampler re-injects noise into suspicious context under an annealed intensity and freezes context updates late in the trajectory. An 8,000-step finetune of a pretrained 170M-parameter masked diffusion model takes under five hours on a 6 GB laptop GPU. The base model assigns roughly 50% probability to observed tokens even when they are uniform-random garbage. For uniform-random corruption, finetuning detects corrupted context with 0.96 recall and 0.91 precision, improves infill token-F1 by 3.8 points at 10% corruption and 6.4 points at 20%, and removes the accuracy dip next to corruptions. Clean conditioning shows no measurable regression, with a false-edit rate below 0.4%. Gains for plausible corruptions are weaker: about 0.5 F1 points at 10% corruption, without statistical significance. Our results suggest that detectability and damage depend jointly on departure from the data conditional: nearby noise is hard to detect and semantically mild, while distant noise is conspicuous and destructive. We interpret absorbing diffusion as the infinite-contrast limit of a trust axis, analogous to the signal-to-noise gap between known and unknown regions in diffusion inpainting. In our setting of a 170M model and a single text domain, intermediate trust is reached by changing forward dynamics rather than architecture.
  </details>

- **[When is a large language model a good calibration agent? Physics-labeled prompting for budgeted engineering simulator calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22379)**  `arXiv:2610.22379`  
  _Ruo-Qian Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Calibrating expensive engineering simulators requires tuning model parameters to match reference data, a process heavily constrained by computational costs. This study investigates whether Large Language Models (LLMs), conditioned on physical diagnostic descriptions of simulation errors, can propose informative parameter updates more efficiently than conventional derivative-free optimizers. We test this on a cylinder-wake Large-Eddy Simulation and a damped-oscillator inverse problem. Physics-labeled prompts are evaluated against four text ablations (removed, anonymized, padded, misdirected) and standard optimizers (Nelder-Mead, Bayesian optimization, CMA-ES, and random search). Results demonstrate a strong early semantic advantage: the first physics-informed LLM proposal improves the initial design in 23 of 25 trials. After five evaluations, the median calibration error is 21.9 times lower than physics-free controls. However, this advantage is transient - disappearing by fifteen evaluations - and inherent LLM decoder variability yields a 9.8-fold performance spread. To address this, we introduce a hybrid workflow that uses the LLM as a warm-start, passing accumulated evaluations to a conventional optimizer. This two-stage numerical continuation policy reaches target accuracy in just 9 simulator runs, outperforming the LLM alone (12 runs), Nelder-Mead (17 runs), and Bayesian optimization (>60 runs). Supported by sensitivity analysis validating the test cases, this hybrid physics-labeled LLM scheme proves highly effective as a calibration agent under strict evaluation budgets.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Fixed Acquisition Functions: Temporal Difference Active Learning for Perturbation Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22122)**  `arXiv:2610.22122`  
  _Xudong Zhang, Jianzhe Li, Yitong Tang, Peijie Zhou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Genetic and drug perturbation screens are important tools for elucidating disease mechanisms and validating therapeutic targets, but experimental costs limit the number of candidates that can be tested. Existing perturbation screening methods typically update the surrogate model with feedback while keeping the acquisition function fixed, limiting their ability to adapt selection to different response landscapes. Experimental feedback reveals current hits and informs subsequent selection by updating candidate predictions, providing a training signal for learning how to select experiments. We formulate budgeted perturbation discovery as a sequential decision problem and propose Temporal Difference Active Learning (TDAL), which learns an acquisition function online through temporal difference learning from a single screening trajectory, accounting for current hits and future discoveries. TDAL uses the Hellinger distance between hit probabilities for unmeasured candidates before and after an experiment to weight successor value, and reuses measured outcomes through leave-batch-out (LBO) to construct virtual transitions and alleviate training data scarcity. Across eight public screens spanning single, double, and triple perturbations under varied experimental configurations, TDAL achieves an average rank of 1.63 among eleven methods and ranks first on four tasks, demonstrating its adaptability across response landscapes.
  </details>

- **[Router Drift Control: Mitigating Off-Policy Degradation in Mixture-of-Experts Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21682)**  `arXiv:2610.21682`  
  _Liangyao Jin, Zhixin Wang, Liming Liu, Mingjun Zhang, Zhengzhou Zhu, Fangcheng Fu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) can become off-policy due to training-inference mismatch, mini-batch updates, and asynchronous training. The resulting mismatch is especially harmful in Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models, where rollout-training discrepancies can change routing decisions and lead to router drift. Rollout Routing Replay (R3) mitigates this mismatch by reusing expert selections from the rollout stage during training. Our analysis shows that routing replay introduces gradient bias by losing contributions from experts selected by the training router but not the rollout router. To address this, we propose Router Drift Control (RDC), which uses routing disagreement between rollout and training as a signal of off-policy staleness and selectively rejects high-drift responses. The sequence-level filtering avoids gradient bias introduced by replay while preserving native expert selection and the associated learning signals for retained responses. Experiments show that RDC achieves larger gains as policy staleness increases and remains effective in asynchronous RL. In asynchronous training, RDC combined with Truncated Importance Sampling (TIS) improves the average peak validation accuracy across AIME 2024 and AIME 2025 by 5.5 points over TIS and 4.5 points over R3.
  </details>

- **[Does On-Policy Distillation Need to Complete Every Rollout?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21337)**  `arXiv:2610.21337`  
  _Zhengxin Zhang, Maosen Tang, Ning Wang, Changhao Li, Jingren Zhou, Bo Dai, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) effectively transfers capabilities from a teacher to a student through feedback on student-generated rollouts. However, generating complete student rollouts and computing teacher feedback for every token make OPD computationally expensive. We introduce Stochastic Truncated On-Policy Distillation (STOPD), which accelerates OPD by randomly truncating student rollouts and correcting the resulting sampling bias with blockwise inverse-probability weighting (IPW). At each training step, we sample a stopping position for each rollout before generation and let the student generate until that position or natural termination. We obtain teacher feedback on every generated token and use blockwise IPW to compensate for the lower probability of retaining later tokens while controlling the weights. We prove that this weighting preserves Vanilla OPD's gradient sum in expectation and derive one-step update-error bounds for SGD, Adam, and Muon. We evaluate STOPD across multiple teacher-student model pairs, task domains, and optimizers. STOPD reduces generated tokens by 25-48% while maintaining accuracy comparable to Vanilla OPD, achieving 1.3-2.2x training speedups.
  </details>

- **[Learnable Hodge-Laplacian Driven Mixture of Experts for Steady-PDE Surrogates on Coarse Unstructured Meshes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20585)**  `arXiv:2610.20585`  
  _Stavros Nousias, Akis Nousias, Konstantinos Gkrispanis_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current approaches for steady partial differential equations surrogate models on unstructured meshes apply a single mixing mechanism to the whole solution. This paper proposes an architecture that partitions the solution among vanilla transformer experts, each focused on a different spectral scale. The partition is built from a discrete Hodge Laplacian $L_0=\star_0^{-1} d_0^{\top}\star_1 d_0$, that serves as a fitting layer measuring high-frequency content. A gating mechanism takes the mesh as input and produces the per-vertex weights that recombine the expert outputs. AirFRANS is a steady Reynolds-averaged Navier--Stokes (RANS) airfoil benchmark. We evaluate our approach in a reduced remeshed version of the benchmark with each unstructured mesh counting 9.6K faces and 4.9K vertices. We compare our approach with the Transolver on the same dataset and dataset slit. Our approach reduces relative $L_2$ error by 31% and interior mean squared error by 47%.
  </details>

- **[Fully sequential Gaussian process regression for preference learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19391)**  `arXiv:2610.19391`  
  _Ilya O. Ryzhov_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study continuous global optimization problems in which the objective function cannot be directly evaluated; rather, we observe only which of two selected design points appears to perform better, subject to noise. Such comparisons complicate the use of Gaussian process regression because they yield non-Gaussian posteriors. We consider a fully sequential approximation, based on moment-matching, that yields a fast closed-form update of the metamodel's mean and covariance. We then propose DOVE, a fully sequential sampling policy that combines an information gain criterion with randomized sampling designed to control error accumulation. Under fairly standard regularity conditions, we prove convergence rates for both statistical error and expected simple regret that depend on kernel smoothness. To our knowledge, these are the first such rates for fully sequential approximate Bayesian inference in continuous global optimization.
  </details>

- **[Statistical Vignettes on the Grokking Phenomenon](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18811)**  `arXiv:2610.18811`  
  _Baye Malick Gning, Elvis Dohmatob_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this work, we study grokking in a simple, analytically tractable setting whose rich phenomenology allows us to disentangle the effects of initialization, signal strength, weight decay, sample size, and input dimension. These factors can produce superficially similar learning curves through distinct mechanisms, complicating their interpretation. Our analysis distinguishes early fitting through nuisance features, core feature learning, and delayed generalization. We identify sharp conditions under which grokking provably occurs and derive analytic asymptotic expressions for the lengths of the generalization plateaus. Our results also characterize regimes of early generalization and failure to reach a prescribed test accuracy target. We show that the interpolation edge constitutes a distinct dynamical regime, where vanishing spectral gaps separate core learning from generalization, helping clarify the role of proximity to interpolation in existing accounts of grokking. The resulting picture is analogous to fast and slow phases in chemical kinetics and suggests that the lazy-to-rich transition discussed in the literature may reflect a similar separation of dynamical time scales. Our theoretical results are faithfully supported by extensive experiments.
  </details>

- **[SP$^2$ec: Adaptive Self-Speculative Decoding for Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18735)**  `arXiv:2610.18735`  
  _Yuqi Huang, Xingyao Li, Yunlong Hou, Fengzhuo Zhang, Jiachun Pan, Vincent Y. F. Tan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative Decoding (SD) has demonstrated its efficacy in accelerating large language model inference, but for Vision-Language Models (VLMs), the diverse input modalities make it difficult for a small trained drafter to achieve consistently high throughput. Self-speculative decoding reuses parts of the target model as a drafter which retains the multi-modal capability of the VLMs, yet its efficiency depends on choosing a suitable draft configuration for each prompt. We propose Single-Peak SPeculative decoding (SP$^2$ec), a training-free bandit algorithm that adapts the draft configuration during generation. SP$^2$ec exploits the single-peak throughput pattern observed in existing self-speculative methods, including SWIFT, KnapSpec, SpecVLM, and our simple cosSim. Our algorithmic design explicitly minimizes a novel notion of wall-time regret for each given query, corresponding to the empirical wall-time latency. Theoretical guarantees on the wall-time regret show that SP$^2$ec can exploit the optimal draft configuration, with negligible exploration cost. Extensive experiments on Qwen2.5-VL-7B-Instruct and LLaVA-NeXT-Mistral-7B across visual and textual tasks show consistent improvements of up to 11\% over the original self-speculative methods, demonstrating the wide applicability of the proposed SP$^2$ec algorithm.
  </details>

- **[Agentic-MatriBO: Capability-Compiled Agentic Bayesian Optimization for Materials and Chemical Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17832)**  `arXiv:2610.17832`  
  _Yu Zeng, Yuchuan Chen, Ziyue Zhang, Zijian Wei, Yanyi Su, Guojiang Zhao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Materials and chemical optimization must choose the next synthesis, characterization, or high-fidelity simulation from sequential observations under a limited budget. Large language models can use task knowledge to propose adjustments, but composing surrogates, acquisition functions, and search strategies is constrained by compatibility, parameter dependencies, and evidence requirements. We present Agentic-MatriBO, a capability-compiled agentic Bayesian optimization (BO) framework that represents BO components and selection conditions as composable capabilities. A language policy proposes BO recipes and candidate plans; a compiler checks proposals against the registered capability catalog; and a deterministic execution layer validates and commits candidates. Oracle observations are stored in research memory and inform later rounds. We compare Agentic-MatriBO with eight baselines on four offline finite candidate pools---COF, PCE10, Buchwald--Hartwig (BH), and Suzuki--Miyaura---using five seeds per condition; results are reported descriptively. Across the four tasks, Agentic-MatriBO has the best mean endpoint quality after orienting each objective so that higher is better. On COF, it reaches the tabulated optimum in all five seeds both in the end-to-end comparison and after shared initialization, where it also retains an advantage in cumulative quality. Its normalized area under the best-so-far quality curve during the BO stage (nAUC-BO) is substantially higher on COF, near parity with the best baseline on PCE10 and BH, and lower on Suzuki. COF ablations show that removing the capability compiler or evidence gate coincides with lower cumulative quality and fewer exact-optimum hits. The framework provides an inspectable interface for evidence-conditioned BO adaptation.
  </details>

- **[Guarded Block Transfers for Polytope Distance: Linear Convergence with Explicit Constants and Gap-Certified Screening for SVM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15829)**  `arXiv:2610.15829`  
  _Mayank Gupta_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hard-margin SVM training is the distance problem between two convex hulls, which the Triangle Algorithm (TA) solves with a certified duality gap. We propose \emph{guarded block transfers}: $k$ Mitchell--Demyanov--Malozemov (MDM) transfers per scan are aggregated into one exact line search, guarded by a comparison with the single best transfer at a cost independent of the number of points $n$, and replaced by an away step when the leading transfer is capacity-clipped. On the Minkowski-difference polytope, where the objective is $1$-strongly convex, we prove global linear convergence with explicit constants and no combinatorial swap-step bounds; the per-step contraction is $k$-independent and only the drop-count bound grows with $k$, so aggregation is provably safe while the saving in scans is measured, not proved. Since the TA certificate is the Frank--Wolfe gap divided by the current distance, gap-safe screening applies, and we bound the iteration after which it has removed every zero-weight point off the optimal faces by $O\!\left(\tfrac{k}{\rho}\log\tfrac{1}{\tau}\right)$ up to logarithmic factors in the geometry, $\rho$ the rate constant and $\tau$ the optimal-face margin. On five LIBSVM benchmarks, with fairer baselines than our submission, the solver matches the baselines to four or five digits wherever both converge, ties LIBSVM on sparse-support hard margins and beats our blended pairwise conditional gradients (BPCG). But our full-cache sequential minimal optimisation (SMO) code ties or beats it on kernel $L_2$ margins, LIBLINEAR's dual solver is up to $4.5\times$ faster on the separable $L_2$ margin, and our solver is one to three orders of magnitude slower than LIBLINEAR's Newton solver on dense-support $L_2$ margins and than LIBSVM on a nearly touching kernel hull. Its edge is the certificate, not speed.
  </details>

- **[SelectArena: A Modern Benchmark for Feature Selection on Real-world Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13644)**  `arXiv:2610.13644`  
  _Bastian Schäfer, Dominika Matus, Lennart Purucker, Ricardo Knauer, Erik Rodner, Frank Hutter, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Selecting a subset of features (columns) from a tabular dataset is a ubiquitous challenge in real-world applications. Industry experts seek to minimize model training costs, climate scientists require an interpretable subset of factors to understand extreme weather events, and clinicians need to choose among diagnostic tests due to financial and time constraints. When looking at the scientific literature to determine which feature selection method to use, practitioners across domains face an unrepresentative landscape of benchmarking. Despite extensive research, existing feature selection benchmarks provide limited guidance because they rely on outdated models, ad hoc evaluation protocols, or artificially generated data that do not reflect real-world problems. To close the gap between scientific benchmarking and real-world applications, we introduce SelectArena, a modern benchmark for feature selection. We compare 15 widely used feature selection methods on 71 manually curated real-world tabular datasets, covering classification and regression problems, using standardized evaluation protocols and state-of-the-art tabular (foundation) models. Our results reveal clear trade-offs across predictive performance, runtime, stability, and validity. Tree-based methods, CART and RFImportance, along with F-Test are Pareto-optimal when taking into account predictive performance and runtime. F-Test, information-theory-, and distance-based methods lead on stability and validity, with F-Test emerging as a balanced choice across our metrics. Search-based methods are slow, unstable and achieve low performance. SelectArena turns these findings into practical guidance and, as an extensible platform, supports future feature selection research.
  </details>

- **[AdaptLSTM: Efficient Adaptive Online Learning for Cloud Workload Forecasting under Distribution Drift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12265)**  `arXiv:2610.12265`  
  _Xinhua Miao, Bowei Yang, Zhengong Cai_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate workload forecasting is critical for elastic resource provisioning in web-scale cloud services, where distribution shifts driven by viral content, product launches, and user behavior degrade offline-trained models rapidly. Naive online learning recovers accuracy but incurs prohibitive per-step compute cost. We propose AdaptLSTM, an adaptive online framework that detects drift via validation-calibrated thresholds and applies selective, targeted updates. On the Alibaba Machine Trace, AdaptLSTM recovers 54\% of Naive Online's improvement at 20\% cost ($2.7\times$ efficiency, $p=0.002$ over 10 seeds). On the more volatile Container Trace, it achieves 96\% at 20\% cost ($4.8\times$ efficiency, $+75\%$ MAE reduction over Static). Unlike classical drift detectors (ADWIN, DDM, Page-Hinkley) which fail to trigger on regression-scale error streams, AdaptLSTM fires 42 times over 301 steps and outperforms matched-budget baselines. Wall-clock profiling shows $1.33\times$ throughput gain and 45\% update-time reduction. The framework is model-agnostic: identical Pareto patterns hold for LSTM, GRU, and Transformer backbones.
  </details>

- **[Bernoulli Flow Models: Self-Consistent Generative Modeling for Binary Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11362)**  `arXiv:2610.11362`  
  _Hao Mo, Liying Yang, Shumin Yao, Xinxing Yu, Ajian Liu, Xudong Mao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Binary diffusion models typically require many function evaluations (NFEs) to generate high-quality samples, making inference costly. Reducing NFEs without sacrificing sample quality or relying on distillation or additional training remains challenging. Existing binary diffusion models define discrete one-step forward paths and derive reverse posteriors accordingly. In low-NFE cross-step sampling, they incorrectly approximate a true multi-step likelihood using a single-step transition, causing severe quality degradation. We propose Bernoulli Flow Models (BFM) to decouple generative dynamics from fixed discrete time steps. Instead of sequential one-step Markov diffusion chains, BFM defines a unified continuous global Bernoulli probability flow path between data and pure noise and derives analytical closed-form posterior transitions over arbitrary time intervals. Reducing the inference NFE therefore only requires re-evaluating this posterior on the new intervals rather than approximating skipped steps. This removes the structural training-inference mismatch of discrete chains and enables strictly self-consistent low-NFE sampling. Experiments show strong robustness under aggressive NFE reduction: on LSUN Churches 256x256, a 256-step-trained BFM achieves an FID of 9.22 with only 16 sampling steps, while the state-of-the-art discrete baseline degrades to 204.10. BFM also remains competitive with continuous and discrete generative baselines under standard full-step inference. These results establish BFM as a theoretically rigorous, self-consistent, and practical framework for fast binary data generation. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Transferability of Learned States in Neural PDE Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10972)**  `arXiv:2610.10972`  
  _Shunye Wang, Haochen Wen, Shuo Li Liu, Xuanyi Wang, Lihao Liu, Zhongying Deng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Assessing useful reuse in neural PDE solvers is challenging: final accuracy can reflect source learning and target-time computation. Our reuse contract separates solution accuracy, learning contribution, and numerical utility through paired state comparisons, matched target information and budgets, and cost accounting. A literature audit extracts 18 version-specific protocol records from 12 papers, documenting retained states, target-time resources, and reported controls. For a fixed linear system and residual tolerance, we construct two initial guesses with identical solution-error, energy-error, and residual norms, reaching the same solution with different conjugate-gradient (CG) iteration counts. Across 240 source-training trajectories, two linear PDE families, Fourier neural operators and convolutional networks, a fixed predictor's benefit reverses across correction algorithms. Among pairs with both relative prediction errors less than or equal to 5 percent on 64 in-distribution tasks (63 by 63 interior grids), reductions in all three norms accompany more CG iterations, at mean taskwise rates of 23.5 percent and 23.9 percent in two libraries. Work-based selection saves 2.50-3.33 CG iterations on held-out in-distribution tasks; matched adaptation demonstrates finite-budget pretraining value. Independent batches confirm a 0.73 percent complete online saving for one physics-trained Fourier neural operator against zero-initialized Poisson-preconditioned CG. Reuse requires matched state comparisons and downstream computational evidence.
  </details>

- **[MS-ECG-FM: Towards a More Universal Electrocardiogram Foundation Model for Health Monitoring using Multi-source Contrastive Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07662)**  `arXiv:2610.07662`  
  _Robert A. Lewis, I-Min Chiu, Kyle Verrier, Karthik Jayaraman Raghuram, Francoise Marvel, Salar Abbaspourazad, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Electrocardiography (ECG) records the electrical activity of the heart, aiding diagnosis by detecting abnormalities in cardiac function. ECG foundation models have demonstrated promising results, but are limited by a reliance on ECG interpretation reports as their sole supervision. Because interpretation reports only capture the subset of waveform information routinely recognized by clinicians, this constrains representation learning to overlook the broader diagnostic signals present in ECG. We introduce a new ECG foundation model --- MS-ECG-FM --- that is trained through contrastive alignment to multiple distinct clinical note types, including ECG, echocardiography, radiology, and discharge reports. We evaluate MS-ECG-FM on an extended set of ECG detection benchmarks, showing that it comprehensively outperforms existing methods on the full span of conditions that ECG can detect, including in reduced-lead configurations. Different reports improve representations for different diagnostic domains, while multi-source alignment captures their complementary information and produces consistently strong representations across clinically diverse tasks.
  </details>

- **[Learnable Spectral Activations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07419)**  `arXiv:2610.07419`  
  _Tamir Shor, Or Litany, Alex Bronstein_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Implicit neural representations (INRs) are shaped by the spectral structure induced by their input encodings and activation functions. Existing methods improve fitting primarily by modifying which frequencies are available to the network, through coordinate encodings or periodic nonlinearities. However, frequency access is not the only bottleneck: signals with localized or spatially varying structure require the network to efficiently compose frequencies into multi-harmonic internal responses. We introduce learnable spectral activations (LSA), which replace fixed neuron-level nonlinearities with a residual truncated Fourier series whose harmonic amplitudes are learned during training. LSA does not expand the asymptotic function class. Instead, it changes the factorization of the representation: linear weights select features while activation coefficients control spectral shaping, and the two are updated by separate gradients. Because the activation output is affine in the coefficients given fixed pre-activations, spectral tuning becomes a more direct subproblem compared to architectures where it is entangled with feature selection. Empirically, this factorization concentrates more target-signal energy in the leading eigenmodes of the neural tangent kernel, consistent with improved optimization behavior. Across audio, image, neural radiance field, and neural acoustic field tasks, LSA also improves reconstruction quality.
  </details>

- **[Exploring the Trade-Off Between Structured Pruning and Fault Tolerance in Deep Neural Networks for Space Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03117)**  `arXiv:2610.03117`  
  _Toon Vinck, Naïn Jonckers, Jaro De Roose, Jeffrey Prinzie, Peter Karsmakers_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep Neural Networks (DNNs) inherently exhibit a degree of robustness to bit-level faults due to their distributed representation of information. As a model increases in width, this information becomes more dispersed, theoretically reducing the impact of any single bit fault. In this paper, we empirically investigate the relationship between model width and robustness to Single Event Upsets (SEUs). We conduct a comprehensive experiment in which baseline models undergo iterative structured pruning to reduce their width while preserving task performance as much as possible. At each pruning stage, we run a targeted fault-injection campaign to evaluate the model's performance under simulated bit-flip scenarios. Our results show that, although structured pruning increases per-inference sensitivity to faults by reducing redundancy, this effect is effectively counterbalanced by shorter execution time, which lowers the probability of encountering an SEU. These findings suggest that structured pruning can yield significant energy and latency savings without compromising overall reliability, providing useful guidance for designing robust AI systems for space applications.
  </details>

- **[Generalization in Neural Networks Through the Lens of Magnitude Potential](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01633)**  `arXiv:2610.01633`  
  _Sahel Torkamani, Henry Gouk, Rik Sarkar_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Explaining generalization and training dynamics in neural networks remains a challenge, and various approaches have been developed to study different aspects of these phenomena. In this paper, we introduce the idea of {\em magnitude potential} -- a quantity based on the theory of metric magnitude -- that reflects how well an arbitrary point is represented by a given set. We find that this basic quantity can be applied to examine various features in neural generalization. The ratio between the magnitude potential with respect to a class and with respect to the entire data, computed at the logit layer, is informative of the representation of the point. In experiments, these ratios for individual training points are found to be correlated with the Feldman memorization scores. Magnitude potential ratios aggregated across points detect structural changes in the decision boundaries and provide a geometric indicator of grokking in modular arithmetic. Although the magnitude potential ratio and neural collapse are both closely associated with intra-class and inter-class geometric structure, the magnitude potential ratio remains informative even when neural collapse is explicitly suppressed.
  </details>

- **[Pretraining Transformers with Quantized Softmax in Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33591)**  `arXiv:2609.33591`  
  _Shangzhen Zhu, Muyan Hu, Tomasz Kozlowski_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-precision Transformer systems increasingly quantize attention matrix multiplications, while softmax often remains at higher precision. During pretraining, an approximate softmax changes the gradients that train the model as well as its forward computation. We study this interaction with K-interval attention, which approximates the exponential using K+1 grid values. We vary per-row grid calibration, interpolation versus hard rounding, and the placement of a straight-through surrogate relative to normalization. We derive the corresponding backward rules, including calibration derivatives, and compare these choices in pretraining experiments matched on model, data, and optimizer. Detaching the row extrema leaves the forward computation unchanged but produces a delayed increase in validation loss. With hard rounding at K=4, min-max calibration and a pre-normalization surrogate incur a large loss gap; changing either choice substantially reduces it. At 124M parameters and 2.5B training tokens, fixed-window calibration with a post-normalization surrogate yields a validation loss gap of +0.019 nats relative to softmax at K=4, and with a pre-normalization surrogate yields +0.004 nats at K=16.
  </details>

- **[An Agnostic Sample Compression Scheme for Squared Loss of Near-Linear Size in the Fat-Shattering Dimension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.29696)**  `arXiv:2609.29696`  
  _Guangjian Zhang, Zhengyi Yang, Xiaoyang Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study the squared-loss sample compression question posed by Attias, Hanneke, Kontorovich, and Sadigurschi in their ICML 2024 paper. For every nonempty class $\mathcal{F}\subseteq[0,1]^{\mathcal{X}}$ and accuracy $0<\alpha\le1$ with $d=\operatorname{fat}(\mathcal{F},c'\alpha)<\infty$, where $c'>0$ is universal, we prove the existence of a deterministic agnostic scheme of total size $O(d\log^3(2/\alpha))$. Its code contains original labeled observations and auxiliary bits, and its reconstruction has empirical loss at most $\alpha$ above the infimum over $\mathcal{F}$ on every finite sample. This establishes the polylogarithmic formulation in Section 5 ofarXiv:1810.01864v2. The construction approximates a near-optimal internal target by a fixed-step boosting ensemble and takes its equal-weight median. Average loss permits an accuracy-dependent number of rounds, so the entire ensemble can be retained without dual-dimension sparsification. Quantized target constraints and round-membership bits determine the reconstructed function exactly. We also prove that, when retained observations must come from the sample, a fixed class of fat-shattering dimension one requires total size $\Omega(\log(1/\alpha))$. With observations and auxiliary bits charged together, this rules out the proceedings version's log-free formulation under its original-example restriction. The framework extends to average power loss at scale $\alpha/p$.
  </details>

- **[Bandit Multiclass PAC Learning: Structural Baselines and a Confidence Direct Sum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.29694)**  `arXiv:2609.29694`  
  _Guangjian Zhang, Zhengyi Yang, Xiaoyang Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  How much does high confidence cost when a multiclass learner sees only whether its prediction is correct? We separate two behaviors in realizable bandit PAC learning. An anchored dimension gives a general lower bound that is attained, up to universal constants, by a family of product-block classes. In contrast, for $n\ge16$ divisible by four, an affine multiplexer with bandit DS dimension $2n-1$ has sample complexity $\Theta((n\min\{n,\ln(1/\delta)\}+\ln(1/\delta))/\varepsilon)$ in the standard accuracy and confidence regime. Its lower bound applies to adaptive randomized learners with improper outputs. The obstruction is that evidence collected against one candidate group cannot be reused against the others. The better of two algorithms matches this cost. One finds the correct group through a positive response. The other exhausts the rank of successive groups. The resulting confidence profile rules out a universal additive-confidence bound linear in the bandit DS dimension. Moreover, classes with identical anchored dimension, bandit DS dimension, and pointwise label width can have polynomially different sample complexities. We also sharpen the confidence accounting in ListCascade and show how one positive response replaces the bandit DS dimension by the anchored dimension in a general upper bound.
  </details>

- **[When Is Language-Model Advice Worth Buying? A Matched-Budget Study of Predictive Gating in Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.29548)**  `arXiv:2609.29548`  
  _Ibne Farabi Shihab, Md Najmus Swaqeeb, Abu Sa-Adat Mohamed Moon-Im Al Ahsan, Joyanta Jyoti Mondal_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language-model advice can accelerate reinforcement learning, but every call has a price, and a returned suggestion may be unparsable, unsafe to execute, or no better than the agent's own action. We introduce certified predictive advice gating, a cost-aware controller that forecasts possible advisor responses, values the action actually executed after parsing, execution checks, and cache reuse, and requests advice only when a calibrated lower bound on that value covers the price. Our analysis separates the gap of executed advice, cumulative regret under an intervention-stable fallback, and myopic acquisition loss. On BabyAI GoToObj with Qwen2.5-1.5B and 7B advisors, the gate improves return over no advice by paired $0.029\pm0.016$ and $0.030\pm0.015$ while using more than $97\%$ fewer calls than always asking. Matched-budget comparisons, however, do not resolve an advantage over an early query schedule, so the gain reflects economical advice volume rather than demonstrated state selection. Groupwise calibration raises coverage on positive-value opportunities from $0.47$ to $0.85$, still below the $0.90$ target. A separate headroom-aware gate reports paired gains over an early schedule of $0.032\pm0.012$ and $0.029\pm0.011$ on GoToObj and $0.027\pm0.011$ and $0.028\pm0.012$ on GoToObjDoor, and adds BabyAI and ALFWorld success, decision-stratum coverage, and component timing. These results separate return gains, query placement, and execution screening; finite-sample deployment guarantees remain unestablished.
  </details>

- **[CastClaw: A Human-in-the-Loop Autonomous Agent for Interactive Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30976)**  `arXiv:2608.30976`  
  _Xiaoyu Tao, Mingyue Cheng, Ze Guo, Bokai Pan, Qi Liu, Shijin Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Practical time-series forecasting requires domain knowledge, constraints, plausibility checks, and human intervention. We present CastClaw, an interactive forecasting system centered on an autonomous forecasting loop and supported by a forecasting-oriented harness. Users create and refine tasks in natural language through command-line and Web interfaces. An internal large language model (LLM) agent reads the task and evidence, selects a permitted tool action or request for human feedback, and uses checked results to decide whether to continue, revisit an analysis, switch a candidate, or stop. The harness supplies tool integration, controlled execution, state management, historical validation, and execution limits. npm distribution connects installation and configuration to interactive execution and forecast and report export. Across five public electricity-price datasets, CastClaw achieves the lowest point-estimate MSE and MAE among 16 baselines. A Nord Pool case study illustrates its inspectable decision process, while provincial electricity-load data from North China (January--June 2026) demonstrate offline task execution.
  </details>

- **[Can Graph Learning Learn Circuits?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08536)**  `arXiv:2608.08536`  
  _Chester Tan, Moritz Lampert, Courtney Maynard, Ankit Ramakrishnan, Tina Eliassi-Rad, Ingo Scholtes_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Circuit localization is a mechanistic interpretability task whose goal is to identify a sparse subgraph of a transformer's computation graph sufficient to reproduce a particular behavior. Most established methods localize circuits independently for each model-task pair. We instead frame circuit localization as a graph machine learning problem in which the edges of a computation graph represent computational pathways, and graph neural networks (GNNs) model interactions among these pathways. We introduce Graph Circuit Learning (GCL), a supervised, amortized framework that trains a GNN across multiple model-task pairs and applies it to unseen cases. To provide sufficient data, we augment the InterpBench benchmark with additional cases derived from TracrBench programs. Of the 46 evaluated GCL configurations, the one selected by cross-validation scored a median edge AUROC of 0.946 (interquartile interval [0.841, 0.980]) on the 16 held-out cases of our evaluation split. Removing all message-passing edges reduces the median to 0.926. We also adapt PGExplainer, a GNN explainability method, to circuit localization, obtaining a median edge AUROC of 0.950 on the same cases. These preliminary results suggest that graph machine learning offers a natural and potentially powerful perspective on circuit localization, one we hope encourages closer exchange between the two communities.
  </details>

- **[PowerAtlas: Feasibility-Aware Language Models for Electricity-Computing Co-Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26710)**  `arXiv:2607.26710`  
  _Kaiwen Jiang, Siya Xu, Ziyue Zhu, Kaiwen Xue, Chao Yang, Anh Tuan Luu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
